i

M. WILLIAM HOLSINGER
Grand Master
October 16, 2002 – October 15, 2003

ii

M. WILLIAM HOLSINGER
Grand Master – 2002-2003
Our Grand Master, M. William “Bill” Holsinger was born
November 12, 1932, in San Bernardino, CA. Bill graduated from San
Bernardino High School, San Bernardino Valley College and completed
courses in Real Estate at the University of California Extension.
Bill served in the U.S. Army at Fort Leonard Wood, Missouri as
an X-ray technician, ambulance driver, and as company clerk in the 54th
Field Hospital.
After returning to California he was employed by the County of
Orange as a Jr. Right of Way Agent, appraising and negotiating the
purchase of real property for public purposes. After three years he
moved to Riverside and performed similar work for the Riverside County
Flood Control and Water Conservation District, and finally moving to
the City of Riverside where he retired in 1993 as Real Property Services
Manager.
He and his wife, Jean, have one daughter, Leslie, a Past Honored
Queen of Bethel 264 in Riverside.
Professionally, Bill is a retired member of the American Right of
Way Association Chapter 57, and is a past president of the Riverside
Society of Appraisers.
Bill and Jean are members of Magnolia Presbyterian Church
where he is an ordained Elder, member of the Endowment Committee,
and a member of the Property and Finance Committee of the Riverside
Presbytery.
Bill joined Evergreen Lodge No. 259 F&AM in Riverside in
1964,and served as its Master in 1971. He is a member of Southern
California Research Lodge and Long Beach Scottish Rite, where he
served as Master of Kadosh of Long Beach Consistory. He was honored
with the rank and decoration of Honorary Inspector General of the 33rd
Degree in 1987. He earlier served San Bernardino Scottish Rite as Wise
master of the Chapter of Rose Croix. He is a member of al Malaikah
Shrine Temple, the Riverside High 12 Club, National Sojourners;
Ungava Chapter No. 106 Order of the Eastern Star. He served Bethel
264 International Order of Jobs Daughters as Associate Bethel Guardian;
and in 1985 President of Southern California Past Masters Association.

iii

Bill has served the Grand Lodge of California on numerous
committees. He was Grand Marshal in 1973, Grand Tiler in 1975, 1976
& 1977, and Grand Orator in 1979; and in October 1999 was elected Jr.
Grand Warden of the Grand Lodge of California.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

3

PUBLIC OPENING CEREMONY
Sunday, October 12, 2003
1:00 PM
The Public Opening of Grand Lodge was held in the Masonic
Auditorium of the California Masonic Memorial Temple, 1111 California
Street, San Francisco, California beginning at 1:00 p.m. The Grand Lodge was
raised by Worshipful Richard G. Weyers, Master of Los Altos Lodge No. 712.
Grand Master M. William Holsinger, accompanied by his Grand Lodge
Officers, entered the Auditorium to the sound of the organ, and assumed their
stations. The Grand Chaplain gave the invocation.
The Grand Master welcomed all in attendance to the Public Opening of
the 154th Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge of California. He thanked
Worshipful Weyers for raising the Grand Lodge.
The Grand Master then noted that the gavel used to raise the Grand
Lodge was a setting maul gavel and sounding block Worshipful Weyers
presented to him at the Installation of Officers of Los Altos Lodge No. 712 on
December 16, 2002. Crafted from the Ohio "Tree of Heaven", or Ailantus tree
by Gregory D. Buschek, Past Master.
The Grand Master further noted that the following gavels would be
used during the program: a setting maul and sounding block crafted of Desert
Ironwood from Southern California and Pacific Madrone from Northern
California, by Glenn C. Johnson and Glenn C. Johnson II, of San Dimas Lodge
No. 428. Implements of Masonry on the sounding block were furnished by
Eugene Bowman, PM, Pomona Lodge No. 246. They were presented by
Worshipful Christopher Madero, Master of Pomona Lodge No. 246, at the
celebration of their 125th Anniversary on November 2, 2002; a gavel and
sounding block crafted of Palaya Wood by Worshipful Bill Hutchison, Past
Master of John D. Spreckels Lodge No. 657. They were presented by
Worshipful Kenneth C. McMillen, Master, at the Installation of Officers,
December 6, 2002; a setting maul and sounding block of Macasser Ebony and
Rock Maple crafted by Worshipful Chris Holme, PM, North Hollywood Lodge
No. 542. The Square and Compass and the three Jewels of the Principal
Officers of the Lodge are inlayed with solid silver and plated in white gold. The
sounding block represents the Three Steps and the Mosaic Pavement. They
were presented by Worshipful Jan Eddy, Master, North Hollywood Lodge No.
542, at the Installation of Officers, December 19, 2002.

At the conclusion of the introductions, the Grand Master remarked, “It
is a special pleasure and a privilege to welcome you to our Annual
Communication. To each of you I express the appreciation of all Masons in
California.”
The Grand Master asked the Grand Marshal to escort Mrs. Janet
McClanahan, Past Grand Matron of the Grand Chapter of California, Order of
the Eastern Star, to the East, and the Grand Master asked her to bring the
greetings on behalf of all the members of the Family of Freemasonry in
California.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

7

The Grand Master thanked Mrs. McClanahan for her remarks, and
asked the Grand Marshal to conduct her to a seat in the auditorium.
The Grand Master then called on Lee Stafko, Pharoh of the Sciots, who
presented the Grand Mater with a Grand Master's Sciot fez.
The Grand Master then directed the Grand Marshal to retire and with
the assistance of the five representatives from the youth organizations, present
the Flag of Our Country and the flags of the five Armed Services of our nation
before the Altar of Freemasonry.
The Grand Master then invited all citizens to join him in the Pledge of
Allegiance to the Flag of Our Country. The Pacific Boychoir Academy
performed the National Anthem.

Masonic Homes of California Presentation
The Grand Master called on Deborah E. Stebbins, Executive Vice
President of the Masonic Homes of California, who gave a report on the new
communication program for the Masonic Homes titled “Welcome Home”. At
the conclusion of her remarks, Mrs. Stebbins introduced a video by the same
title explaining the services of the Masonic Homes and the evolving needs of the
membership.
The Grand Master thanked Mrs. Stebbins and all of the staff of the
Masonic Homes of California for their efforts in providing excellent care to
Master Masons, their wives and widows at the Home and through Masonic
Outreach Services and the several children in the children’s program at Covina.

Remarks from the Youth Orders
The Grand Master called on the four youth leaders of the three
Masonic youth orders in California. Miss Jennifer Anderson, Grand Bethel
Honored Queen, represented the International Order of Job's Daughters; Miss
Lauren Maiman, Grand Worthy Advisor, represented the International Order of
Rainbow for Girls; Brother Carl Baughman, Jurisdiction Master Councilor,
represented Northern California Jurisdiction, Order of DeMolay; and Mr.
Matthew McKinney, Jurisdiction Master Councilor, represented Southern
California Jurisdiction, Order of DeMolay.

Presentations to the Youth Orders
As each youth leader completed his or her remarks, the Grand Master
presented each one with two contributions. The first was a contribution in the
amount of $1500 to each from the California Masonic Foundation to be used by

8

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

the youth orders for leadership development. The second contribution was a
result of the Gold Pin program. The Grand Master explained that beginning in
1995, the Grand Lodge raised funds for the Tournament of Roses Parade float
through what was called the Gold Pin Program. Proceeds from the program
were used to support the float with the provision that if our participation in the
parade ceased, the money would support our Masonic youth organizations. Our
participation in the parade having ended and Gold Pin funds totaling
approximately $54,000, the fund would be distributed to the three youth
organizations.
Accordingly, the Grand Master presented contributions of $18,000 to
each of the three youth orders (Northern and Southern California Jurisdiction of
the Order of DeMolay each received $9,000).

Daniel Carter Beard Masonic Scouter Award
The Grand Marshal was directed to conduct the Daniel Carter Beard
Masonic Souter Award recipients West of the Altar. The Grand Master, with
the assistance of the Deputy Grand Master, presented the following brethren
with this award recognizing their service to youth through Scouting: Gerald W.
Van Wagner Sr., David E. Tate, Juan A. Soto, Timothy D. Schalles, D. Brad
Olson, PM, Thomas Joseph Hanson-Smith, Robert A. Zeroun, Kenneth C.
Hedrick, William R. Knight, and Most Worshipful C. Ray Whitaker, PGM.

Grand Master's Youth Support Award
The Grand Master announced that four brothers had been selected to
receive the Grand Master's Youth Support Award to recognize their outstanding
contribution to the Masonic youth orders. However, the Grand Master
announced that one of the brothers would not be present. He said:
Worshipful Harold "Bud" Whitfield, was Master of Beach
Cities Lodge No. 753 four times. He served Lomita Bethel
No. 36, International Order of Job's Daughters for fourteen
years. He was Associate Bethel Guardian of Manhattan
Beach Bethel No. 273 for eight years. Brother Whitfield was
well known for his fund raising efforts on behalf of Job's
Daughters. Most notably, he led a fund raising effort to allow
the Bethel daughters and adults to attend a Supreme Session in
Australia.
Brother Whitfield passed away on July 20, 2003. The Youth
Support Award will be presented to his widow at a later date.
The Grand Master then called upon Worshipful Raymond Daley,

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

9

Chairman of the Grand Lodge Youth Orders Committee to assist him in
presenting the Grand Master’s Youth Support Award to three of the honorees.
The Grand Master directed the Grand Marshal to escort the recipients
to a place West of the Altar. The Grand Master and the Deputy Grand Master
then presented the awards while Brother Daley read the biography of each
honoree.
The distinguished Masons were saluted with the Public Grand Honors.
The Grand Master then asked the Grand Marshal to conduct the recipients to
seats in the auditorium.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

DEVOTIONAL SERVICE
The Grand Master then observed that as the program drew to a close,
we would turn our thoughts inward and to the attention of the Grand Chaplain
for an Inspirational Message.
The Grand Master then called upon the Grand Chaplain, the Very
Reverend George L. Davis, for his message.
The Grand Master thanked the Very Reverend George Davis for his
truly inspirational message.
The Grand Master concluded by thanking all for attending the Public
Opening of the 154th Annual Communication of Grand Lodge. He led the
Grand Lodge Officers out of the auditorium.

ONE HUNDRED FIFTY-FOURTH
ANNUAL COMMUNICATION
MONDAY MORNING, OCTOBER 13, 2003
th

The 154 Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge of Free and
Accepted Masons of the State of California was called to order by Grand Master
M. William Holsinger, on Monday, October 13, 2003.
Worshipful Richard H. Greenberg, Master of Evergreen Lodge No.
259 raised the opening session of Grand Lodge.

Reception of the Past Grand Masters
The Grand Master welcomed the brethren and directed the Grand
Marshal to present the Past Grand Masters of the Grand Lodge of California at
the Altar. The Grand Marshal then introduced each Past Grand Master,
following which the Grand Master, accompanied by the Deputy Grand Master,
personally greeted each one. The Grand Master asked the brethren to join him
in saluting our Past Grand Masters by the battery of three times three. The Past
Grand Masters were then escorted to seats reserved for them in the auditorium.
The Grand Master then directed the Grand Secretary to send to each absent Past
Grand Master the usual letter of greeting from the Grand Lodge.
The Grand Master read a communication from Most Worshipful
Myron E. Smith, Past Grand Master, expressing his regrets at not being able to
attend and extended his best wishes to the Grand Master and the Grand Lodge.

Roll Call
The Grand Secretary was asked to call the roll of Grand Lodge
Officers, Past Grand Masters, and Past Grand Officers, and to ascertain if a
constitutional number was present to convene the Grand Lodge of California.
The following were present as indicated by their response to the calling of the
roll:

Grand Lodge Opening

The Grand Master then opened Grand Lodge in ample form. The
Grand Marshal and the Deacons retired to escort the Flag of the United States of
America into the Auditorium, which was followed by the Pledge of Allegiance.

Introductions
The following were asked to rise as groups for recognition: Hiram
Award Recipients, Golden Veteran Award Recipients, 75-Year Recipients,
Worshipful Masters, Lodge Secretaries, Grand Lodge Committee members,
members of Constitutional Boards, District Inspectors, Former Grand Lodge
Officers, and Visiting Brethren from other jurisdictions. Each group was
greeted with a round of applause.

Gavels
The Grand Master announced that the gavels to be used in this session
were as follows: a gavel hand-crafted from walnut by Eugene Ethridge, a
member of Evergreen Lodge No. 259 and Past Master of Moreno Valley Lodge
No. 804; a setting maul and sounding block, with a representative piece of wood
inlaid in it, made by Worshipful Bro. Gregory D. Buschek, and presented on
December 16, 2002 by Worshipful Bro. Richard G. Weyers, Master of Los
Altos Lodge No. 712 at their Annual Installation of Officers; a setting maul and
sounding block crafted by Worshipful Bro. Chris Holme, a Past Master of North
Hollywood Lodge No. 542, and presented on December 19, 2002, by

14

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Worshipful Jan Eddy, Master of the Lodge, at their Annual Installation of
Officers. It is made of Macasser Ebony and Rock Maple, with elaborate inlays
of the tools and jewels of Masonry; a gavel presented by Teodulo “Ted” C.
Galsim, Master of Atwater Larchmont Tila Pass Lodge No. 614, from his
personal collection of Masonic Memorabilia, at the Installation of Officers on
December 28, 2002; a setting maul gavel crafted from maple and cherry wood
by Terry O’Barr, and Eardean Johnson, Past Master, members of TemescalPalms Lodge No. 314, in Corona, and presented by Worshipful Bob Barden,
Past Master, and the Master of the Lodge, at the Official Visit on February 6,
2003; an oversized gavel hand-crafted from walnut by Gordon Dague, a
member of Perris Lodge No. 411. It was presented by Worshipful Donald
Young, Master of Elsinore Lodge No. 289, at the Installation of Officers on
January 5, 2003; and a giant gavel presented by Worshipful Bro. Stephen
Alderete, Master of Inland Empire Lodge No. 306 on the occasion of their
Annual Installation of Officers on December 7, 2002.

Memorial Service
The Grand Master called on Very Worshipful George L. Davis, Grand
Chaplain, and Chairman of the Memorial Service Committee, to present a
service of remembrance for our departed brethren.

Eulogy for Ralph H. Head, Past Grand Treasurer
Most Worshipful Roy J. Henville, PGM, delivered a eulogy for the
Very Worshipful Ralph H. Head, Past Grand Treasurer, who passed away
October 19, 2002.
“Neither do men light a candle, and put it under a bushel, but on a
candlestick; and it giveth light unto all that are in the house.”
“Let your light so shine before men, that they may see your good
works, and glorify your Father which is in heaven.”
Matthew 5:15-16
This beautiful passage, recorded in the fifth Chapter of the Book of
Matthew, characterizes for us the influence in our lives of the brilliant
light of life which radiated from the great and good man whose
memory we here honor today. If, as we are taught, man is a ray of the
divine intellect, surely this man has been many rays of the divine
intelligence – for he has imparted to us vast light in our travels from
darkness to light. And through his good works, our Father in Heaven
indeed has been glorified.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

Ralph Humphrey Head was born on the first day of Spring in 1913
in Orange County, California.
His skill for leadership was established early in life when he was
student body president at Huntington Park High School. He studied
Journalism at U.S.C. but was forced to withdraw before graduation
because of financial hardships during the Depression. He then began a
successful and long association with the Pierce family, starting first as
the private secretary to an executive in the family mortuary business,
and eventually becoming President of Pierce Brothers Mortuaries and
Pierce National Life Insurance Company (of which he was the
founding General Manager). He was recognized nationally in his
industry as an officer of the National Selected Morticians.
In his community, he served as a member, and President, of the
Toluca Lake Rotary Club. He was a member, and President, of the San
Fernando Valley Council, Boy Scouts of America. For his scouting
service, he was recognized with the Silver Beaver Award. He chaired
the Red Cross Campaign in the East San Fernando Valley. He served
on the Board of Directors of the Midnight Mission for forty-two years.
The Midnight Mission is a non-Masonic charity on Los Angeles skidrow, which offers brotherly love, meals, and clothing as a way out for
the homeless. He was appointed by the Queen of England to the
American Society of the Order of the Hospital of St. John of Jerusalem.
He has been a loyal and active member of churches in the various
communities in which he has lived, including El Montecito
Presbyterian Church in Montecito. Ralph and his wife, the former
Miss Frances Towler, had been married for more than sixty-two years
at the time of his death. He died on October 19, 2002.
Ralph was made a Mason in Sunset Lodge No. 352 in 1935, and
served as its Master in 1942. That Lodge is now Metropolitan Lodge
No. 352 through consolidation. Ralph also became an affiliated
member of La Cumbre Lodge No. 242 in Santa Barbara. He has served
the Grand Lodge of California in many capacities, including Grand
Orator, Grand Treasurer, and for twenty-five years as a trustee of the
Masonic Homes of California, including several terms as President.
For his outstanding service to the Masonic Homes, on September 18th
of this year the Administration Building in Union City was dedicated
as the “Ralph H. Head Administration Building”.
He also served as a trustee of the California Masonic Memorial
Temple, and served the Los Angeles Masonic Service Bureau for more
than sixty years, including many terms as a director, and three terms as
President.

15

16

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

In the concordant and appendant bodies Ralph held membership
and office in most of them. He was a member of the Board of
Governors of the Los Angeles Unit of the Shriners Hospitals for
Children. In 1942 Ralph joined the Los Angeles Valley of the Scottish
Rite, and later served as Venerable Master of the Lodge of Perfection,
and as Wise Master of the Chapter of Rose Croix. In 1949 he was
honored with the rank of Knight Commander of the Court of Honor,
followed by being coroneted an Inspector General Honorary of the
Thirty-Third Degree in 1957. In 2001 he as accorded one of the
highest honors in the Scottish Rite, that of Knight Grand Cross of the
Court of Honour. He was a member of the York Rite Bodies and the
Red Cross of Constantine. Brother Ralph was a man of impressive
stature, affable in manner, with a keen mind, excellent memory,
pleasing speaking voice, good sense of humor and a winning smile, all
of which enabled him to participate in ritual work with great success.
It is perhaps for his twenty-eight years as Editor of the California
Freemason magazine that Ralph will be remembered best. Through his
writings in the California Freemason, Ralph has shared his vision of
Applied Masonry with the world. Through this magazine Ralph taught
several generations of Masons the meaning and impact that
Freemasonry could have for their lives if they learned its important
lessons.
In 1985, when he received the Golden Veteran Award for 50 years
of membership, the Grand Lodge of California recognized the
tremendous impact that Ralph had made on Freemasonry by honoring
him with the coveted title of “Mason of the Year”.
In 1994, Ralph and his family were saddened by the untimely
death of Roger, his eldest son, a U.S. Air Force Colonel, Retired, and
brother to Steve, a member of Van Nuys Lodge No. 450.
In his eighties Ralph returned to school to complete the education
that the Depression had caused him to abandon. In 1996 he completed
his Bachelor of Arts Degree in History at the University of California,
Santa Barbara, at the age of 83. At the time he was the oldest in his
class – and an inspiration for young men and women years removed
from Ralph’s own generation. He was a successful businessman,
Philanthropist, avid golfer, loyal churchman, scholar, writer,
community and Masonic leader, and above all, a Mason. For Ralph
there was no greater honor than that of being a Master Mason, and his
life demonstrated that he truly understood what it means to be just that.
From his Grand Oration, titled, “Freedoms Holy Light” given in
1957, he said, “We need not change our rituals. What we need is a
change in the understanding of them. We need not change precepts or

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

17

doctrines or duties. What we need is to recognize their significance
and how to apply them in practical and useful ways in our daily lives”.
We shall, forever cherish in our hearts the memory of our departed
Brother.

MONDAY AFTERNOON, OCTOBER 13, 2003
The Grand Lodge convened at 1:15 p.m., and after prayer by the Grand
Chaplain, the Grand Master noted that Grand Lodge was raised by Worshipful
Bro. John D. Goodloe, Master of San Diego Lodge No. 35.
The gavels used were as follows: a gavel and sounding block made by
Worshipful Bro. Chester Rogers, and presented on December 21, 2002, by
Worshipful Bro. John D. Goodloe, Master of San Diego Lodge No. 35 on the
occasion of his Installation as Master of the Lodge. It is solid black walnut,
with each piece inlaid with a replica of the Grand Master’s Jewel; a gavel
presented on February 2, 2003, by Most Worshipful Tony Wordlow, Past Grand
Master. It is a replica of the gavel that Bro. George Washington used when
laying the cornerstone of the United States Capitol Building in 1973. The head
is made from marble from the Capitol steps. The occasion was a Reception for
the Grand Master, and a celebration of the 250th Anniversary of Bro. George
Washington’s Fellow Craft Degree; a gavel, made of a pecan log by Worshipful
Bro. Bill Hutchinson, Past Master, and presented on March 15, 2003. The gavel
is representative of the “traveling gavel” used in the 492nd Masonic District to
promote visitation among the lodges. It was presented by Worshipful Bro.
Kenneth McMillen, Master of John D. Sprekles Lodge No. 657 in San Diego at
a Reception for the Grand Master; a gavel presented by Bro. Tom McKinney,
Master of Kern River Valley Lodge No. 827 on April 9, 2002. The event was a
Cornerstone Laying Ceremony for South Fork Middle School at Weldon; a
schoolhouse gavel and sounding block. It was hand-made by Bro Curly Jenkins
and Bro Bob Cunningham, of Woodbridge Lodge No. 131 and Lodi Lodge No.
256. Made of three types of wood, representing the three degrees of Masonry, it
was present on April 26, 2003, on the occasion of the laying of seven
cornerstones at Elk Grove Lodge No. 173. It was presented by the 280th
Masonic District.

Credentials Committee Report

Adoption of the 2003-2004 Grand Lodge Budget
The Grand Master called on the Deputy Grand Master, Right
Worshipful Howard D. Kirkpatrick, to present the 2003-2004 Grand Lodge

Chairman of the Finance Committee, Worshipful Donald W. Finton, moved the

Treasurer, and was carried by a majority affirmative vote.

Adoption of the 2004-2005 Grand Lodge Per Capita

Worshipful David R. Doan, to present the 2004-2005 Grand Lodge Per Capita.

amount of $25.00, which was seconded by the Grand Treasurer, and carried by
a majority affirmative vote.

Election of Officers
The Grand Master then asked the Grand Secretary to explain the
balloting procedure for the election of officers. No ballot would be necessary if
the office were not contested. If more than one nomination were received for an
office, ballot cards would be distributed for the purpose of balloting for that
office.

Election of the Grand Master
Deputy Grand Master Howard D. Kirkpatrick was nominated Grand
Master by Most Worshipful Charles H. Alexander, Past Grand Master. No
further nominations appearing, it was moved, seconded and carried that the
nominations be closed, and that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a
unanimous ballot for Right Worshipful Howard D. Kirkpatrick to serve as
Grand Master for the ensuing Grand Lodge Year. Upon his acceptance, he was
declared unanimously elected.

Election of Junior Grand Warden
The Grand Master announced that pursuant to Section 510 of the
California Masonic Code, Worshipful Brother Melvyn B. Stein, a Past Master
of San Diego Lodge No. 35 in San Diego had been recommended for the office
of Junior Grand Warden of the Grand Lodge of California for the ensuing
Grand Lodge Year. The Grand Secretary then read the biography previously
circulated in accordance with this section, while Brother Stein stood before
Grand Lodge. He was then escorted to the Grand Tiler’s Room, while other
nominations were called for. None appearing, the Junior Grand Warden moved
that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a unanimous ballot for Worshipful
Brother Melvyn B. Stein to serve as Junior Grand Warden for the ensuing
Grand Lodge Year. The motion was seconded by the Grand Lecturer and
unanimously carried. Upon his return to the floor of the Grand Lodge, and upon
his acceptance, he was declared unanimously elected by the Grand Master.

Lodge from refreshment to labor.
Grand Lodge was then closed in ample form.

GRAND MASTER’S MESSAGE
It is my great pleasure to welcome the delegates and members of the
Grand Lodge of California to this, the 154th Annual Communication of your
Grand Lodge. We have assembled in peace and harmony, and may our
deliberations continue in that same spirit.
It is also my privilege to welcome the distinguished guests from other
jurisdictions. We are honored by your presence here, and we hope you enjoy
your visit in this beautiful city, and ask that you inform us if there is anything
we can do to make your visit more enjoyable.
Our theme this year has been “Freemasonry Seeks Good Men and
Makes Them Better – Tell a Friend”. By that message I have attempted to
encourage our members to share with their worthy friends the good works
performed by Masons whithersoever dispersed that we might be better known to
all men.
Further it has been my goal to encourage Lodges to enhance their
Masonic Education program that we might all be enlightened about the history
of our fraternity and the great lessons we would impress on all who seek light
by joining our Brotherhood.
We have shared this message from Crescent City in the north to Chula
Vista in the south, and I believe that those who have listened have benefited.
Section 1060 of the California Masonic Code requires the Grand
Master to “present at each Annual Communication, a written message setting
forth: (1) All of his official acts during the year; (2) The general condition of
Masonry within the jurisdiction; and (3) Recommending such legislation as he
deems necessary or expedient for the welfare of the Craft”.

State of the Craft
Although many of our Lodges are receiving petitions for the first time
in years and a general surge in interest has been observed as we have traveled
the jurisdiction this year, yet our numbers continue to drop. Our membership as
of June 30, 2003 was 78,644. This represents a decrease of 4.46% as compared
to one year earlier. The rate of decline has dropped from 4.84% to 4.46% as
compared to the earlier year, but still represents a loss. This continuing
reduction in our numbers is a great concern to me and the members of the
Executive Committee because if your Grand Lodge is to continue to provide
programs and leadership to encourage our constituent Lodges in the face of
dropping membership it requires greater financial support by fewer members,
and at some time in the future we will reach a point of diminishing returns.

36

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

It is my observation that our Lodges are being led and supported by a
limited number of members. That is, many are proud to call themselves
Masons, but fewer are willing to demonstrate that support in their words and
actions. Until we convince a much larger share of our members that we are
slowly fading away and that they must be a part of the solution, it seems to me
that our future is not bright.
In short, the state of the Craft is diminishing but salvable if we can
motivate more Masons to come to the aid of, and support this great order.

Membership
I have shared with you the unhappy numbers; now, what can we do
about the problem? First, let me make it clear I do not want to sacrifice quality
for numbers. Ours is an organization of GOOD MEN who have been found
worthy, and we must maintain those high standards: But I am convinced there
are many worthy men who have not been attracted to Masonry either because no
one has bothered to inform them or they have felt they do not have the time to
devote to our organization.
In November the Grand Lodge Officers occupied the stations during
the conferral of a First Degree on the stage of the Shrine Auditorium in Los
Angeles. This was a special meeting of Evergreen Lodge No. 259, and special
invited guests were those Entered Apprentice and Fellowcraft Masons who had
failed to progress during recent years. We attempted to rekindle the flame of
interest which seemed to have been extinguished.
Although the number in attendance was disappointing, the concept
worked because most of those in attendance returned to their Lodges and have
now become Master Masons. This simply points out the need for the leaders of
our Lodges to constantly encourage our candidates and never permit them to
drop by the wayside and fail to complete the journey they started by being
Raised to the Sublime Degree of Master Mason.
At the beginning of this year I appointed Wor. Phillip McAllister, as
Chairman of the Membership Development Committee, and charged him and
his committee with the task of developing a jurisdiction-wide comprehensive
Membership Development Program that would guide all Lodges toward
increased membership. Wor. Bro. McAllister will present his committee report
later during this Annual Communication. Listen carefully and put his
recommendations to work in your Lodges.
A topic which is much discussed throughout the Masonic world is that
of “the one day conferral”. It has many supporters and some detractors. Simply
stated it is the conferral of the Three Degrees of Masonry in one day, waiving
mandatory proficiencies prior to receiving the succeeding degree. Applicants
for the degrees submit petitions to their respective Lodges, are investigated, and

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

37

voted upon as usual. If the Lodge desires to participate, the candidate is then
given the option of receiving his degrees as usual (with required memorization
and Masonic education examinations), or participating in a regional one-day
class.
If the lodge approves and the candidate selects the one-day class, a
mentor from his Lodge accompanies the candidate to the place of the one-day
conferral, remains at his side during the entire day and assists him in limited
participation in the ceremonies of the three degrees, observing one active
candidate in each degree.
Following each degree, a Masonic Information Lecture is provided
which explains the history and meaning of each degree. At the conclusion of
the Third Degree each candidate returns to his Lodge to sign its by-laws and is
given the option of learning the prescribed work and being examined in each of
the three degrees, although this is not mandatory.
The ritual is conferred by highly proficient degree teams, and the
degrees are conferred in their entirety including opening lectures, and closing
Lodge in each of the three degrees.
This method of conferring degrees was first utilized in 1992 in the
District of Columbia and has now been used by more than half of all Grand
Lodges in the United States.
In an evaluation of one-day conferrals published by the Scottish Rite
Research Society, the following conclusions were reached:
•

The number of raisings each year has increased significantly
over the number before then, and this increase is steady and
continuing.

•

Men raised in one-day conferrals and those raised by the
traditional method remained in Masonry (that is did not dimit or stop
paying dues) in about equal proportions each year.

•

Men who became Brethren in one-day conferrals and those
Raised by the traditional method are also roughly equal in the
proportion who became active officers or are
otherwise active
in their Lodges or in Grand Lodge.

•

The same number and percent of Lodges continue to do
degree work in the traditional manner each year and to use the one-day
conferral each year
without any decline in traditional degree
work, with most Lodges using both methods.

•

In short it is reasonable to conclude the one-day conferral is a
useful tool for some Lodges and some men. At the same time there are
some lodges which prefer to use the traditional degree method, or a

38

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

combination of the two. There is no evidence that proves any of these
choices are harmful to Freemasonry.
My Brethren, there are many men in the world who are of the opinion
they do not have the time to devote to the traditional method. Although
incorrect, however, as they are of that opinion, they will not join. Why not give
them an alternative? This afternoon I shall present a specific recommendation
that will give these men an alternative.

Financial Affairs
The Executive Committee – the four progressive elective Grand Lodge
officers – has, during my four years, made every effort to reduce the budget and
hold the level of expenditures at a level that does not become burdensome to the
Lodges, and at the same time provide programs that will stimulate renewed
interest, a positive public image, and growth in our numbers. This has been a
difficult challenge. Reductions have been made which are not always popular,
and couple that with a sluggish economy and sharp downturn in the Market
which drastically affects our income, and we find ourselves in a very
challenging situation.
The Market shows some limited signs of recovery, but it will take time.
All of our entities are affected, but hardest hit are the Masonic Homes of
California. Our Board of Trustees under the leadership of President Allen B.
Gresham, P.G.M., has made good progress in making the Homes an efficient
cost effective organization; and at the same time they are attempting to aid and
assist more and more of our Brethren, their wives and widows. At the same
time they are concerned with preserving the Endowment Fund so that it will be
here for future generations.
I challenge the Masonic Homes Board of Trustees to continue to
explore how best to serve our Brethren fully utilizing all our facilities and at the
same time retaining our sound financial base.
A key element of providing these services is what has been known as
the Masonic Homes Endowment Campaign each year and is now known as
Masonic Philanthropies. That effort, under the leadership of our Director of
Development, Doug Ismail, now provides a choice for contributing Brethren.
During this past year contributions were encouraged for the Foundation’s
Masonic Student Assistance Program, and for the Masonic Homes M.O.S.
Program which was highlighted for you at yesterday’s public opening, and yes,
contributions can still be designated to the Masonic Homes Endowment Fund.
The size of the average gift increased by almost 50% this year, and I thank those
contributors for their generosity.
During my year as Grand Master, I suggested that in lieu of gifts to my
wife or myself that contributions be made to the Masonic Student Assistance

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

39

Program Endowment Fund which has been established by the California
Masonic Foundation. I am pleased to inform you that approximately $28,000
has been so donated; and to all Lodges and individuals who participated I
express my sincere thanks.
In addition, Most Worshipful Melville H. Nahin has informed me that
during my year a Brother who had passed away in southern California had made
provision in his estate for 2% to be given to the then Grand Master’s project.
Most Worshipful Nahin advises me that this could amount to approximately
$100,000 which will also be placed in the MSAP Endowment Fund. Such
generosity clearly demonstrates the deep feeling and high regard our members
have for our work, and I am grateful.

Long Range Planning and Leadership Training
The Research Committee under the Chairmanship of Larry Adamson
has continued this year to look to the future, and examine new ways of
approaching challenges. This effort should be continued both at the Grand
Lodge and constituent Lodge levels. That is one of the approaches taught at
Wardens Leadership Retreats, a program which helps develop leaders for our
Lodges. Leadership development is one of the most useful programs developed
by our Grand Lodge. It is unfortunate that only approximately 50% of our
Lodges send their Wardens to this beneficial program.

Public Relations
The Public Relations Program developed by Most Worshipful Melville
H. Nahin in 1999-2000 has evolved into a program of advertising special events
such as Public School Cornerstone Ceremonies and events recognizing public
school teachers who have attended our MSAP programs, or students who are
recipients of California Masonic
Foundation scholarships. Grand Lodge develops and pays for publicity
in local newspapers to inform the public of such events. These ads along with
the MSAP Program and the Child I.D. Programs (which provide wonderful
community services) also give Masonry an opportunity to display its support for
our communities.

Rose Parade Float Funds
Beginning 1992 Most Worshipful Stanley L. Channon chaired a
committee which arranged funding, designed, built and sent a float down
Colorado Boulevard in Pasadena as a part of the annual New Year’s Day
Tournament of Roses Parade. This float was provided in the name of the

40

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Family of Freemasonry. The final float was seen on New Years Day 2000.
Most Worshipful Bro. Channon attempted to obtain funding from other Grand
Lodges to continue the float but was unsuccessful.
In the early years of the program funds were raised by selling pins and
the solicitation contained a reference to Youth Groups who would be honored
on the floats. Some $54,000 remained in that account and it was felt consistent
with the original reference to youth in those early pin sales, to earmark that
money for the youth organizations. That was the source of the $18,000 given
yesterday to Jobs Daughters and Rainbow and the $18,000 divided equally
between Northern and Southern Jurisdiction DeMolay. It has been specified
that such funds are to be used for leadership training by those organizations.
The balance of the funds left from the Rose Parade Float program,
amounting to some $24,000, has been placed in an account to be used for
Masonic Membership Development, which I feel is consistent with the original
purpose of the Float Program.
Our thanks to Most Worshipful Stan Channon who spent many years
guiding and carrying out this program. Without his leadership, it would not have
continued for so many years.

The North Trip
During 2003 nine of the Grand Lodge officers spent 15 days visiting
the Lodges north of San Francisco and Sacramento – Lodges which are
relatively isolated, and some of which are in remote locations, and are seldom
visited by Grand Lodge. It was an honor, a privilege, and an inspiring
experience for those on the journey. The Masons we encountered are dedicated
to keeping Masonry alive in their communities – an example worthy of all
imitation.

75 Year Pins
One of the privileges of being Grand Master is the responsibility of
presenting 75-year diamond jubilee awards. These Brethren are obviously a
minimum of 96 years of age, and although I started the year with a list of 27
Brethren who were eligible for the award during the year, actual presentations
amounted to 11. And these are 11 of the most inspiring men I have ever had the
privilege of meeting.
But let me tell you about one I was unable to present. In the process of
attempting to locate the Brother his Lodge provided his telephone number but
had not been in contact with their member for some time although he was a life
member. On calling the number provided by the Lodge we discovered the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

41

Brother had been deceased for 8 years, and his Lodge was unaware even though
he had lived less than 100 miles from the Lodge.
Why do I mention this sad tale? Simply to make a point that we are
neglecting many of our older members who don’t come to Lodge. They are still
members and we owe them our concern and our best wishes. Every member of
every Lodge should be contacted a minimum of one time each year, and more
often if circumstances dictate. To ignore members who do not regularly attend
Lodge is to ignore our obligation to aid and assist. Remember, aid and assist is
not only financial aid, it is also emotional aid. Just a kind word, a simple how
are you will let him know he is not forgotten. My Brethren, without that show
of concern our members feel forgotten because they have been forgotten. Is
there any wonder that as our members pass away fewer are requesting a
Masonic funeral?

Reportable Actions
During this year now concluding it has become apparent to me that a
certain amount of disharmony seems to exist in some, but happily not all, of our
Lodges. A reluctance to follow the direction of the Master, and in some cases,
the Grand Master. We have attempted to deal with each of these situations as
they have arisen. In many cases a word of advice from the Inspector, Assistant
Grand Lecturer, or the Grand Master has been sufficient to quiet the storm; in
other instances more drastic action was required, and I now summarize three
such cases:
Pasadena Lodge No. 272 – Early in my year as Grand Master, it
became apparent that due to the sale of certain assets without approval of the
Lodge, disharmony was about to fracture the Lodge. On November 5, 2002 I
arrested the charter prior to the scheduled election of officers. I appointed a
committee chaired by Wor. Bro. Willis Rogers to make an investigation and
recommendation on future actions. Based upon their findings, I presented a list
of 12 requirements for the Lodge to complete in order to regain their charter. I
removed and appointed replacements for the Sr. Warden, Treasurer, Secretary
and all members of the Board of the Pasadena Masonic Temple Association.
I am pleased to inform you that all 12 requirements have been met, and
peace and harmony have been restored to the Lodge: On September 2, 2003 I
returned the charter to Pasadena Lodge No. 272, and they will hold an election
of officers at their November stated meeting.
Vallee de France Lodge No. 329 – After numerous meetings with the
officers of Vallee de France Lodge No. 329, the Grand Lecturer informed then
Grand Master, Most Worshipful Bro. C. Ray Whitaker, that the Lodge had not
held an election of officers nor an installation since November 1999. Based
upon this, Most Worshipful Bro. Whitaker, by letter dated August 22, 2002

42

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

informed the Master of Vallee de France Lodge that if the Lodge did not have a
full corps of officers qualified, in the ritual of our jurisdiction, to the satisfaction
of the Grand Lecturer by November 1, 2002, their charter would be suspended.
Their qualification consists of their ability to confer all three degrees in the
French language, and not a mixture of English and French, and their ability to
confer the first degree using their approved Scottish Rite Ritual.
Since Vallee de France was unable to meet the November 1, 2002
deadline established by Most Worshipful Bro. Whitaker, on November 6, 2002 I
arrested the charter of Vallee de France Lodge, and informed them they must
demonstrate their proficiency as outlined, consolidate with another Lodge, or
face revocation of their charter at the 2003 Annual Communication.
I appointed former Assistant Grand Lecturer, Willis H. Rogers,
Inspector of the 330th Masonic District. Wor. Bro. Rogers, who speaks French,
worked with the officers and on September 7, 2003 qualified the officers in all
three degrees.
Accordingly on October 1, 2003 I returned the charter to Vallee de
France Lodge. They will hold an election of officers at their November stated
meeting; and they will hold an installation in December. They are planning to
confer the French first degree in Pasadena in the spring of 2004.
Culver City-Foshay Lodge No. 467 – In mid-year 2002, it came to the
attention of then Grand Master Most Worshipful Bro. C. Ray Whitaker that
factionalism was developing in Culver City-Foshay Lodge No. 467. This
factionalism arose over use of the Masonic Center by a non-Masonic group.
The factionalism continued and began demonstrating its presence at the
ballot box with rejection of candidates. At the request of Grand Master
Whitaker, several Grand Lodge officers attended a meeting of the Lodge and
attempted to encourage the members to resolve their differences.
The factionalism continued, as did candidate rejection, and I authorized
Grand Secretary Very Worshipful Bro. John L. Cooper III to arrest the charter
of the Lodge on January 19, 2003.
On February 5, 2003 I attended the stated meeting of the Lodge and
removed the Master from office and appointed Wor. Bro. Douglas Calhoun
Master. I directed Worshipful Bro. Calhoun to appoint a committee composed
of members of the Lodge to meet, to determine the cause of the factionalism and
to report to me how such factionalism could be eliminated. I requested Most
Worshipful Bro. R. Stephen Doan to meet with the committee and serve as
Moderator.
Such meetings have been held, and I have received their report.
Although great progress has been made, it is my recommendation that the
incoming Grand Master continue the arrest of the charter until he is satisfied that
peace and harmony have been restored to Culver City-Foshay Lodge.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

43

Publications and Communication
During 2002 Very Worshipful Ralph H. Head was forced to resign due
to ill health as Editor of The California Freemason, a position he had held with
great distinction for twenty-nine years. Ralph was called by the Great Architect
to go and serve Him with his talents, and we found it necessary to find a new
Editor. That duty has been assumed, at least for the time being, by Wor. Bro.
Allan Casalou, the Assistant Grand Secretary. This instrument by which we
communicate with all our Brethren of this jurisdiction, as well as widows of our
former members, has taken on a new look. Each issue has a theme and all
articles contained in that issue are related to that theme.
I feel the new format is interesting, and if our members will only take
the time to read it they will profit therefrom.
I took a bold step this year and discontinued Proclamations. In place of
proclamations, a quarterly publication has been created called “News from the
Quarries”. This newsletter is a joint effort of the Grand Lodge and the Masonic
Homes. It is mailed to Masters, Wardens, and Secretaries of all Lodges. Each
issue contains a message from the Grand Master (wherein programs formerly
encouraged by Proclamations are covered), and a Masonic Education article is
included to assist the leaders of our Lodges in encouraging Masonic Education.
The balance of the newsletter is devoted to the Masonic Homes and
enlightening the Brethren on policies and news relative thereto. If our Masters
and Wardens will share the information found in “News from the Quarries”, I
feel it will do a far better job than reading proclamations.
Cipher Ritual
The original printing of our cipher ritual was exhausted last year, and
the books have now been reprinted. In doing so it was necessary to incorporate
new material authorized by Grand Lodge since the original cipher ritual was
authorized. And example of the new material is the optional “short form”
closing of a lodge. I have determined that – apart from the correction of
obvious typographical mistakes in the original – this new material conforms to
changes in the ritual as authorized by this Grand Lodge, and that the reprinted
cipher ritual conforms in all respects to our adopted ritual. The cipher ritual as
republished is therefore the official ritual of our Grand Lodge, and is to be
received and used as such by all.

Acknowledgements
When I accepted the call to have my name proposed for Jr. Grand
Warden back in 1999, I had no idea of the diverse issues that would come my
way during the next four years. It is only with much help and advice that one is
able to meet the challenge.

44

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

First and foremost is my wife, Jean, who has been at my side the entire
time and helped me more than I can say. I shall be forever grateful to a
wonderful staff in the Grand Lodge office under the direction of Very
Worshipful John L. Cooper III. We are lucky to have such a dedicated and
knowledgeable Mason for our Grand Secretary. Every Grand Master looks
upon his Grand Lodge Family as the epitome of dedication to the Craft and that
is the way it should be. I am no exception, my Family is the best ever because
they are mine and I thank each of them and their wives for their loyalty and their
service to Masonry. The elective line behind me is well prepared to assume
their new responsibilities, and I thank them for their support this year. The fact
that Grand Masters serve one year and pass the baton of leadership to another is
a very healthy tradition. It brings new ideas, new ways of doing things, and
new concepts on which to focus to the forefront. Stagnation is prevented and
progress is enhanced.
To paraphrase General (and Brother) Douglas Mac Arthur who
observed that “old soldiers never die, they just fade away”. I might say that “old
Grand Masters never die, they just go with the sun setting in the west at the
close of the day”, and tomorrow a new sun will rise in the east to lead this
Grand Lodge. May he be blessed with the same good fortune that has been
mine this year, and may God bless us all in the important work in which we are
engaged.
Respectfully submitted,
M. William Holsinger, Grand Master

APPENDIX B

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

67

REPORT OF THE DEPUTY GRAND MASTER
2003-2004 Grand Lodge Budget
Howard D. Kirkpatrick, Deputy Grand Master
(As mailed to the Secretaries of the Lodges in a
Memorandum dated August 25, 2003)
Each year the Deputy Grand Master prepares for submission to the
Annual Communication the budget for his year as Grand Master, beginning the
following November, and continuing through the end of the October following.
He is required to do so by Section 7040 of the California Masonic Code. Again
this year we are presenting a balanced budget to you and ask for your approval.
We have presented it to the Finance Committee and it was approved
unanimously. I ask you to review it with your lodge, and come prepared to vote
on it in October.
Our commitment to you, our constituent lodges, remains the same.
This year, as we have in previous years, we reduced spending and staff in our
Grand Lodge office, and at the same time have dealt with increased costs and
reduced membership. The Grand Master and the Executive Committee have
again made recommended changes in the California Masonic Code to allow us
to continue to present to you a balanced budget.
The budget is funded by the per capita of $22.15 which was adopted at
the last Annual Communication, and which the lodges in this jurisdiction have
now paid, or are in the process of paying. The budget is based on a total billable
membership of 76,125 Master Masons in good standing on the rolls of our
lodges as of June 30 of this year (78,601 Master Masons minus 2,476 dues
remissions).
The budgeted expenses for 2003/2004 are $1,959,347. This includes
$109,713 for publishing the California Freemason magazine. The difference
between the income and the total expenses as stated is $69,875 and this amount
is allocated to our Depreciation Reserve. Again this year nothing will be added
to the Grand Lodge Endowment Fund. The Endowment Fund is intended to
help offset future decline in membership when it will be very difficult to
continue crucial programs for our lodges when we have a much smaller
membership.

68

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

69

70

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

71

72

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

INSTALLATION ADDRESS
HOWARD D. KIKRPATRICK
Grand Master of Masons in California, 2003-2004
This morning before I go into my prepared remarks I want to make a
couple of comments.
First – I congratulate the Grand Lodge members for voting positively
on the Grand Master’s decision and recommendations. I take that response as a
confirmation of what your leadership is doing. Also, I believe it confirms to the
membership that leadership is listening and responding to you.
Yesterday you heard our Grand Orator, Bill Bray, describe an
individual who helped to change the world as a hero. Have you ever
experienced a feeling that you helped to change the world? Yes, I have. Was I
feeling that I was a Hero? No! Do I believe an average person, doing what he
believes is right, doing it as an individual can have a worldwide effect? Yes!
In September of 1990, I traveled to Novosibirsk, Siberia and visited the
communist party headquarters school. It is like a graduate school here and the
son of former Secretary Cherniko headed it and he stated to me, “We need your
help – We need people who can teach us management skills – Can you help
us?” A chill ran up my back, as I realized I was the first foreigner to ever walk
into the building. Communication was beginning between the common men on
this earth. Later that same day, I met a young farmer and he questioned me
about the politics of my city, of which I was a former mayor. I told him that if
he wanted to change things he had to get involved. Shortly thereafter, he ran for
the Duma, which is the equivalent of our House of Representatives. My last
contact with him, he was the Secretary of Agriculture for all of Russia. Did I
have an influence on him? I certainly hope so. If my encouragement to him
caused him to run for office, I am proud of that. Will my encouragement to you
to get involved in your lodge, cause you to do it? I certainly hope so. It is the
common man encouraging and influencing the common man that causes change
to take place.
Gosh! What a day this has been. I want to thank everyone for
attending today. It is comforting to know when you have support that is shown
here this morning.
The theme I have chosen this year is “Personal Responsibility – Accept
it and Teach it.” This will mean something different to each one of you. That is
okay because if you put it into practice we will have a greater success than if we
restricted it to one subject.
We need a change of attitude. Maybe that is what the people of
California were saying a week ago yesterday. Have you ever contemplated how
diverse we are in this State?
We have beaches, valleys, mountains,

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

73

manufacturing, farms, films, timber, water, large cities and small towns. You
name it and we have it. When we make a new law we apply it State-wide to
everyone, regardless of their shoe size. Is this what we are doing in
Freemasonry? Do we apply the rules strictly to everyone regardless of age,
location or local social conditions? Sure our basic tenets apply to everyone –
Brotherly Love, Relief and Truth.
I served in the U.S. Army almost 50 years ago. At that time I was a
young, spry, eager, tall and skinny teenager and when the Star Spangled Banner
played, I could jump to attention immediately and hold a smart salute. But
today things are different. My instincts, my sincerity, my patriotism are still
there, however, my physical condition is different. I cannot instantly jump to
my feet and stand straight as a board and hold a snappy salute as I did 50 years
ago. My feelings are the same. I still shed a tear when I sing “that our Flag is
still there”. I have to recognize I am not the same person I was 50 years ago. I
am not as brash or dictatorial as I once was. I have found I can negotiate better.
Am I as determined as before? Yes. Have my values lessened? No. In fact,
they have expanded.
Our Fraternity has changed but some of us are not recognizing those
changes and are resisting. Some of us want to keep the “good old days” forever.
But remember, without change, we will have the “same old days” not the “good
old days”.
How would you like to have an operation without anesthesia or how
would you like to drive a 1940 Ford with 150,000 miles on it, at 75 miles an
hour from Los Angeles to Redding today? Oh yes, without air conditioning, of
course.
We are the oldest and largest Fraternity in the World. Our history,
according to some researchers, goes back some 6,000 years, way before what
we call modern Masonry, which started in 1717. When Masonry first came to
this country in the 1730’s it remained along the East Coast where the population
was located.
We had many men in our Fraternity who were thinkers and doers. Ben
Franklin, George Washington and Paul Revere. These men met and were
responsible for forming our Nation using the basic beliefs of Freemasonry as the
foundation. They had a vision and were willing to risk their fortunes and their
lives to give that vision a chance at survival.
That same spirit came west in the late 1840’s with the 49ers. The first
Masonic meeting in California was held just north of Chico on May 8, 1848,
under dispensation from the Grand Lodge of Missouri. The Charter was carried
over land by Peter Lassen. The same dedication and vision was present at this
meeting as was present when Masonry came to America originally. Take a look
at the founders of our State and cities in California and you will find Masons
involved in a vision.

74

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Today we are faced with problems in California Freemasonry and our
concordant bodies. Since most of our concordant bodies require Blue Lodge
membership, or at least a relative belonging to Masonry, the success of these
concordant bodies is directly related to the success of Freemasonry. We can use
a lot of different names to describe these problems. For example, age, memory,
time, distance, cost, leadership, not caring, lack of dedication, death, social
values, and many more. Our emphasis needs to shift to uncovering the apparent
declining relative value that Masonry offers today.
What is it that would elevate our thinking? What would we be
thinking about if our aspirations were higher? We need to look at this from the
perspective of the foundational values of Masonry. These foundational values
are what we have always offered to the communities in which we live and
contribute. We must ask ourselves to what are we devoted? What is our sacred
duty? This is our opportunity to work directly on our perceived values.
In my opinion, we are fractured, must as rock hits a windshield and
makes a nick. If you don’t fix it right way, it spreads and you end of replacing
the windshield. However, if in fixing it you apply too much pressure the
window will crack more quickly and you will still have to replace the
windshield.
The leadership of our Fraternity is responsible for finding a solution,
but we are working under a hardship too. We have one year to initiate and set
in motion a solution. Sometimes, one year doesn’t even give us the chance to
get the word to each Lodge and have them act on the solution.
David Doan, Fred Sorsabal, Mel Stein and I have agreed to embark on
a process to create, and begin working toward, a long-term vision for California
Masonry. A worthwhile vision must both inspire and give impetus to the
actions necessary if the vision is to become a reality.
This vision must be one that enables all of us to become inspired and to
devote whatever energies we have in establishing Masonry as the preeminent
source of men in the service of family, community and the greater good of
humanity. With a challenge of this magnitude we are seeking not only guidance
from the holiest of sources but also from all of you who are hearing this today.
Some of the topics we will be looking at are:
We need to better understand the forces that are driving our Fraternity
today.
We need to recognize the social, demographic and cultural trends that
have impacted the state of Freemasonry today.
We need to present a clear awareness of the reasons behind our present
situation.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

75

We need to encourage personal responsibility through inclusion in the
visioning process.
How do we get there:
1.

Interpret the past – the answers of where we need to go lie in
the history of where we have been.

2.

Interpret the present – if we don’t start thinking differently
about the future we won’t create a future different from our
present.

3.

Create a vision of our desired future – this needs to extend our
thinking far enough out to be meaningful to even our youngest
members. The active engagement of our current leaders is
legacy building.

4.

Define the path toward our desired future – our steadfastness
will come from the rightness of our path. Our guidance will
come from our shared values and beliefs. Our resources will
be allocated strategically. Our endeavors will be exemplary of
the vision we are seeking.

I am asking that each of you who feel you have a vision to write to me
at the Grand Lodge Office or email me at hkirkpatrick@freemason.org to let me
know how you can contribute to this effort. I’m sure that I cannot appoint all of
you to this task force but I assure you your thoughts will be considered.
Thank you and God bless you all.

76

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

APPENDIX C

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

79

REPORT OF THE SENIOR GRAND WARDEN
Per Capita Recommendation for 2004-2005
David R. Doan, Senior Grand Warden
(As mailed to the Secretaries of the Lodges in a
Memorandum dated August 25, 2003)

It is the duty of the Senior Grand Warden to recommend the per capita
that will fund the Grand Lodge Budget for the year that he will be Grand
Master. I have been working with the Grand Lodge staff and the Executive
Committee to develop a tentative budget for my year as Grand Master. I have
sought input from all of the Masters and Wardens in California, on some of the
ideas that were considered for the budget. I have also reviewed the history of
per capita and the forecasts that were developed for the next ten years on
membership.
First, let me thank each of you who responded to the per capita survey.
Your input was helpful and was used to develop this per capita
recommendation. The per capita is an emotional topic and there are many
opinions about why it has increased over the years. The primary reason that per
capita has been increasing and will continue to increase for the foreseeable
future is the rapid decline in our membership coupled with the significant
increases in the costs to Grand Lodge, such as insurance. Over the last several
years, the Grand Lodge Budget has been reduced and we have reached the point
where we can no longer cut enough out of the budget to hold the per capita
increase to a cost of living adjustment. We have reached the point where we
must make some radical changes to reduce expenses and increase our revenue.
Your Executive Committee has been working on several ideas to
reduce expense and stabilize funding for the Grand Lodge Budget that will
require code changes over next couple of years. This year, Grand Master’s
Recommendation No. 2003-02 Flexible Dates for Annual Communication is an
effort to reduce costs to Grand Lodge that come right out of the per capita, as
well as improve attendance. There are other ideas that are being researched and
one or more of them will likely be submitted for your consideration in 2004 and
2005. I can assure you that the Executive Committee will continue to look for
ways to reduce expense, while we work on ways to attract and retain the high
quality members that are the foundation of our great fraternity.
It will take some time for these changes to take effect; therefore, I have
developed a tentative budget that generally limits the increases in the budget to
3% inflation, except in areas where higher increases are extremely likely, such
as general/liability insurance and workers compensation coverage. I have also
suggested larger increases for the areas of leadership development, masonic
education and membership development. These three areas are the focus of the
Grand Lodge’s Strategic Plan. Grand Lodge is not trying to do the lodges work.

80

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Our focus has been to provide direct support by providing leadership training
and quality materials for masonic education and for the lodges to use in their
membership development efforts. The cost of tentative budget was divided by
the number of dues paying members expected to be on rolls in July of 2004.
The result is the recommendation that the per capita, to be collected in July of
2004, be twenty-five dollars.
Attached is information about how the per capita will be spent. There
is also a chart that depicts the printed information. Two thirds of the per capita
is spent on services that go to support lodges, members, or Inspectors. Grand
Lodge continues to work on reducing the budget while maintaining the
important programs and services that benefit the membership.
Due to the continued rapid decline in membership, the increase in this
proposed per capita is more than a cost of living increase. We must invest in the
future of fraternity and quit hoping that if we only do the minimum or do
nothing at all that things will get better by themselves. The values taught by our
fraternity are worth far more than what we are paying in dues. We are a great
fraternity and the lessons we teach are needed more today than ever before.
There is a renewed interest in our ideals by many men in our state. We must
develop that interest in the good men we seek and continue our efforts to rebuild
Masonry in California. With your input and support we can and will make our
communities and our state a better place for our families to live. SHARE THE
LIGHT.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

81

85

REPORT OF THE GRAND TREASURER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
As has been the custom, the Grand Treasurer’s Report to Grand Lodge
will be brief.
The annual audited financial statements for the Grand Lodge of
California and its entities (Masonic Homes of California, California Masonic
Memorial Temple, California Masonic Foundation, Nob Hill Masonic
Management and the Retirement Plan) and all related trust accounts are
published in their entirety in the Proceedings. I urge you to take the time to
review these financial statements and become familiar with the fiscal operations
of this, your Grand Lodge.
As your Grand Treasurer, and on behalf of the financial staff, I would
like to take a few minutes this morning to share several significant projects that
were undertaken and completed during the past year to define, improve and
strengthen the financial function within the Grand Lodge and its several entities.
1.

The responsibilities of the Grand Treasurer, as set forth in the
CMC, were reviewed, evaluated and implemented to fully
comply with all provisions of the Code and best business
practices. It is noteworthy that this review confirmed the
Treasurer’s dual responsibilities as both Treasurer and Chief
Financial Officer of the Grand Lodge and its entities.

2.

During this past year, the Finance Department of the Grand
Lodge and its entities was restructured from an expensive,
straight-line, hierarchical organization to a cost-efficient,
management-team-based organization, with clearly defined
areas of responsibility. This extensive restructuring was
required to control costs, while improving all three levels of
financial services (accounting, reporting and investment
management), not only for the Grand Lodge, but also for its
three charitable foundations, its one for-profit company, its
retirement or pension plan and over fifty special purposes
funds and trust accounts. This new structure consists of a
Manager of Financial Operations, a Manager of Financial
Reporting and a Manager of Investments—all three reporting
to the Grand Lodge Director of Finance, who reports to your
Grand Treasurer.

3.

The monthly accounting reports for Grand Lodge and its
entities were redesigned to separate operational performance
reporting from investment performance reports to ensure

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

86

better visibility and management for all expenditures.
4.

Working with the Investment Committee and governing
boards, spending policies were established for all investment
funds. Basically, spending policies were set at 5% of a fiveyear rolling average as determined by fund value on
December 31st of the preceding year. I would recommend this
or a similar spending policy to those lodges with investment
portfolios. Regardless of size, a consistent and realistic
spending policy protects the principal of the investment
portfolio over good and not-so-good investment cycles.

5.

It was determined that a comprehensive policy statement was
required by, and for, our boards to fully address board
governance and accountability.
Accordingly, a “Board
Governance and Accountability Policy Statement” was
developed, considered, accepted and adopted by all four
boards.

6.

Working with the Investment Committee, finalized the
creation and operation of a Master Trust Agreement, with
Bank of New York as custodian for all investment funds
currently consisting of seventy individual accounts.

7.

Again, working with the Investment Committee, conducted a
formal search to identify and retain a financial service
company specializing in trust administration to provide both
asset and trust management, as well as trust accounting and
reporting. This new service will greatly enhance the ability of
the Grand Lodge Director of Masonic Philanthropy to offer a
wider range of options to our charitable donors.

In brief, my brethren, this has been a challenging, exciting and
productive year for your Grand Treasurer and for the Financial Services staff,
and we look forward to another year of service to this great fraternity.
Respectfully submitted,
Warren J. Blomseth, PGM, Grand Treasurer

APPENDIX E

167

REPORT OF THE GRAND SECRETARY
This is my twelfth report to you as Grand Secretary. The complete
report will be printed in the Proceedings, but here are some of the highlights of
the past year:
•

As of June 30, 2003, we had 78,601 Master Masons on the rolls of our
constituent lodges. The year before that we had 82,318 Master
Masons. That is a 4.5% decline in the number of Master Masons since
the year before. 926 of those were either dimits or withdrawals of
multiple membership. 1,122 were lost due to suspension for nonpayment of dues. 2,935 of the losses were due to death. There is
obviously nothing that can be done about the number of deaths, but
certainly there is something that each lodge can do about the number of
suspensions for non-payment of dues. I have noted that where lodges
actively pursue a program of communication with their members,
actively use their delinquent dues committees to truly find out why
dues are not being paid, and extend Masonic charity where members
are truly in need, the number of suspensions for non-payment of dues
decreases dramatically. Brethren, these are not just numbers. These
are Master Masons who are our brothers, and who once found
Freemasonry so attractive that they asked to become Masons of their
own free will and accord. Many that we are suspending have been
Masons for many, many years. Surely we owe it to them to find out
why they no longer want to pay dues before we suspend them. And in
the process, we may discover that these brethren are just waiting for
someone to take an interest in them.

•

The Grand Lodge Administrative Website continued to grow in
popularity and use. As more and more of our lodge leadership signed
up for it and began to use it, we expanded the kinds of information
made available on the website. Not only is communication easier, but
the cost-savings are significant. In a day when postage rates continue
to go up, electronic communication saves not only time, but significant
dollars.

•

The Grand Secretary has been assigned by the Grand Master the
responsibility for advising him on our relations with other Grand
Lodges. This past year our Grand Lodge received a request for help
from our neighbor to the south, the Grand Lodge of Baja California.
The Grand Lodge of California has enjoyed good fraternal relations
with this Grand Lodge since its creation as an independent Grand
Lodge in 1932. The Grand Lodge of the Valley of Mexico,
headquartered in Mexico City, had invaded the territory of the Grand
Lodge of Baja California by chartering three lodges in their
jurisdiction. After careful investigation, our Grand Master determined

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

168

that the Grand Lodge of Baja California was the aggrieved party, and
suspended our recognition of the Grand Lodge of the Valley of
Mexico. Despite our attempts to persuade them to cease and desist this
unmasonic activity, the Grand Lodge of the Valley of Mexico told us in
writing that they would continue to consider the State of Baja
California as “open territory,” and would also consider the State of
California to be “open territory.” Such behavior is not acceptable. It is
therefore my recommendation that the suspension of recognition of the
Grand Lodge of Baja California be approved by this Grand Lodge.
•

Although there are a number of new Grand Lodges that requested
recognition from the Grand Lodge of California, none have been in
existence long enough for a recommendation to be made on granting
recognition at this time. In addition, it has become increasingly
difficult to identify the correct Grand Lodge already recognized by us
in some countries due to divisions in many Grand Lodges, with splinter
groups claiming that they are the legitimate government of the Grand
Lodge concerned. If you are in doubt, please contact the Grand
Secretary for help. We can assist you in making the right decision
when you receive visitors from abroad, and particularly from Grand
Lodges in the newest parts of the Masonic world.
Most Worshipful, that concludes my report.
Respectfully submitted,
John L. Cooper III, Grand Secretary
