APPENDIX F

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

189

REPORT OF THE GRAND LECTURER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Brethren, I wish to preface this report by expressing my sincere
gratitude for the opportunity and privilege of serving you and this Grand Lodge
as its Grand Lecturer.
In accordance with the duties of this office, this report will give an
account of services rendered during the year with respect to ritual and Lodge
conditions.
The diligent efforts of your Ritual Committee continues to place great
emphasis upon the required and needed instruction of our fraternal principles
and values and is its number one priority. How and when these lessons are
taught are of great importance. We must always concentrate upon the quality of
degree conferrals and continued Masonic Education. Otherwise, the intended
fraternal teachings will deteriorate and end with negative results.
Lodge consolidations continue to occur. Some consolidations have
benefited its members, but in some areas the Masonic identity has been lost in
that California community. We say we are builders. Traditionally, we are
taught to be builders of character, principles and values. Are we putting forth
our best individual efforts to support our great fraternity, or are we complacent
to just watch what happens and follow? Each of us should look in a mirror and
ask ourselves that question.
This is the second successive year to combine the Inspectors Spring
Conferences into two conferences versus five. The Northern Conference
consisting of Divisions I, II and V was held in Vallejo. The Southern
Conference consisting of Divisions III and IV was held in Norwalk. The
implementation of this plan reflects a notable financial savings while taking
nothing away from ritual exemplification and review.
In each Division, two Ritual Seminars for Officers Coaches and two
Ritual Seminars for Master Masons were conducted and well attended and
received with much enthusiasm. Your Ritual Committee continues to seek ways
to improve training and instructional techniques and the encouragement of
member participation. Suggestions from the attendees are evaluated and
oftentimes implemented. These Seminars are in addition to the regularly
scheduled Division Inspectors Schools of Instruction that are conducted by each
respective Assistant Grand Lecturer.
More Lodges are experiencing an increase of applications from
younger candidates. While younger men are seeking membership, it is also
noted that more of them want to learn and give the long form proficiency. This
is a positive indication of great importance of how and what is being taught to
our candidates. Learning our Masonic principles and values appear to have a

190

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

higher priority versus how rapid they might receive their degrees. This
indication further supports the need for proper and continuous Masonic
education.
At our last Annual Communication, a progress report was given on
Grand Master Decker’s Recommendation No. 4. This recommendation was
approved and authorized a Task Force to prepare a “Ritual Manual” as a
supplement to our present cipher ritual. Your Ritual Committee and others
compiled more than 550 pages of research and information that was applicable
to the conferring of our three degrees. As a result of this research and effort,
diligent and considerable thought has produced a useful and simplified
supplement or companion that has been condensed to about 60 pages.
Basically, this document or “Ritual Manual” consists of four parts; a Table of
Contents, written instructions, pictorial illustrations and diagrams. At this time,
we are going to show you a sample of some of the contents in the form of slides.
First, a cover page to identify what this document is and then an
“Introduction” page that describes the intent ofthis “Ritual Manual” or
document.
The contents page will assist you in finding a particular instruction,
illustration or diagram.
This picture illustrates the grounding of a rod and hand along with
written instructions on how each should be done.
This is an illustration of throwing or placing the rod on the left side of
the Officer with instructions to accomplish this movement.
Here is an Officer standing and demonstrating proper posture.
This is a typical layout of a Lodge room. Not all floor work or
movements are included, because Lodge rooms vary in size and shape. Your
Inspector can properly advise you. The illustrations that will be displayed are
for a typical Lodge room. Adjustments may be necessary for your own Lodge.
If so, consult with your Inspector before making any adjustments.
First Degree Conferral. This diagram illustrates Officers’ direction and
position with arrows and dotted lines showing their paths of movement. This is
typical on most of the diagrams.
First Degree Conferral. This illustrates and instructs where the line of
perambulation is and when the scripture reading begins. Each degree has
appropriate diagrams and instructions.
Second Degree Conferral Floor Work. We show the illustration of
Deacons direction of movement and placement.
Third Degree Lodge Opening Floor Work. Each respective Officer
movement and position in communicating the word.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

191

Third Degree 2nd Section. We now illustrate the beginning locations
or placement for the FC’s, R’s, SFM, WFM, Senior Deacon and Candidate.
Third Degree 2nd Section. Illustrates R’s movement from rubbish to
Moriah.
Third Degree 2nd Section. We now illustrate the Secretary’s position
and instruction for calling the roll.
Other Floor Work – Flag Escort. Illustrates Marshal and Deacons
direction and place at altar to retire for Flag presentation.
These few slides demonstrate some of the contents included in this
supplement. Testing and critique is in the final stages. CD copies should be
available, for a nominal cost, by the end of this calendar year and your Ritual
Committee requests that each Lodge place their order with the Grand Lodge
Supply Room.
I extend my sincere thans to the entire Ritual Committee for all their
efforts. Each Assistant Grand Lecturer made notable contributions in the
various phases of this assignment. I applaud each of them for their dedication
of that needed time and energy to accomplish this task. Their other duties and
assignments also demanded much of their time, but was completed as required.
My sincere appreciation is extended to each Assistant Grand Lecturer.
One member of this committee is retiring at the close of this Grand
Lodge session. Worshipful Robert Watanabe has served this Grand Jurisdiction
in various capacities and for the past nine years, served as the Assistant Grand
Lecturer of Division II. We commend and recognize him for his dedicated
leadership, service and fraternal contributions. Indeed, it has been our pleasure
to work with Bob and his wife Sharon and this committee will certainly miss
him. Please join me in applauding Worshipful Bob for his years of dedication.
In conclusion, my sincere appreciation is extended to Most Worshipful
M. William Holsinger and his wife Jean. It has been a pleasure to serve under
and with such a fine leader. This Jurisdiction can be proud to have had his
capable leadership during some challenging times. Many thanks to our Grand
Secretary and his most capable staff for all their fine assistance and cooperation.
The labors and dedication of each Inspector cannot be overlooked as their
position and duties are so important. We appreciate and thank each of them for
all their time and concerted efforts.
And to you, my Brethren, thank you for your friendship, confidence
and kind support in allowing me to serve as your Grand Lecturer.

Respectfully submitted.
Harold A. Macy, Grand Lecturer

APPENDIX G

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

195

GRAND CHAPLAIN’S MESSAGE
by The Very Reverend George L. Davis

God give us men and women!
Times like these demand strong hearts, true faith and ready hands –
Men and women whom the lust of office does not fill.
Those whom the spoils of office cannot buy
Those who have honor those who will not lie
Men and women who can stand before an enemy or demagogue and damn their
Treacherous flatteries without winding tall souls sun crowned who live above
The fog in public duty and in private thinking –
God give us such men and women!

Y’ gotta get a glory in the work you do –
A hallelujah chorus in the hearts of you
Paint or tell a story, sing or shovel coal –
Y’ gotta have a glory or the job lacks soul!
So Lord give me a glory, is it much to give
For y’ gotta have a glory or you just don’t live!
The great whose shining labors make our pulses throb.
Are folks who had a glory in their daily job. The battle may be
boring and the odds unfair – but the one who’s got a glory never knows despair.
So Lord, give me a glory and a workman’s pride – for y’ gotta get a glory
or you’re dead inside.

196

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Walk the world proudly, wherever you are!
You’re a soul out of space sent to grace this earth star
You’re a soul sent from heaven with power and worth to
bring a bit of heaven to earth –
So, walk the world proudly and walk with love too –
For you have a mission entrusted to you To lift those who stumble and those who would plod.
That they too may walk as a proud child of God.

Isn’t Life glorious – isn’t it grand!
Take it – hold it tight in your hand.
Squeeze every drop of it into your soul
Drink of the joy of it – sun-sweet and whole.
Laugh with the love of it – burst into song –
Scatter life’s riches as you stride along –
Isn’t life splendid and isn’t it great –
You can always start living – it’s never too late.

APPENDIX H

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

199

REPORT OF THE GRAND ORATOR
Most Worshipful Grand Master, Past Grand Masters, Distinguished
and Honored Guests, Grand Lodge Officers, and my Brothers.
A year ago, I was honored to stand before you and be installed as the
Grand Orator of the Grand Lodge of California. During the past year it has
been a tremendous experience to be able to travel up and down this great State
of ours delivering speeches on behalf of all the Masons of California. I was
personally able to participate in 22 cornerstone and other ceremonies from the
deserts of Southern California to the mountain communities in the North.
Unfortunately during the last four weeks, I was unable to travel due to my
health, and was limited to only writing speeches. I want to thank those Brothers
who delivered the speeches for me. I also want to extend my thanks and
appreciation to the many Brothers who sent their prayers and best wishes for my
speedy recovery.
This year, the theme of Most Worshipful William Holsinger was
“Masonry seeks good men and makes them better - tell a friend.” This
statement is a challenge to all Masons to be called to action for the benefit of
our Fraternity. Brothers, “ask not what Masonry can do for you, but what can
you do for Masonry? Of course, this call to action immediately reminds us of
our 35th President of the United States, John F. Kennedy, who on his
inauguration day called the citizens of the United States to action. Those who
responded to that call can be considered modern day heroes. But what makes a
person a hero? Is a hero someone who can leap over tall buildings in as single
bound? Can anyone be a hero? What makes a person a Masonic hero? Which
Brothers here today will answer the call to action and become a Masonic hero?
I decided to start with the dictionary definition of the word hero. What I found
were three definitions.
1. In mythology and legend, a man, often of divine ancestry, who is
endowed with great courage and strength, celebrated for his bold exploits, and
favored by the gods. To me, this seemed like a person who could leap over tall
buildings in a single bound.
2. A person noted for feats of courage or nobility of purpose,
especially one who has risked or sacrificed his or her life. To me, this sounded
like someone who changes the world.
3. A person noted for special achievement in a particular field. Now to
me, this sounded like someone who doesn’t try to change the world
intentionally, but rather works so hard at what they are trying to accomplish that
they end up changing the world along the way.

200

PROCEDINGS OF THE

While I liked the dictionaries’ definitions, I couldn’t help but feel
something was missing from the definitions I found. Yes, technically they had
described some of the characteristics of a hero, but they didn’t even come close
to describing the emotion people feel when saying the word hero.
I visually tried to picture Masonic heroes. George Washington came to
my mind and he certainly fits the person who changes the world. I was having
more difficulty trying to think of Masonic heroes that worked so hard at what
they are trying to accomplish that they end up changing the world along the
way, and then I realized that we had a number of Brothers in our Lodges that fit
this definition of a Masonic hero to a “T” and I list these Brothers in no
particular order:
How about the Brother who for the last forty years comes to the Lodge,
turns on the lights, makes the coffee, and in the winter sees that the heater is on
and in the summer sees that the air conditioner is working properly. He is the
last one to leave and makes sure the lights are turned off and the parking lot is
secure. My Brothers, he is a Masonic hero.
How about the Brother who is the first one to volunteer for the hard
jobs. Painting, plumbing, and electrical repairs are his specialty. He may be
absent at times from meetings, but he is always there when you need a project
done. My Brothers, he is a Masonic hero.
Then there’s the Brother, who visits the sick and those in the hospital.
He also sends cards and letters to his Brothers who are unable to leave their
homes. My Brothers, he is a Masonic hero.
Or the Brother who donates his time and talent to the Shrine hospitals
or local schools. My Brothers, he is also a Masonic hero.
And let us not forget the Brother who is a master of the ritual and
coaches candidates two nights a week. In teaching ritual he is able to show his
students the principles and ideals of Masonry. His job is so much more than just
memory work. My Brothers, he is a Masonic hero.
This list could go on and on forever. I am sure each of you knows
someone who is a Masonic hero. Glance at the person next to you and even
around the room. I bet someone considers the Brother sitting next to you a
Masonic hero.
These men do not think of themselves as heroes, but to me they are our
Masonic heroes. How can we honor these Brothers? How can we assure that
there will be future Masonic heroes in California? What action must be taken
today?
The first lodges in California were established in the Gold Country in
1850. That same year the Grand Lodge of California was established in
Sacramento. Today, there are more than 74,900 members and nearly 360 lodges

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

201

throughout the State. We are one of the largest Grand Lodges in the world and
yet we need to take action today.
We need to respond to the call for ACTION. WE NEED TO TAKE
THOSE STEPS NECESSARY FOR THE SURVIVAL OF OUR CRAFT.
While preparing my remarks, I researched and read books and
magazines about our craft. One author wrote about how Masonry needs each
one of you to give your time, effort, and support to your Lodge. I would like to
share some of this Brother’s points of view with you.
As Masons, we may all reach the place where we realize that unless we
do something to help our Masonic Fraternity survive, the Fraternity we all
treasure and hold dear, will in the end perish. So what can we do?
I would like to present to you what I believe are serious considerations
that may instill new life in our Masonic Fraternity for anyone that is serious
enough to change their old way of thinking and are willing to “do” some
personal adjusting within themselves.
Of course, whenever you want to experience change you need to start
by acknowledging that change cannot occur unless YOU, my fellow Brothers,
are willing to think and do something entirely different from what we have done
in the past.
Begin by getting clear about what you really want. When I talk about
getting clear, pay attention to the way you and your fellow Brothers have
thought and talked about making changes. I am sure that the thinking and
talking have prevented changes from being made.
Wake up! Most people talk themselves out of being successful in
making changes, all the while complaining they were discouraged by others. Be
clear - others cannot influence your decisions unless you decide to give them the
authority. This is precisely why you need to pay attention to how you handle
the input of others and the pep talks you give yourself and your fellow Brothers.
Do not fall victim to saying things such as, “I know it isn’t apt to
happen” or “I know this seems reasonable, but” and other such statements that
immediately undermine your commitment to change.
Use statements such as, “We will”, “We can” “We will not stop until”
and other types of messages that confirm to yourself and your fellow Brothers
that you are committed and don’t intend stopping until you succeed in making
changes. It may sound unusual, but you can and generally do stop your
successes from happening right in the thinking stage before you ever take one
deliberate step. Reassess your thinking style in order to poise yourself for
success. Always consciously commit to saying encouraging and reinforcing
types of statements that you would use with a Brother that is trying to create

202

PROCEDINGS OF THE

new and good things for our Fraternity. Remind yourself that if you can
conceive it in your mind you can make it happen, one step at a time.
Get committed! Make change a must. Here are a few prompts to keep
you on line with your goals:
Refuse to consider any seduction to quit, keep using those “We can,
We will” messages.
Look carefully at what may be holding us back in any way.
Find new courses of action that will get us beyond the place where you
are stuck.
Do more research to enable you and your Brothers to get clear about
what you need to do in order to achieve.
And keep going until you and your Brothers achieve what is desired.
There are no other options! Decide consciously and deliberately as to
what actions are needed to be taken to keep our great Fraternal family alive and
well into the next decade.
Only you, my Brothers, can learn from the past. Do not let it suppress
the future. Having failed in the past does not determine the way your future
endeavors will turn out.
Always look for a gentle approach to resolving any holdups in your
goals of revitalizing Masonry in California. Sometimes, the habit of
“toughening it out’’ or doing things the hard way make us believe that anything
of value to be achieved must be hard won. Not so. You can decide to forge a
new path for you and your Masonic Brothers. It is entirely up to you and your
fellow Brothers to decide what you want. Just so, is your ability to make it
happen.
If you find you are thinking about quitting, imagine how you and your
fellow Masons’ life will turn out if Masonry does not survive. It will help you
keep your resolution to ‘‘refuse to stop’’. Shake off any notions you have of
failing. There is no such thing as failure. Every situation allows opportunity for
education, inspiration, and new insight.
Generally speaking, most Brothers, if not all, wish for Masonry to
continue in California for years and decades to come. They hope that
circumstances will change to allow such longevity. Each Brother must show a
willingness to attempt new behaviors and thinking in order to create a selfsustaining Fraternity. Although there is much over which we have no control,
there is also much that each Brother can do to help the brotherhood survive. It
is not always easy, although the premise is simple.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

203

I am sure all of us have a vision. Here is my vision which was adapted
from a fellow Mason’s vision.
Twenty-five years ago a Mason had to wait for an opening in the
“line”, sometimes for years. Thirty was the average age of a Mason. He was
considered honorable and notable in the Lodge and the community and Masons
were respected individuals in their neighborhoods.
Look at us today. Gone are the events that swelled our banquet halls.
The parades and pageantry of the old days are gone, fallen prey to community
indifference. Some Lodges are barely able to achieve a quorum. The average
age of the membership is double than what it was 30 years ago. Every year, our
organizations suffer net losses. The occupation of the average Mason is
“retired”. The average Mason's service history is "Been there - done that". Due
to the lack of new members, chairs are hard to fill. Some members cannot serve
because they are too old, too infirmed, can't see to drive, and so on.
So, what will the future of Masonry be like? I believe we stand at a
crossroad - choose one. The boulevard to failure or the path towards success.
Imagine: Lodges again filled to capacity. New petitions will be read at
every meeting. There will be a waiting line for persons to fill chairs. The
average Mason's age is once again 30. His occupation is blue collar, white
collar, and retired. Childcare is offered for children downstairs during the
meeting. Internet access is available on computers in the lodge for spouses
during meetings. Computer games for the youth. Large screen televisions in
reception rooms with cable TV for entertainment. The Lodge is open for
aerobics on meeting nights for member's wives. Pre schools are available
during the day for the children of Masons. Baby-sitting services are available
during Lodge events. Vans are available to bring Lodge members to attend
meetings and social events. Does this sound Farfetched? Not to me.
If we did nothing and expected change, what would our organizations
look like then? To be successful, we will need a new strength and strategy.
So why doesn't what we did in the past, work today?
because times have changed.

Maybe its

How many Brothers here today have or had fathers who were Masons?
Grandfather’s who were Mason’s? How many of you joined because you had a
friend in a Lodge? How many Brothers here today have children that are 18
years or older? Of those Brothers how many of your children are Masons?
The point is, that we also need to look at our own family for future
members.
Lifestyles have changed: In 1960 the man worked, the wife was a
homemaker. He came home, ate dinner, visited with the kids, and then he was
off to his Lodge meeting. Today, both the man and wife work. Dinner is fast

204

PROCEDINGS OF THE

food or the parents take turns cooking. Both come home to homework, laundry,
and bills. The wife is just as tired as the husband and watching the kids just so
he can run out to a Lodge meeting is a burden.
The complexity of the family has changed. Single families abound.
Parents of the breadwinners may have to be cared for.
Community life- is non-existent. There is no civic pride anymore. Life
is too transient.
Technological advancements abound. The world now comes to our
doorstep. E-mail and Internet services is where the world shops, communicates,
and gets involved.
We now live in a 24 hour society. While the world runs around the
clock, the lodge is open only a few hours each week. Why?
Have our norms also changed?
In a few years, the largest majority by age will be those over 65 years.
So, what is the answer? It comes from us, you my fellow Brothers.
We need to change and adapt to current trends. But, change is not easy. We
need to go where the candidates are in order to reach them and then connect
with them in some way. In doing so we have to be careful that we attract men
that meet our Masonic standards. Those men who will become our Masonic
heroes and have the utmost INTEGRITY.
Our commitment to integrity occurs whether or not there is a system of
checks and balances peering over our shoulders. Are you the type of person
who only pauses at a red light, rather than coming to a full stop, when it is 2 in
the morning? Are we, as a part of the Masonic family, assuring that this
organization will remain standing for generations to come? Do we accurately
assess our participation in this organization so that our grandchildren’s children
will know the wonders of the Masonic Fraternity that we know?
For instance, Masonry cannot continue into the next generation unless
we have a plan for bringing qualified candidates into the Fraternity. As a
United States Navy man, I know, for example, that our young veterans would
make ideal candidates for membership. They have already made a commitment
to uphold the Constitution of the United States and the principles hammered out
by our brethren Founding Fathers. Depending on each other while in service to
our country, these veterans also understand the concept of a Fraternal
organization. The analogies between the experiences of our veterans and of the
types of candidates we seek to join the Fraternity go on and on. This is but one
example of a job that we need to do well, so that like a well-built house, the
Masonic Fraternity will have a strong foundation to carry it beyond this
generation.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

205

We also need to work with the other Fraternal Organizations that make
up our Masonic Family. A commitment must be made to each of them to insure
their continued survival.
When we look at protecting the legacy and future of the Masonic
Fraternity, we have some choices. We can choose the Masonic Lodge where we
would like to belong; or we can choose whether we want to wear a black or
white tuxedo jacket to a function. At the same time, there are some things that
are not optional, such as assuring a credible future membership. We cannot hope
to rest on the laurels of our many years of existence.
The continuation of our Fraternity is dependant on our exemplary
work, our dedication to the craft, and our ability to adapt to the changing world.
If we are to move into the 21st century in a proactive way, we must start
making changes now. Now is the time to commit to the survival of
Freemasonry in California. Let’s look for those who will respond to the call for
action and become our future Masonic heroes.
Brethren, I thank you.
Respectfully submitted,
William J. Bray III, Grand Orator

GRAND MASTER’S DECISIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS
Grand Master Holsinger’s Decision No. 1
Advertising and Public Relations by Lodges
I was asked to interpret the meaning of Section 15115 of the California
Masonic Code as it pertains to advertising and public relations by our
constituent lodges. Section 15115 contains language that, at first glance,
appears to be rather restrictive. It states, in pertinent part, that a lodge may:
Publish in a newspaper of general circulation an announcement
showing the Masonic emblem, the name and number of the Lodge and the dates
of meetings.
Narrowly interpreted, that section of the code seems to say that this is
all a lodge can do to inform the public of its existence as a Masonic lodge in the
community. If that were the case, lodge websites would not be legal, because
they are not a “newspaper of general circulation,” nor would it be legal for a
lodge to inform the community of special programs that benefit the public, such
as the Child I.D. Program, nor would it permit a lodge to inform the public
about Freemasonry, or seek publicity about lodge events. Such was clearly not
the intent of Section 15115, which also speaks of specific lodge publications,
such as a roster. The intent of this section is to list some of those utilitarian
publications pertaining to lodge business which are permitted, but it does not list
every kind of publication that is permitted. For example, it does not restrict the
lodge from telling the community about Freemasonry in general, nor the
activities of the lodge in particular. Indeed, Grand Master R. Stephen Doan’s
Decision No. 2, approved by vote of Grand Lodge in 1993, clearly states that
“Masons may sponsor programs to explain what Masonry is and invite nonmasons to the program in an attempt to create a favorable impression toward the
fraternity.”
It is therefore my decision that Section 15115 of the California
Masonic Code pertains only to the subjects contained in that section, namely
publications pertaining to the business of the lodge, and not to a lodge public
relations program which includes informing the public about Freemasonry in
general, and the activities of the lodge in particular.
If you approve this decision, the following narrative will be added to
the appendix of the California Masonic Code in which the Grand Master’s
interpretative decisions are recorded:

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

210

Interpretation of Section 15115 of the California Masonic Code.
Advertising & Public Relations by Lodges
(2003 G.M. Decision No. 2)
Section 15115 of the California Masonic Code pertains only to the
subjects contained in that section, namely publications pertaining to the business
of the lodge, and not to a lodge public relations program which includes
informing the public about Freemasonry in general, and the activities of the
lodge in particular.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Our Grand Master M. William Holsinger has been asked to interpret
Section 15115 of the California Masonic Code as it pertains to advertising and
public relations of our constituent lodges. Section 15115 contains language
that, at first glance appears to be rather restrictive. It states in part that a lodge
may “publish in a news paper of general circulation an announcement showing
the Masonic emblem, the name and number of the lodge and the date of
meetings.”
In 1999, this Grand Lodge enlarged its identification with members of
the public by a Public Relations Program defining the origin, the activities and
the meaning of our Grand Lodge Programs. In subsequent years and in
different forms Grand Masters have continued to encourage the public relations
process necessary to gain acceptance by the general public of our fraternity and
endeavor to restore it to its early prominence and membership.
Grand Master Doan recognized this problem in 1993 clearly stating,
“Masons may sponsor programs to explain what Masonry is and invite nonMasons to the programs in an attempt to create a favorable impression toward
the fraternity.” Thus we have had positive impact on new membership by virtue
of our non-Mason programs to which we as members invite those who were not
members of the Craft so as to inform them of who and what we were.
Our Grand Master in his Decision No. 1 fully sets forth his ideas of
what a lodge Public Relations program should be.
The Decision is consistent with the Policies and General Purposes of
this Grand Lodge.
Decision AFFIRMED.

Grand Master Holsinger’s Recommendation No. 2003-01
One Day Conferrals
There has been a growing interest in the idea of the conferral of all
three degrees of Ancient Craft Masonry in one day throughout the United
States. Today more than half the Grand Lodges in the United States allow this
as an option to supplement the traditional conferral of the degrees one at a time
over a period of weeks or months. I believe that it is now time for California to
join this movement. I therefore propose that we amend our code to allow the
optional conferral of all three degrees in the same day, but under strict controls,
and only with the permission of the Grand Master.
In order to do this, it will be necessary to amend some portions of the
California Masonic Code to allow for one-day conferrals to occur. The
following changes will have to be made:
1.

The present limit on the number of candidates that can be
accommodated at any one time must be waived.

2.

The present requirement that a candidate memorize the Obligation, and
the Words, Signs and Modes of Recognition must be waived, along
with the mandatory Masonic Education requirement.

Nothing else needs to be changed, although the legislation as proposed
would allow the Grand Master to waive any pertinent parts of the code
necessary to implement the “one day conferral.” I therefore recommend that the
following language be added to the California Masonic Code which would
permit the conferral of all three degrees in one day on any number of candidates.
If you adopt this recommendation, Section 26280 of the California
Masonic Code will be amended to read as follows and a new Section 26285 will
be added to the California Masonic Code to read as follows:

§26280. RESTRICTIONS ON CONFERRING DEGREES.
Except as provided in Section 26285, nNo Lodge in this Jurisdiction
shall confer:
A.

Any degree on Sunday;

B.

Degrees upon more than five candidates at any one meeting;

C.

More than one degree upon a candidate at any one meeting;

D.

Any of the degrees upon more than one candidate at a time;

E.

More than five degrees in any one day;

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

212

F.

A degree where the Lodge has been called down for the
purpose of examination;

G.

A degree without completing it at the time it is conferred,
except because of an emergency over which Lodge officers
have no control; or

H.

A degree or any part of a degree prior to the time fixed for
convening a meeting therefor.

§26285. ONE DAY CONFERRAL OF DEGREES.
Nothing in the Ritual or the California Masonic Code shall prohibit the
Grand Master from authorizing the conferral of one or more of the
degrees of Freemasonry on any number of candidates at one time at
one or more locations within this Jurisdiction. The proficiency and
Masonic education requirements for those thus initiated, passed and/or
raised shall be determined by the Grand Master.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Grand Master’s Recommendation No. 2003-1 would permit the
conferral of all three degrees of Masonry on multiple candidates in a single day.
It is believed by many that this change in our ritual is necessary to avoid
Masonry in California shortly becoming a thing of the past. Our membership
has been in decline for many years. Since 1965, the number of California
Masons has dropped by almost 75%. We have a highly aged membership that is
dying off far more rapidly than the pace at which we currently initiate new
candidates. All projections indicate this steep decline in membership will
continue unabated.
Currently, 35 jurisdictions across the United States permit one-day
conferrals. Experience in these other jurisdictions has shown that the one-day
conferral is an excellent way to increase membership. Many states, such as
Ohio and New York, have reported astounding results. Last year in Ohio,
approximately 7750 men became Master Masons in one day in a statewide
conferral coordinated through the 10 Scottish Rite Valleys in that state.
Furthermore, most jurisdictions have reported that men receiving the one day
conferral, many of whom are civic leaders, have become active in their
respective Lodges, and the Lodges themselves have been rejuvenated by the
influx of new members.
This Recommendation contains the same safeguards for screening
members as does the current California Masonic Code, thus assuring that only
men of good character will be considered for membership in our fraternity.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

213

Under this Recommendation, the proposed candidate still must have
recommenders and references. It will still be the responsibility of the Lodge to
receive and review the application for degrees, investigate the candidate, and
ballot on the petition. Furthermore, it will be up to each Lodge to decide
whether or not to participate in a one-day conferral. Each Lodge will still be
free to confer the degrees in the traditional manner.
This Recommendation represents a change in the current policy of this
Grand Lodge. However, it is consistent with the general purposes for which this
Grand Lodge has been established.

Recommendation CARRIED-OVER.
Grand Master Holsinger’s Recommendation No. 2003-02
Flexible Dates for Annual Communication
Section 410 of the California Masonic Code specifies that the Annual
Communication of this Grand Lodge shall always commence on the second
Monday in October. The inflexibility of this arrangement means that we have
no choice in negotiating better rates for hotels when other major citywide events
conflict with our Annual Communication, such as Fleet Week. In addition, in
many years our Annual Communication occurs at the same time as the
observance of Yom Kippur, creating a burden on our members of the Jewish
faith who must choose between the Annual Communication and their religious
observance. It is therefore my recommendation that we amend the California
Masonic Code to allow the holding of the Annual Communication on a specific
day within the twenty days of the second Monday in October as may be selected
by the Grand Master at least one year in advance. Such advance notice will give
ample opportunity to make hotel reservations well before the scheduled date.
If you adopt this recommendation, Section 410 of the California
Masonic Code will be amended to read as follows:

§410. ANNUAL.
Grand Lodge shall hold an Annual Communication for the transaction
of its regular business commencing at 9:00 a.m. on the second Monday
of each October, in the City and County of San Francisco.
commencing at a time and on a day within twenty (20) days before or
after the second Monday in October, as selected and announced by the
Grand Master at least one year in advance.

214

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Grand Master Holsinger’s Recommendation Number 2 seeks to
establish flexible dates for the Annual Communication of this Grand Lodge.
From 1864 to 1955, 91 years, this Grand Lodge met on the second
Monday of October. From 1956 to 1961 the date of the Annual Communication
was changed to the last week in September.
At the Annual Communication of 1961, Grand Master Breslauer
recommended that the Annual Communication be changed back to its original
date, as established in 1864, to the second Monday in October. He noted in his
recommendation that those Brethren engaged in agriculture and those whose
business’s required their attention in the area of bookkeeping and accounting,
found the end of September to be most inconvenient to them. He also noted that
there will always be someone who will be inconvenienced by the time (and
date) fixed for the Annual Communication. His recommendation was adopted
by a 5/6 vote.
In 1996, a resolution, which is similar, to this recommendation: IE
changing the Annual Communication to the third week in October was defeated.
The San Francisco Convention Bureau reports that although there are
numerous conventions and meetings 20 days before, during and 20 days after
our Communication, the average room rate does not fluctuate during this period.
There will always be someone who will be inconvenienced by
whatever time or date is selected. Changing the date each year for the Annual
Communication is not consistent with the Policy or General Purposes of this
Grand Lodge.

Recommendation CARRIED-OVER.
Grand Master Holsinger’s Recommendation No. 2003-03
Finance Committee Duties
The duties of the Grand Lodge Finance Committee have changed in
recent years, and it is necessary to revise the language in the California Masonic
Code to reflect those changes. Some of the duties are now the responsibility of
the Grand Secretary. Perhaps most important of all, supervision of the
California Masonic Retirement Plan is now the responsibility of the Finance
Committee. Finally, the statement concerning the changing of the Grand Lodge
Budget should be transferred to the section pertaining to the Grand Lodge
budget where it belongs. It is therefore my recommendation that the code be
revised to reflect these changes.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

215

If you adopt this recommendation, Sections 6100 and 7040 of the
California Masonic Code will be amended to read as follows:

§6100. FINANCE.
The Committee on Finance shall:
A.

Assist and consult with the Grand Master, Grand Treasurer
and Grand Secretary regarding the financial affairs of Grand
Lodge Administer the California Masonic Retirement Plan, on

California. This Plan has been established for the benefit of
the employees of the Grand Lodge and its entities, including
the Masonic Service Bureaus, and such other entities as may
qualify under the Plan. The Finance Committee shall perform
all the duties and responsibilities and exercise all powers of
the Retirement Plan Committee under the Plan;

B.

Advise and assist all boards in the Grand Lodge and its
entities regarding their financial matters, when requested;

C.

Assist in the preparation of and monitor compliance with the
Grand Lodge Budget to the extent provided in Section 7040 of
this Code;

D.

Estimate and make provision for the reimbursement of the
expenses of Grand Lodge officers, Past Grand Masters,
Inspectors, and members of committees and boards as
approved by the Grand Master or adopted by Grand Lodge
Review the proposed annual Grand Lodge Budget and
recommend to Grand Lodge whether to adopt the Budget as
presented prior to the vote; and

E.

Supervise compliance with the adopted budget of Grand
Lodge;

F.

Prescribe procedures for the filing of all expense claims and
be responsible for approval or disallowance of the claims;

G.

Report on every appropriation of money before it can be
made; and

H.

Upon receipt from the Grand Secretary of proposed

of the cost impact of each, if adopted, and transmit, at least 75
days prior to the commencement date of the next Annual

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

216

Communication, a report of such cost estimates to the Grand
Secretary.
Every over-expenditure of budgeted items, needing supplemental
appropriations approved by the Grand Master, shall be referred to and

No appropriation of money shall be made at any Annual

§7040. GRAND LODGE BUDGET.
Grand Lodge shall have a budget for each fiscal year as follows:
A.

The Grand Lodge Budget shall be prepared under the
direction and control of the Deputy Grand Master. The Grand
Treasurer and the Grand Lodge Committee on Finance shall
consult in the preparation of the Grand Lodge Budget as
requested by the Deputy Grand Master. The Deputy Grand
Master shall be assisted in the preparation of the budget by
such other persons as he may select to assist him.

B.

Each Grand Lodge Budget shall be based upon the per capita
sum to be collected for Master Masons carried on the rolls of
each Constituent Lodge as of the June 30th most immediately
preceding the commencement of the fiscal year covered by
such budget.

C.

The proposed Grand Lodge Budget shall be transmitted by the
Grand Secretary to the Masters of the several Lodges,
Inspectors and members of the Committee on Finance at least
30 days prior to the commencement date of the next Annual
Communication.

D.

The proposed Grand Lodge Budget shall be referred to the
Committee on Finance for its review and report to Grand
Lodge before the vote is taken whether to adopt the budget.

E.

The Deputy Grand Master shall present the proposed budget
to the members of Grand Lodge for their approval. The
Grand Lodge Budget shall be adopted by a majority vote.

F.

The Grand Treasurer and the Committee on Finance shall
monitor compliance with the adopted Grand Lodge Budget
during the Grand Lodge year.

G.

There shall be three expense categories in the Grand Lodge
Budget:
fraternal, appropriations and general and

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

217

administrative. An expense category in the Grand Lodge
Budget shall not be exceeded without the consent of the
Grand Master. An expense category shall not be exceeded by
more than five percent without the consent of at least two of
the following: the Grand Secretary, the Grand Treasurer and
the Chairman of the Committee on Finance.
H.

No appropriation of money shall be made at any Annual
Communication after the election of Grand Officers, except by
a three-fourths vote of the members present.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Grand Master Holsinger's Recommendation No. 3 seeks to revise
certain language in the California Masonic Code to reflect recent legislative
changes in the duties of the Grand Lodge Finance Committee and to place some
of its other duties in more appropriate Sections of the Code.
These changes are of a housekeeping nature and will update and clarify
the duties of the Grand Lodge Finance Committee.
This recommendation is thus consistent with Grand Lodge policy and
general purposes.

Recommendation ADOPTED.
Grand Master Holsinger’s Recommendation No. 2003-04
CMMT Restructure
In an ongoing effort to reduce the cost of operations of the Grand
Lodge and its entities, Grand Lodge has, in the past, approved the restructuring
of committees and boards to provide services at a lower cost. In the spirit of this
effort, I recommend that the Board of Trustees of the California Masonic
Memorial Temple be reduced from nine members to five members. Although
the board only meets quarterly, the elimination of four trustee positions will
save money without sacrificing the needed oversight which the trustees provide.
Most of the business operations are now conducted by Nob Hill Masonic
Management, which rents the auditorium, exhibit hall and parking garage. The
need for a large board to conduct the business of the California Masonic
Memorial Temple has therefore been reduced, and I recommend that this costsaving measure be approved.
In implementing this legislation, the Grand Master shall have the
power to adjust and/or terminate the terms of existing trustees to accommodate
the intent of this legislation.

218

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

If you adopt this recommendation, Section 10020 of the California
Masonic Code will be amended to read as follows:

§10020. COMPOSITION OF BOARD.
The Trustees of the California Masonic Memorial Temple shall be nine
five in number. The Junior Grand Warden shall be a Trustee
ex-officio. The other eight four Trustees shall be appointed by the
Grand Master for terms of three years each. The terms of at least two
one of the appointed Trustees shall expire at the close of each Annual
Communication of Grand Lodge. When a vacancy occurs, the
appointee shall serve the remainder of the unexpired term. The Grand
Master may remove a Trustee at any time, upon request of two-thirds
of the Trustees, and appoint another.
If a member shall have served for nine consecutive years, including
service as a Chairman, he shall be ineligible for reappointment until the
lapse of one year.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
In the course of governing our Grand Lodge the sitting Grand Master
of necessity has to view not only the economics of our Grand Lodge but the
necessity to changing the numbers and membership of our various committees
to suit the ever changing duties and responsibilities of those committees.
Under Section 10020 of the California Masonic Code the California
Masonic Memorial Temple Board presently has nine members. The Grand
Master believes that the job of the Board can easily be accomplished with five.
The Board meets only quarterly; the change in numbers will be a reduction of
four Trustee positions and will save money without sacrificing the needed
oversight which the Trustees provide. Nob Hill Masonic Management performs
many of the function that ordinarily the Trustees of the California Masonic
Temple would perform, the number of members should be reduced and so the
Grand Master recommends that Section 10020, Composition of the Board of the
California Masonic Memorial Temple be amended to change the number of the
trustees from nine to five. The Grand Master by this recommendation is acting
in his executive capacity. This Recommendation is consistent with the policy
and general purposes of this Grand Lodge.

Recommendation ADOPTED.

Grand Master Whitaker’s Recommendation No. 1
Safeguards for Lodge Finances and Investments
The Grand Lodge Investment Committee has asked me to propose
changes in the California Masonic Code to better safeguard the finances and
investments of our Lodges. I am therefore asking you to adopt the changes
below in our law to better protect Lodge assets.
If you adopt this recommendation, the California Masonic Code will
be amended as marked:

§21010. CONTROL AND USE.
A Lodge must retain control of its own funds and property (other than
real property). It may deposit its funds only in a sound and reputable
depository institution bank or banks that is regulated and insured
(including FDIC for banks, NCUA for credit unions or SIPC for
brokerages). A Chartered Lodge may, after receiving consent from the
appropriate Grand Lodge Committee on Temple Properties and
Taxation, cause the incorporation of a separate entity Temple
Association for the purpose of purchasing, erecting or maintaining and
holding title to real estate for a temple for Lodge uses and purposes;
and a Lodge or Lodges may hold shares of stock in such corporation.
Space in sSuch temple real estate may be rented for commercial and
other uses consistent with the purposes of the Lodge or the corporation
and not in violation of this Code.
A Lodge shall not incorporate. It shall not divide its funds or property
with its members, even those withdrawing to form a new Lodge or with
the new Lodge thus formed. A Lodge shall not divide its funds with an
existing Lodge by way of a contribution.
A Lodge shall adopt a budget for the following twelve months at the
first Stated Meeting held after the annual installation of the officers of
the Lodge.
No money shall be drawn without the express consent of the Lodge,
even though included in a Lodge budget, except that:
A.

Dispensed by the Charity Committee;

B.

Due Grand Lodge; and

C.

Required to pay the taxes or other charges of local, State or
Federal governmental agencies.

220

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Lodge may approve bills against the Lodge only when the bills are
supported by proper documentation, such as an invoice or receipt. No
warrant check or draft shall be drawn issued unless there are sufficient
funds to honor it.
A Lodge shall not invest any of its funds in any manner or obligate
itself in any way for the purpose of acquiring land or building a
Masonic temple except upon compliance with the provisions of this
Code.
A Lodge shall not establish or form a foundation separate from the
Lodge with power to receive, hold, administer, invest or reinvest funds
received by the Lodge.

§21030. INVESTMENT.
Exclusive of a Lodge’s investment in an incorporated Temple
Association real estate entity as set forth in Section 21010, a Lodge
may invest its funds only as provided in this section. All Lodge
investments shall be held in the name of the Lodge. The Lodge’s
investments allowed under this section shall be administered either by
an investment committee appointed by the Master or by a Board of
Trustees elected by the Lodge. If a Lodge uses a Board of Trustees
elected by the Lodge, tThe Trustees shall be governed by a standing
resolution of the Lodge, the form of which shall be promulgated by the
Grand Master.
The Trustees shall consist of at least three members of the Lodge, each
serving staggered three-year terms, and shall be elected at the Stated
Meeting next succeeding the annual installation of Lodge officers.
Each Trustee shall sign, and be governed by, a Conflict-of-Interest
Statement as promulgated by the Grand Master. One who acts as
trustee, for other than the Lodge, under any trust agreement by the
terms of which the Lodge has a present or future interest, direct or
indirect, is ineligible as a Lodge trustee, and the Master shall
immediately remove him as a Lodge trustee when the Master becomes
aware of such other position. The vacancy shall be filled in the manner
provided for in the standing resolution.
The Lodge Trustees shall have the power to acquire, sell and convey all
Lodge investments and to invest and reinvest its funds. The Trustees
may exercise such powers, provided that the Trustees shall always be
under the control of the Lodge, and may not make any final investment
decisions without prior approval of the Lodge. The Trustees may take
emergency action without prior Lodge approval only if they deem the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

221

action to be financially necessary and prudent. In such circumstances,
the action taken will be presented to the Lodge at the next Stated
Meeting.
The Lodge Trustees shall provide a full oral and written report of their
activities as such at the February and August Lodge Stated Meetings.
This report shall include at a minimum total and annualized
performance results, contributions and withdrawals and any changes in
the investments since the previous report.
Through the Lodge investment committee or Board of Trustees, the
Lodge shall confer and counsel as to any proposed or contemplated
investments under this section with a Registered Investment Advisor of
its choice. A Lodge may freely choose to accept or reject any and all
recommendations made to it by the Advisor. Alternatively, a Lodge
may retain a Registered Investment Advisor operating as an
professional investment manager who offers standardized portfolio
management services through a managed investment account. Any
Registered Investment Advisor or professional investment manager
utilized by the Lodge shall execute a Conflict-of-Interest Statement as
promulgated by the Grand Master.
A Lodge shall not margin, borrow against or hypothecate any of its
investments.
A Lodge shall not sell its property for less than fair market value of the
property. Where there is no ready market to establish the fair market
value of a holding, the value for sale shall be determined by a qualified
and independent appraiser.
A Lodge may invest its assets, exclusive of Lodge Temple property and
Lodge Temple securities, as follows: Except for investments held by a
Center/Hall Association, a Lodge shall invest its assets as follows:
A.

At least 20% The greater of six months of the annual budgeted
expenses of the Lodge, or 10% of the assets available for
investment, as valued at the time of deposit or purchase, must
be kept in:
1.

Cash or cash equivalents;

2.

Accounts of depository institutions insured by the Federal
Deposit Insurance Corporation, including but not limited
to checking accounts, savings accounts and certificates of
deposit up to a maximum of one-year maturity;

3.

Obligations of the United States Government or
obligations whose payment is pledged by the full faith
and credit of the United States Government up to a

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

222

maximum of one-year maturity;
4.

Obligations of the State of California and its political
subdivisions including by not limited to general
obligation bonds, revenue bonds, warrants, revenue
anticipation notes, certificates of participation and
comparable securities;

45.

Short-term evidence of indebtedness with a maturity
of not greater than one year and whose issuer holds a
credit quality of Prime A1, A2, or A3, among the top two
rating tiers of a nationally recognized fixed income rating
agency, as determined by a nationally-recognized credit
rating organization, including but not limited to

agreements, certificates of deposit bonds, debentures and

56. Investment companies or investment trusts Mutual funds
(open- or closed-end) registered pursuant to the

B.

Up to 80% The remainder of the assets available for

1.

Evidences of indebtedness, preferred stocks and common

Investment companies or investment trusts Mutual funds

Investor Rule. Particular consideration shall be given to
the overall diversification and asset allocation of the
investment portfolio. All other forms of investment are
prohibited without prior written approval of the Grand
Master.

The Trustees will periodically (at least annually) rebalance the Lodge
investment portfolio to assure compliance with subsection A of this
section. In no event shall any combination of deposits in a single
institution exceed the appropriate fund deposit insurance limits.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

223

§21040. TITLE TO ASSETS.
A Lodge may hold title to all of its assets with the power to acquire,
mortgage, sell and convey property and to invest and reinvest its funds.
It may, through trustees provided for in a standing resolution, exercise
such powers, provided that they shall always be under the control of
the Lodge, and may not buy property for the Lodge or sell property of
the Lodge without prior approval of the Lodge.
One who acts as trustee, for other than the Lodge, under any trust
agreement by the terms of which the Lodge has a present or future
interest, direct or indirect, is ineligible as a Lodge trustee and the
Master shall immediately remove him. The office shall be filled in the
manner provided for in the standing resolution.
A Lodge shall not sell its property for less than fair market value of the
property.

§21050. LIFE MEMBERSHIP.
The original amounts paid for life memberships shall be forever kept
and maintained as and for a permanent Life Membership Fund. Only
interest or dividends paid in cash on bank deposits or authorized
investments in securities investment earnings and realized capital gains
of the Fund shall be available for general Lodge use. A Lodge may
invest its life membership funds provided for in this Code under
Section 21030 B if such investments are kept separate and apart from
all other Lodge assets.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Our Grand Master, at the request of the Grand Lodge Investment
Committee, recommends amendments to California Masonic Code Sections
21010, 21030 and 21050 with deletion of 21040, all of which deal with Lodge
funds and real estate.
Presently a Lodge has the option to provide for the corporate holding
of real estate. Adoption of this Recommendation, including the revision from
the optional or permissive word “may” to the mandatory word “shall” would
require that all real estate of Lodges be held and administered by a corporation.
Such incorporation can provide a Lodge, its members and its funds a
shield from liability for personal injuries and financial risks due to loan
foreclosures. As Lodges cannot incorporate pursuant to Section 21010,

224

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

corporate protection would be afforded through incorporated ownership.
This Recommendation would also permit a standardized procedure for
ownership of real property throughout our Lodges in California with Uniform
Articles of Incorporation and Uniform By-Laws already established by Grand
Lodge pursuant to Section 22010.
The explanation of the technical provisions regarding conflicts of
interest, reports, limitation of investment of funds and administration of
investments by a Board of Trustees elected by the Lodge are referred to in the
Grand Master’s Recommendation and need not be reiterated.
Each of the recommendations further safeguard Lodge finances and
investments and therefore this Recommendation serves the best interests and
general purposes of our Grand Lodge.

Recommendation NOT ADOPTED.
Grand Master Whitaker’s Recommendation No. 4
Changing Name of California Masonic Memorial Temple To
California Masonic Memorial Center
Although Freemasonry is most emphatically not a religion, nor a
substitute for a religion, some of the terms used by Freemasons are construed by
the public to imply that we are. The use of our traditional language within our
tiled Lodges and a tiled Grand Lodge poses no problem; the use of some of our
traditional language on our public buildings does. In particular, the use of the
word “temple” on our buildings leads to the wrong impression on the part of the
public that our buildings are used for some time of “worship” in competition
with religious organizations. That is certainly not the case, but it is hard to
make this clear when we use the word “temple” on our buildings.
In recent years we have been asking our Lodges to discontinue the use
of the word “temple” to describe their buildings, returning to the use of the
much older term “Masonic hall” or using the more modern term “Masonic
center.” If this is a good recommendation for our Lodges, it is also a good
recommendation for Grand Lodge. I am therefore proposing that the California
Masonic Memorial Temple be renamed the California Masonic Memorial
Center. This change has the unanimous support of the Board of Trustees of the
California Masonic Memorial Temple, and indeed, we have been informally
using the term California Masonic Memorial Center in many of our publications
over the past several years without any negative effect.
I therefore recommend that the California Masonic Memorial Temple
be renamed the California Masonic Memorial Center.
If you adopt this recommendation, the California Masonic Code will be

ARTICLE X
CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE CENTER

§10010. PURPOSE.
The non-profit corporation, known as the California Masonic Memorial
Temple Center, shall hold title to and operate the California Masonic
Memorial Temple Center and all property owned by it.

§10020. COMPOSITION OF BOARD.
The Trustees of the California Masonic Memorial Temple Center shall
be nine in number. The Junior Grand Warden shall be a Trustee exofficio. The other eight Trustees shall be appointed by the Grand
Master for terms of three years each. The terms of at least two of the
appointed Trustees shall expire at the close of each Annual
Communication of Grand Lodge. When a vacancy occurs, the
appointee shall serve the remainder of the unexpired term. The Grand
Master may remove a Trustee at any time, upon request of two-thirds
of the Trustees, and appoint another.
If a member shall have served for nine consecutive years, including
service as a Chairman, he shall be ineligible for reappointment until the
lapse of one year.

§10030. MANAGEMENT.
The Trustees of the California Masonic Memorial Temple Center shall
elect officers within forty-five days after the close of each Annual
Communication of Grand Lodge. The Grand Secretary shall be
secretary and the Grand Treasurer shall be treasurer of the Board of
Trustees. They shall adopt, alter or amend By-laws for their own
guidance and for the management of the corporation, all subject to
approval of the Grand Master. They shall have the right to incur
indebtedness, but not to borrow money without the consent of the
Grand Master. The Grand Master shall at all times have the right of
visitation and inspection, and the supervision of the entire
management.

The Grand Master is recommending that the California Masonic
Memorial Temple be renamed the California Masonic Memorial Center.

use the term “Center” for advertising and clerical purposes only. Many
California lodges have adopted the term “Center” rather than “Temple” to avoid
a religious connotation.
The official name change requires changing all references from
“California Masonic Memorial Temple” to “California Masonic Memorial
Center.”
This Recommendation is in accordance with the policy and general
purposes of the Grand Lodge.

Resolution 02-01
Alternative Dues Structure
The proponents of this Resolution seek to permit Lodges to
automatically increase dues when the Grand Lodge per capita is increased.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the California Masonic Code requires that each Lodge in
California adopt the Uniform By-Laws in Section 19040, which includes only
one method to set the dues of a member; and

230

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

WHEREAS, for the last several years, many Masters and Wardens
have asked for the ability of their lodges to adopt an alternative method to set
the annual dues; and
WHEREAS, the use of a formula to determine the dues of a member
does not violate any Masonic Tenet, there is no reason why a lodge should not
be able to adopt a formula to set its dues; and
WHEREAS, only one of the methods to set the dues of a member can
be included in a Lodge’s By-Laws, and, if this Resolution is adopted and a
Lodge desires to use the formula, rather than a singular dues amount, it need
only follow the procedures in Article XIII of the Uniform By-Laws to do so.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Article IX of Section
19040, Uniform By-Laws, of the California Masonic Code be amended as
follows:
ARTICLE IX
DUES
Section 1.
The dues of each member of this Lodge shall be
$_____ per annum, payable in advance on the first day of January.

Or in the alternative

The dues of each member of this Lodge shall be the sum of $______
plus the Per Capita sum approved by Grand Lodge at the immediately
preceding Annual Communication, per annum, payable in advance on
the first day of January.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to establish a method by which
dues of a Lodge would automatically increase or decrease based on the Grand
Lodge per capita assessment. It would establish an optional method for
determining the dues for a Lodge and the Lodge would choose one of the two
methods.
A Lodge could adopt (a) a fixed amount for dues as now required or
(b) a fixed amount for the dues of the Lodge plus the per capita assessment
approved by Grand Lodge. If a Lodge chooses to adopt the alternative dues
structure, it would only have to follow the procedures in Article III of the
Uniform By-Laws to do so. Life memberships would not be affected by this
change.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

231

In 1998, a Resolution that mandated Lodges to change their dues
structure so that the per capita assessment automatically raised or lowered the
dues was defeated. This Resolution differs from that Resolution in that each
Lodge has the option to use one or the other method, giving each Lodge
flexibility in managing its dues structure.
This Resolution is consistent with our Grand Lodge policy and general
purposes.

Report of the Committee on Finance:
While this Resolution will have no financial impact on this Grand
Lodge, the Finance Committee feels that it will have a very important impact on
our member lodges.
This Committee also strongly feels that the financial stability of our
Grand Lodge is heavily reliant upon the financial stability of our local lodges.
The Finance Committee has been struggling with the necessity of raising per
capita assessment each year and at the same time finding some way to permit
our local lodges a means of recovering that increased assessment without having
to constantly amend their by-laws in order to increase their dues.
This Resolution permits, not requires, but permits, such action by our
lodges.
While the Finance Committee does not normally make
recommendations but only reports on the financial impact of a particular
resolution, we feel so strong about the necessity of this Resolution, that the
Finance Committee recommends a “yes” vote on Resolution No. 02-01.
Resolution ADOPTED.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to change the name of the
Grand Lodge Temple Properties and Taxation Committee to the Masonic
Properties Committee.
Since some members of the general public misunderstand the use of the
word “Temple”, the proponents seek to change the word “Temple” and replace
it with the word “Masonic”.
This would require that the following Sections of the California
Masonic Code be amended to change the name of Temple Properties and
Taxation Committee to the Masonic Properties Committee: Sections 6020,
6050, 21010, 22010 and 22020. By changing the name of this Committee, its
purpose becomes more apparent.
This Resolution is consistent with the principles and general purposes
of this Grand Lodge.
Resolution ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 02-07
Requires Temple Associations for Real Property
The proponents of this Resolution seek to clarify the California
Masonic Code to require that Lodges use an incorporated Temple Association
for the purpose of purchasing, erecting or maintaining and holding title to real
estate for a temple for Lodge uses and purposes.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, ownership of real estate and buildings exposes the owners
and Lodge members to certain liability risks; and
WHEREAS, corporation status, if properly constructed, can shield
Lodges and their members from such liability risk, such as personal injury and
financial risk due to loan forfeiture; and

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

234

WHEREAS, Grand Lodge has over the last few years created Uniform
By-Laws to be used for the incorporation of Temple Associations, whether the
Temple is owned by one or more Lodges jointly; and
WHEREAS, the various sections of the CMC which deal with
ownership and maintenance of Lodge assets seem to permit management of
Lodge assets either by Trustees (see Sections 21010 and 21040) or by
incorporated Temple Associations (see Section 22010); and
WHEREAS, the Trustee arrangement can expose Lodges and their
members to the aforesaid risks, which can be minimized or eliminated by
corporation status.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED, that the California Masonic
Code be amended as follows:

§ 22010. ORGANIZATION AND OPERATION.
All Lodges shall use an incorporated Temple Association for the
purpose of purchasing, erecting or maintaining and holding title to real
estate for a temple for Lodge uses and purposes, and a Lodge or
Lodges may hold shares of stock in such corporation. Lodges shall
seek and receive the consent of the Grand Lodge Committee on
Temple Properties and Taxation before establishing such corporation,
and may reduce the annual per capita payment to Grand Lodge by the
amount of the California corporation filing fee(s) incurred in
establishing and maintaining such corporation. Lodges with such
existing corporations shall seek review by the Grand Lodge Committee
on Temple Properties and Taxation of its corporate status and the
extent to which that status shields Lodge members from liability, to the
extent permissible by California State law.
All Temple Associations shall be organized and shall operate as
follows:
A.

A Temple Association may be organized either as a non-profit
or as a general law corporation under the laws of the State in
which it owns or is to acquire real estate, land or a building;

B.

The exclusive purpose for which a Temple Association may
be organized is to hold title to property, collect income
therefrom and turn over the entire amount thereof, less
expenses, to its members or shareholders, all of which must be
organizations described in Section 501(c) of the Internal
Revenue Code of 1954. A Temple Association shall not
engage in any activities or exercise any powers that are not in
furtherance of this exclusive purpose. No part of the net

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

235

earnings of a Temple Association shall inure to the benefit of
any member or private shareholder, as defined for purposes of
Section 501(c)(7) of the Internal Revenue Code of 1954;
C.

Uniform Articles of Incorporation and By-Laws for Temple
Associations written by the Grand Lodge Committee on
Temple Properties, with the approval of the Grand Master,
shall be adopted for use by each Temple Association. Any
amendments to such Uniform Articles or By-laws by the
Grand Lodge Committee on Temple Properties, with the
approval of the Grand Master, also shall be adopted for use by
each Temple Association. Appropriate revisions to the
Uniform Articles and By-laws may be authorized by the
Committee on Temple Properties for Temple Associations
with more than one member or shareholder;

D.

A Lodge shall exercise its rights as a member or shareholder
of a Temple Association, including its right to elect or remove
directors, pursuant only to the authorization of a majority of
members of the Lodge present at a Stated Meeting of the
Lodge at which a quorum is present. Such authorization shall
not be contested if written instruments, executed by the
Master or Secretary of the Lodge, setting forth the
authorization are filed with the Secretary of the Temple
Association. The Lodge may exercise its rights through the
Master of the Lodge, or any person authorized by proxy or
power of attorney duly executed by him who is authorized to
execute any documents and to vote the Lodge’s membership
or shares at any members’ or shareholders’ meeting of the
Temple Association in accordance with the terms of any
action previously authorized by the Lodge;

E.

A Temple Association shall submit a written report to each
Lodge which is a member or shareholder of the Association
on August 1 of each year and on the following February 1.
The report shall be presented at the next Stated Meeting of the
Lodge. The August 1 report shall include a statement of
income and expenditures for the six month period ending on
the preceding June 30, together with a statement of the assets
and liabilities of the Association on the preceding June 30.
The February 1 report shall include a statement of income and
expenditures for the twelve month period ending on the
preceding December 31, together with a statement of the
assets and liabilities of the Association on the preceding
December 31. A copy of each such report shall be given to
the Inspector of the District to which such Lodge is assigned;

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

236

F.

Annually on or before the 15th day of the fifth month
following the end of its fiscal year, each Temple Association
shall submit to the Grand Secretary a full and correct report of
its transactions for that preceding fiscal year on a form as may
be provided. The report shall include a statement of assets,
liabilities and net worth as of the end of the 12 month period.
Temple Associations formed during the 12 month period shall
transmit a report from the date of incorporation;

G.

A Temple Association may not hold or administer funds for
any purpose not specifically set forth in this Code; and

H.

Temple Associations funds, not Lodge funds, must be used to
compensate employees.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
This Resolution would require that all Lodges use an incorporated
Temple Association for the purpose of purchasing, erecting, maintaining or
holding title to real estate for a Lodge Temple. This Resolution would also
reduce the annual per capita payment to Grand Lodge by the amount of the
California Corporation filing fees incurred in establishing and maintaining said
corporation. It would also require existing Temple Associations to seek review
of their corporate status by said Grand Lodge Committee.
The proposed new verbiage would be added to the existing California
Masonic Code Section 22010.
Presently, Sections 21010, 21040 and 21060 are permissive and use the
word “may” in reference to their provisions. The proposed amendment uses the
mandatory word “shall” to require that all Lodges use an incorporated Temple
Association for the purpose as stated.
By the use of the incorporated Temple Associations, Lodges and their
members may be shielded from liability for personal injuries and from financial
risks due to loan foreclosure. The Resolution would also clarify Sections
21010, 21040 and 21060 and make them applicable only to property other than
Lodge temples. This Resolution is distinguished from Grand Master Whitaker’s
Recommendation No. 1 which seeks to have all real estate of a Lodge held by a
corporation.
It has been the policy of the Grand Lodge for many years to require all
Lodges holding title to their Lodge Hall to become incorporated as a Temple
Association.
Although incorporation should not be considered a substitute for
adequate insurance, the proposed Resolution serves the best interests and
general purposes of our Grand Lodge.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

237

Report of the Committee on Finance:
Resolution No. 02-07 would permit a lodge to reduce the per capita
assessment paid to Grand Lodge by the amount of the initial filing fees for
incorporation and the annual filing fee. There are approximately 250 Hall
Associations currently with an annual filing fee of $10.00 each. The initial
filing fees for new Hall Associations could be as high as several thousand
dollars each.
Should this Resolution pass, the minimum annual cost to Grand Lodge
would be $2,500.00 plus the cost of any new associations.
Resolution ADOPTED.
