2003 RESOLUTIONS
Resolution No. 03-01
Allows Lodges And Masonic Organizations To Participate In Gambling
Activities Permitted Under California State Law
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow Lodges and Masonic
Organizations to participate in gambling activities which are permitted under
California state law.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Lodges and affiliated Masonic organizations need
effective fundraisers; and
WHEREAS, California statutory law and the California Masonic Code
have very similar, over lapping, but not entirely parallel provisions concerning
prohibited gambling, bingo, lotteries and raffles making it necessary to review
two bodies of law and to comply with the more restrictive; and
WHEREAS, the resulting confusion makes it more difficult for the
Grand Lodge to interpret and administer the pertinent law governing fund
raising and for Masons to comply with fund raising law than would be the case
if only one body of law governed fund raising; and
WHEREAS, California case law, attorney general opinions and
interpretations, and local district attorney guidance would be directly applicable
to interpret California Masonic Law if it adopted by reference the California
statutes pertaining to such activities.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 15090 of the
California Masonic Code be amended to read as follows:

§15090. GAMBLING.
Neither a Lodge, a Masonic Organization, nor a Mason shall, directly
or indirectly, give or attempt to give the aid or countenance of Masonry
to, or participate in, the conduct or preparation of any raffle, lottery or
other form of gambling, conducted in the name of, or directly or
indirectly for the monetary benefit of, any Lodge or of any Masonic
Organization which is prohibited by California state law. A Lodge,
Masonic Organization and a Mason may participate in any raffle,
lottery or other form of gambling to the extent permitted by California
state law. A Lodge, a Masonic Organization and a Mason may
participate in any other fund raising activity otherwise allowable under
this Code if not prohibited by California state law. A Lodge shall not

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

239

accept any of the proceeds or profits of any raffle, lottery or other
gambling enterprise.
Prizes may be awarded if not purchased from monies collected as
admission fees and if no separate charge is made for the chance to win.

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that Section 15095 of the California
Masonic Code which now reads as follows be repealed:

§15095. BINGO.
A Lodge or Masonic Organization may sponsor a bingo game, if the
sponsor observes the following rules:
A.

Attendance at the event is limited to the sponsor’s members,
their families and friends.

B.

There are no signs or other advertising visible to the general
public referring to the game.

C.

No donation, admission fee, meal purchase or other charge is
accepted or required for the opportunity to participate.

D.

Prizes of minimal value may be awarded if purchased by the
sponsor or donated.

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that Section 1060 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§1060. DUTIES.
The Grand Master shall:
A.

Preside in Grand Lodge at all Annual and Special
Communications. There shall be no appeal from his rulings as
presiding officer;

B.

Present, at each Annual Communication, a written message
setting forth:
1.
2.

All his official acts during the year;
The general condition of Masonry within the
Jurisdiction; and

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

240

3.

Recommending such legislation as he deems
necessary or expedient for the welfare of the Craft;

C.

Constitute the Lodge for which a charter is granted in
accordance with the ancient usages and regulations. He may
do so either in person or by a duly authorized representative;

D.

Cause to be personally served on any Mason appearing to be
engaged in the conduct or promotion of any raffle, lottery or
other form of gambling prohibited by this Code that is being
conducted, or is about to be conducted in the name of, or for
the benefit of any committee or other subordinate body of any
Lodge or Masonic Organization, a notice requiring that Mason
to immediately discontinue his participation.
If the Mason fails to discontinue his participation in such
enterprise, the Grand Master shall refer the matter to a Lodge
of this Jurisdiction for trial on the charge of unmasonic
conduct. The Junior Warden of the Lodge shall prefer the
charges against the Mason. Pending the trial, the Grand
Master may suspend the Mason from all the rights and
benefits of Masonry;

E.

Exercise general and careful supervision over the Craft, and
see that the Constitution and Ordinances of Grand Lodge are
strictly maintained, supported, and obeyed; and

F.

Discharge all necessary executive functions of Grand Lodge
when it is not in session.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to conform the California
Masonic Code with state law and local ordinances whereby Lodges and
Masonic Organizations would be allowed to participate in and conduct
gambling activities.
Sections 15090 and 15095 of the California Masonic Code prohibits Lodges
and Masonic Organizations from participating in any form of gambling that
otherwise may be permitted under California state law and local ordinances.
This Resolution would change the policy on gambling and permit Lodges to
sponsor different forms of gambling for the purpose of financial gain of a
Lodge.
Permitting Lodges and Masonic Organizations to conduct gambling is
not consistent with the policy and general purposes of this Grand Lodge.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

241

Resolution No. 03-02
Reduces Stated Meeting Quorum From Seven To Five
The proponents of this Resolution seek to reduce the quorum required for Lodge
Stated Meetings from seven to five members.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, most of the Lodges in this Jurisdiction are experiencing a
decline in membership; and
WHEREAS, approximately twenty percent of the Lodges in this
Jurisdiction have 100 members or less; and
WHEREAS, many of these smaller Lodges close or consolidate each
year; and
WHEREAS, it is a benefit to Masonry to keep these small Lodges
open; and
WHEREAS, many of these Lodges cannot conduct business for lack
of a quorum.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 16010 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§16010. CONDITIONS FOR CONDUCTING AND BUSINESS
TO BE TRANSACTED.
A Lodge shall not be opened, nor shall any work be done or business
transacted, except by, or in the presence of, and under the continual
direction of the Master or the ranking Warden or the Grand Master or
his designated representative. All business, except the examination of
candidates in the lectures of the Entered Apprentice and Fellow Craft
degrees, the conferring of the Entered Apprentice and Fellow Craft
degrees, and the consideration of relief of a worthy Brother, shall be
done in a Lodge of Master Masons. No Lodge shall transact any
business with less than seven five members present except the
conferring of degrees. The Tiler is present while at his post in the
Tiler’s room.
All business shall be transacted at a Stated Meeting, except the
following may also be conducted at a Special Meeting:
A.

Collections or appropriations for charity;

B.

Conferring degrees;

C.

Ceremonial observances;

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

242

D.

Electing a representative to the Grand Lodge;

E.

Balloting for commissioners to try charges of unmasonic
conduct; or

F.

After dispensation from the Grand Master:
1.

Balloting for and conferring the degrees upon an
applicant without reference to a committee, or

2.

Receiving and acting upon the application of a
rejected applicant within less than 12 months after the
date of his rejection.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of resolution No. 03-02 seek to reduce the quorum
required for a Lodge Stated meeting from seven to five.
They cite our declining membership as a primary reason that many
lodges cannot conduct business for lack of a quorum.
Research reveals that the real reason for the quorum of seven is from
our ritual. Entered Apprentice lodges consist of seven and until about 1843
lodges in this country conducted their stated meetings on the First Degree.
It has been the policy of the Grand Lodge of California since its
formation in 1850 to required Stated Meetings to be conducted in a lodge of
Master Masons (Third Degree). This reflects the practice which emerged after
the Baltimore Conference of 1843 when all Grand Lodges were urged to require
their lodges to conduct business on the Third Degree.
The theory of the day was that an imposter would have to memorize
three times as much ritual in order to gain admission into a Stated Meeting.
However, the ritual was not changed to reflect this innovation in Masonry and
we still keep the Entered Apprentice quorum of seven for our Stated Meetings,
thus, reflecting our roots in the Grand Lodges from which our first five lodges
came.
This resolution is a change that is not consistent with current Grand
Lodge policy.
Resolution CARRIED-OVER.
Resolution No. 03-03
Allows Entered Apprentices And Fellow Crafts To Withdraw And Modifies The
Procedure For Them To Transfer To Another Lodge
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow Entered Apprentices and

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

243

Fellow Crafts to withdraw from the fraternity and to modify the procedure for
Entered Apprentices or Fellow Crafts to transfer to another Lodge.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, Section 23100 of the California Masonic Code only
permits a Dimit to be issued to an Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft Mason
only if he is transferring to another Lodge; and
WHEREAS, as an Entered Apprentice or Fellow Craft Mason joined
the Fraternity of his own free will and accord, so he should be permitted to
withdraw from membership of his own free will and accord; and
WHEREAS, there is presently no provision in our law for an Entered
Apprentice or Fellow Craft Mason to withdraw from the Lodge with jurisdiction
over him.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 23100 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§23100.
WITHDRAWAL AND DIMITS FOR ENTERED
APPRENTICES AND FELLOW CRAFTS.
Entered Apprentices and Fellow Crafts, against whom no charges are
pending, may withdraw from their Lodge and may apply for
membership and advancement in another Lodge by obtaining a Waiver
of Jurisdiction and Dimit from their Lodge at any time. Such
withdrawal shall be obtained by a written request, over his signature, to
the Secretary of the Lodge that a Dimit be issued. At the next Stated
Meeting after the receipt of the request, the Master shall order the
Secretary to issue a Dimit immediately, in the form provided, as
evidence of the withdrawal.
Entered Apprentices and Fellow Crafts, against whom no charges are
pending, may apply for membership and advancement in another
Lodge by submitting an application for affiliation to the second Lodge.
Upon submission of the application, the second Lodge shall notify the
first Lodge that the application has been submitted and request a
Waiver of Jurisdiction to receive the application. The first Lodge shall
vote on the request for a Waiver of Jurisdiction, and if approved by a
majority vote of those in attendance at a Stated Meeting, the second
Lodge may receive the application and vote thereon in the same
manner as it would on applications for affiliation by Master Masons. If
elected, the second Lodge shall request a Dimit from the first Lodge (if
one has not already been issued), and a transfer of the Entered
Apprentice or the Fellow Craft from the first Lodge to the second
Lodge shall be made. The effective date of the transfer shall be the

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

244

date of the election in the second Lodge.

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that Section 13 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§13 DIMIT:
A document, bearing the seal of a Lodge and attested to by the
Secretary, terminating membership (if issued to a Master Mason) or
effecting a withdrawal (if issued to an Entered Apprentice or a Fellow
Craft.).

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The purpose of this resolution is to give an Entered Apprentice or
Fellowcraft the right to withdraw from his Lodge without prior conditions, just
as Master Masons can. Current Masonic law requires that before an Entered
Apprentice or Fellowcraft may withdraw from his Lodge, he must first obtain a
Waiver of Jurisdiction and Dimit. Passage of this Resolution will allow the
Entered Apprentice or Fellowcraft to withdraw at any time. This is in keeping
with our teachings that as a man may become a Mason only of his own free will
and accord, so too should he be permitted to withdraw from a Lodge of his own
free will and accord. The Resolution further provides that before the Entered
Apprentice or Fellowcraft can join another Lodge, it will be necessary for him
to obtain a Waiver of Jurisdiction and Dimit. Thus the rights of the first Lodge
as well as the rights of the Entered Apprentice or Fellowcraft will be protected.
This resolution is consistent with the policy and general purposes of
this Grand Lodge.
Resolution ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 03-04
Allows Lodge Members To Nominate Candidates For Membership
The proponents of this Resolution seek to allow Lodge members to nominate
candidates for membership.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

245

WHEREAS, during the period of July 1, 2001 to June 30, 2002, the
Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted Masons of California posted a membership
loss of 5, 315 Masons; and
WHEREAS, using this figure as an estimated average loss with 82,318
Masons in good standing on California rolls as of June 30, 2002, the logical
conclusion indicates that only 16 years remain for Masonry in California; and
WHEREAS, the Grand Master adopted a theme of “Freemasonry
takes good men and makes them better – tell a friend” and to support this theme
it is apparent that, if we are to survive as a viable fraternity, Freemasonry must
adopt a different attitude towards membership acquisition; and
WHEREAS, Rule 159 of the Constitutions of the Ancient Fraternity of
Free and Accepted under the United Grand Lodge of England, our mother
Grand Lodge, permits Lodge members to propose candidates for initiation; and
WHEREAS, there seems to be ample evidence that this method was
used in early American Lodges and is currently in use in Prince Hall Lodges;
and
WHEREAS, there does not seem to be a serious membership problem
under that system in England; and
WHEREAS, it would seem to be a propitious moment to re-adopt such
a method as a means of satisfying quality Lodge membership; and
WHEREAS, there does not appear to be any prohibitions in the
California Masonic Code for such a method.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that a new Section 26095
be added to the California Masonic Code to read as follows:

§25095. NOMINATING CANDIDATES FOR MEMBERSHIP.
At least two members of a Lodge may nominate a potential candidate
to receive the degrees in that Lodge. Nominations for the degrees shall
be on the form approved by the Grand Master. The nominators should
know the potential candidate well enough to attest to the Lodge
regarding his worthiness. Nomination of a potential candidate for
membership is a personal pledge of Masonic honor to the Lodge by the
nominators that the potential candidate possesses the positive
qualifications required. Nominations shall be balloted upon at any
Stated Meeting in the same manner as a ballot on an applicant for the
degrees, and if the ballot is unanimous, the Master shall thereupon refer
the matter to a membership committee composed of the nominators
and, if appropriate, another member of the Lodge well versed in
Freemasonry.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

246

The membership committee shall visit the potential candidate and
inform him of his nomination and answer appropriate questions he may
have regarding Freemasonry. The visit will conclude by leaving the
potential candidate an application for the degrees in the Lodge, signed
by the nominators as recommenders. The membership committee shall
advise the candidate that the final decision about membership in the
Lodge must be his and that the membership committee must not render
any influence on his decision. Unless the potential candidate has
already advised the membership committee or the Lodge that he either
wishes to apply for membership or that he has no wish to do so at that
time, the membership committee may contact the potential candidate
once more within ten days of the initial visit to inquire about his
decision.
If the potential candidate wishes to apply for membership, he shall
submit an application for the degrees in the manner provided elsewhere
in this Code, which application shall be received, investigated and
balloted upon as provided elsewhere in this Code.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
This Resolution would permit the submission of proposals for initiation
of new candidates by at least two members of the Lodge without the proposed
candidate's knowledge. The proposal would be voted upon at a Stated Meeting,
and if no objection by the members present, the Master would refer the matter to
a Membership Committee. The Committee would visit the candidate, inform
him of his nomination and leave a petition for membership. The Committee
would contact the candidate within 10 days to determine his decision. If an
application for membership is filed, the usual candidate application procedure of
investigation and voting would be required.
While the resolution seeks a method to increase membership, it is
unnecessary as the same results are achievable under the existing procedure
specified in the 1993 Grand Master's Decision No. 2 in the appendix of the
Masonic Code. The Decision specifies what a Mason may ask a non-Mason
concerning membership.
The enactment of this resolution would require a double ballot and a
double personal home visitation and is therefore not consistent with the current
policy of this Grand Lodge.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 03-05
Makes Affiliated Past Masters From Other Jurisdictions

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

247

Members Of Grand Lodge
The proponents of this Resolution seek to make affiliated Past Masters from
other jurisdictions members of the California Grand Lodge.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

248

WHEREAS, Section 25190 of the California Masonic Code states:
One who is a Past Master as defined herein but of a Lodge within the
jurisdiction of any other Grand Lodge, and who is a member in good standing in
a Lodge in this Jurisdiction, may take the honorary title of “Past Master,” but
shall not thereby become a member of this Grand Lodge; and
WHEREAS, Past Masters from other jurisdictions are as much a Past
Master as the rest of us; and
WHEREAS, they are some of the hardest working, dedicated members
in many of our Lodges; and
WHEREAS, over thirty states in the United States make Past Masters
from other jurisdictions members of their Grand Lodge.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 25190 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§25190. TITLE OF PAST MASTER.
The title of Past Master applies to one who:
A.

Has been regularly elected or named in a Charter;

B.

Has been installed as Master of a chartered Lodge in this
Jurisdiction;

C.

Has served until the installation of his successor; and

D.

Remains a member in good standing in a constituent Lodge in
this Jurisdiction.

Where a Master who has been regularly elected or named in a Charter
and installed as Master of a chartered Lodge dies, while serving as
Master, he may by resolution of his Lodge be designated as a Past
Master.
When two or more Lodges consolidate, the Masters and Past Masters
who do not become Master of the consolidated Lodge become Past
Masters of the Lodge thus formed.
One who is a Past Master as defined herein but of a Lodge within the
jurisdiction of any other Grand Lodge recognized by this Grand Lodge,
and who is a member in good standing in a Lodge in this Jurisdiction,
may take the honorary title of “Past Master,” but shall not thereby
become a member of this Grand Lodge shall have the title of Past
Master and shall be entitled to all of the rights and privileges of a Past
Master of this Jurisdiction, including membership in this Grand Lodge.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

249

If Grand Lodge charters a Lodge formed by a Grand Body recognized
by this Grand Lodge, it may by resolution provide that the title of Past
Master shall apply to the Past Masters of such Lodge, and entitle them
to the rights and privileges of Past Masters of this Jurisdiction.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Under Section 25190 of the California Masonic Code, "One who is a
Past Master as defined herein but of a Lodge within the Jurisdiction of any other
Grand Lodge, and who is a member in good standing in a lodge of this
jurisdiction may take the honorary title of 'Past Master' but shall not thereby
become a member of this Grand Lodge."
Currently the membership in the Grand Lodge of California is reserved
for Past Masters of California and sitting Masters, Senior Wardens and Junior
Wardens thereof who represent their Lodge members by virtue of their elected
office. Past Masters of other jurisdictions who are now affiliated with a
California Lodge have held no such office for that Lodge and have not earned
the right to represent its members. An almost identical Resolution was proposed
at our 2001 Annual Communication and the brethren of this jurisdiction did not
adopt the same.
It is in the best interests of our Grand Lodge that its delegates
legitimately represent the members of their lodges by the authority of their
elected offices.
This Resolution like the one we discussed in 2001 is not consistent
with our Grand Lodge policy and general purposes.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 03-06
Changes Ballot On Affiliations To A Simple Majority Voice Vote
The proponents of this Resolution seek to eliminate the unanimous ballot for
affiliation of a member and require a simple majority voice vote only.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, brothers in good standing of a Lodge recognized by the
California Grand Lodge have met the requirements of membership in our
fraternity.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 26160 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

250

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

§26160. MEMBERS PRESENT MUST VOTE.
Every member of a Lodge, when present, must vote upon a ballot for
the degrees or for affiliation.
The Master should not permit anyone to enter or retire from the Lodge
room after the announcement of the report of the Investigating
Committee or during a ballot except for sickness or other imperative
reason. The entrance of members at the conclusion of an initial ballot,
a single cube appearing, should not be allowed to interfere with the
taking of a second ballot to correct a possible mistake.
The “Lodge room” does not include the Tiler’s room or, for voting
purposes, the preparation room.

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that Section 26170 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§26170. BALLOTING PROCEDURE.
The vote on applicants for the degrees shall be taken by use of the
ballot box. Before a ballot is taken there may be discussion of the
qualifications of the applicant for the degrees and the propriety of
receiving him into the Lodge.
If, during balloting, an objection is made that the ballot box is not
properly prepared, the Master must, before announcing the result of the
ballot, examine it, and if not properly prepared, he must at once rectify
the error and have a proper ballot taken.

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that Section 26180 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§26180. METHOD OF BALLOTING.
When balloting upon two or more applicants, whether for the degrees
or affiliation, or both, the Master may at his discretion determine
whether a separate ballot shall be taken on each applicant or whether
the entire group shall be balloted upon collectively.
When the method of group balloting is employed the Master shall read
the names of the applicants with the essential information about each,
as supplied by the application, and as furnished by the Committee of
Investigation. A ballot shall then be taken in the usual manner and on

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

251

the applicants collectively, and if no cube appears, each applicant shall
be declared elected. If one or more cubes appear, the Master may
either direct an individual ballot or divide the names of the applicants
into groups. The Lodge shall then proceed to ballot in the usual
manner on each individual or on each group. The Master shall declare
each applicant in a group elected if no cube appears. This procedure
shall continue until an individual ballot has been taken in the usual
manner, if necessary, and all applicants have been balloted upon.

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that Section 26190 of the California
Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§26190. UNANIMOUS BALLOT.
A Lodge shall have but one ballot for the three degrees. It shall require
a unanimous vote of the members present to elect an applicant for the
degrees or for affiliation. Except for the three officers whose duty it is
to examine the ballot box, no one is entitled to know the number of
votes cast for or against an applicant. If all members of the Lodge have
voted and no negative vote appears, the ballot is unanimous regardless
of the fact that one or more members failed to deposit a ball at the time
he voted.

BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED that a new Section 26195 be added
to the California Masonic Code to read as follows:

§26195. VOTE ON APPLICANTS FOR AFFILIATION.
The vote on applicants for affiliation shall be taken by a voice vote
rather than a ballot. It shall require a simple majority voice vote of the
members present to elect an applicant for affiliation.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to change the method of
electing a brother for affiliation from a California Grand Lodge recognized
jurisdiction. By eliminating the use of the ballot box, a brother can not exercise
his right to a secret ballot per Section 26210 of the California Masonic Code.
The proposed Resolution also deletes the long-standing requirement that
election of affiliate members must be unanimous.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

252

This Resolution is not consistent with Grand Lodge policy and general
purposes.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 03-07
Changes Instruction Before The Obligations To “Your Full Name”
The proponents of this Resolution seek to insert the word “full” between the
words “your” and “name” in the sentence before the start of the obligations.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the members of many Lodges in this Jurisdiction think
that this Resolution will clarify the wording in all obligations, and avoid
repetition and possible distractions to the candidate and a new Master; and
WHEREAS, many other Jurisdictions use such wording as “pronounce
your full name,” “pronounce your name in full,” “pronounce your full given
name,” etc.; and
WHEREAS, there should be little cost in informing all Lodges in this
Jurisdiction of this addition to the ritual.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that the Ritual of this
Jurisdiction be amended to insert the word “full” between the words “your” and
“name” in the sentence before the start of the obligations, and that until the
California Cipher is reprinted the Grand Lecturer shall allow all Lodges in this
Jurisdiction to use “pronounce your full name” when administering all of the
obligations.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of Resolution 03-07 seek to insert the word "full"
between the words "your" and "name" in the sentence before the start of the
obligation.
Prior to the 1995 communication of Grand Lodge, Section: 26310 of
the California Masonic Code required that changes in ritual could only be made
to "restore" it to the ritual that existed from time immemorial. Thus, proposed
changes to our ritual were dealt with rather rapidly. These resolutions either
restored the ritual to its original form or they didn't and were not adopted.
Since the 1995 amendment to Section 26310, each suggested change in
our ritual has been considered on its merit and has reflected the need of the
times in which the change was proposed. Further consideration has been given
to its impact as a whole, i.e., will it clarify the ritual; will it be easy for the ritual

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

253

committee to communicate to the Inspectoral corps and then to the membership?
What is its impact on the candidate? Will it make the ritual easier to understand
or more meaningful?
This change is unnecessary as the Master states the candidate's full
name when he announces the reason the Lodge was convened. He should know
therefore whether the candidate has stated his name in full when requested to do
so. Historically, any omission by the candidate at the alter is corrected by the
Master at that time.
It is not the policy of this Grand Lodge to legislate changes in the ritual
that make no difference in its intent.
This resolution is not consistent with Grand Lodge policy or general
purposes.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 03-08
Adds An Instruction To The Newly Obligated Brother On His Return To The
Lodge In The Entered Apprentice And Fellow Craft Degrees
The proponents of this Resolution seek to add an instruction to the newly
obligated brother on his return to the Lodge in the Entered Apprentice and
Fellow Craft degrees.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the Masonic Ritual of this Grand Lodge presents a
beautiful learning experience for our candidates; and
WHEREAS, one of the foremost learning methods is repetition; and
WHEREAS, consistency of repetition aids learning; and
WHEREAS, informing the candidates that the Master is the ultimate
authority in the Lodge is an important lesson in Masonry; and
WHEREAS, Section 26310 of the California Masonic Code authorizes
Grand Lodge to make changes in the Ritual.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that that the Ritual of this
Jurisdiction be amended to insert the following language in the Senior Deacon’s
part at the start of the second section of each of the Entered Apprentice and
Fellow Craft degrees before closing the door: “You will wait a time with
patience until the Worshipful Master in the East is informed of your request and
his answer returned.”

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

254

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to amend the ritual to provide
that the Senior Deacon will give the same instructions to the candidate upon
leaving him at the door of the preparation room at the beginning of the second
section of a degree as given at the beginning of the first section.
Similar legislation 89-04 and 91-14 did not pass. Proponents of this
legislation advocate this change on the grounds that it would display "the
ultimate power of the Lodge Master." As the ultimate power of the master has
been displayed to the candidate in earlier ritual, there is no need for repetition.
It is not the policy of this Grand Lodge to legislate changes in the ritual
that make no difference in its intent.
This Resolution is not consistent with Grand Lodge policy and general
purposes.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

Resolution No. 03-09
Authorizes One Day Classes
The proponents of this Resolution seek to permit one day classes in California.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the decline of the membership of the Grand Lodge F & A
M of California has been rapid and severe in the past decade; and
WHEREAS, the average age of Master Masons in the Grand Lodge of
California exceeds 65 years; and
WHEREAS, the measures and remedies used to stem membership loss
have largely been unsuccessful; and
WHEREAS, several Masonic jurisdictions have had considerable
success with the one day conferrals of the degrees of Masonry.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that the California
Masonic Code be amended to add the following new Section 26285:

§26285. ONE DAY CONFERRAL OF DEGREES.
Any other provision of this Code or the Ritual of this Jurisdiction to the
contrary notwithstanding, the Grand Master may authorize the
conferral of one or more degrees of Freemasonry at one time and at one
or more locations within this Jurisdiction on any number of candidates

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

255

previously elected to receive the degrees in any Lodges of this
Jurisdiction and on any number of Entered Apprentices and Fellow
Crafts of this Jurisdiction. The proficiency requirements for those
initiated, passed and/or raised at such conferrals shall be determined by
the Grand Master.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Resolution No. 03-09 would permit the conferral of all three degrees of
Masonry on multiple candidates in a single day. It is believed by many that this
change in our ritual is necessary to avoid Masonry in California shortly
becoming a thing of the past. Our membership has been in decline for many
years. Since 1965, the number of California Masons has dropped by almost
75%. We have a highly aged membership that is dying off far more rapidly than
the pace at which we currently initiate new candidates. All projections indicate
this steep decline in membership will continue unabated.
Currently, 35 jurisdictions across the United States permit one-day
conferrals. Experience in these other jurisdictions has shown that the one-day
conferral is an excellent way to increase membership. Many states, such as
Ohio and New York, have reported astounding results. Last year in Ohio,
approximately 7750 men became Master Masons in one day in a statewide
conferral coordinated through the 10 Scottish Rite Valleys in that state.
Furthermore, most jurisdictions have reported that men receiving the one day
conferral, many of whom are civic leaders, have become active in their
respective Lodges, and the Lodges themselves have been rejuvenated by the
influx of new members.
This Resolution contains the same safeguards for screening members
as does the current California Masonic Code, thus assuring that only men of
good character will be considered for membership in our fraternity. Under this
Recommendation, the proposed candidate still must have recommenders and
references. It will still be the responsibility of the Lodge to receive and review
the application for degrees, investigate the candidate, and ballot on the petition.
Furthermore, it will be up to each Lodge to decide whether or not to participate
in a one-day conferral. Each Lodge will still be free to confer the degrees in the
traditional manner.
This Resolution represents a change in the current policy of this Grand
Lodge. However, it is consistent with the general purposes for which this Grand
Lodge has been established.
Resolution WITHDRAWN.

256

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Resolution No. 03-10
Establishes A Masonic Apron For 50 And 75 Year Members
The proponents of this Resolution seek to establish a Masonic Apron for 50 and
75 year members.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the Scottish Rite of California awards 50 year members a
distinctive blue cap so that all recognize that status; and
WHEREAS, it would be a great step forward if the California Blue
Lodge would also have apparel such as an apron to recognize a member who
has reached 50 or 75 years of membership; and
WHEREAS, while the California Golden Veterans Award Pin and
Certificate are very much appreciated, the certificate goes on the member’s wall
at home and the lapel pin is not well recognized by many members; and
WHEREAS, an apron worn at the Lodge would identify the 50 or 75
year members much better as it is a great honor; and
WHEREAS, several suggested designs are submitted with this
resolution for consideration which L.A. Fraternal Supply and others can
produce; and
WHEREAS, these aprons can be either machine or hand embroidered
with the cost based upon the design submitted; and
WHEREAS, there would be no cost to the Grand Lodge for this
resolution because each Masonic Lodge issuing this award would bear this
expense or the recipient member would purchase his own apron.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that the Grand Secretary
make available for purchase by or for eligible members (at no cost to Grand
Lodge) a distinctive apron for recipients of the Golden Veteran’s Award and a
distinctive apron for recipients of the Diamond Jubilee Award, that the exact
design of each such apron be approved by Grand Lodge from recommendations
of the Grand Secretary and that consideration be given to the designs submitted
with this resolution.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this Resolution seek to establish a Masonic Apron
for 50 and 75 year members.
The California Golden Veteran’s Award Pin and Diamond Jubilee Pin
distinguishes an individual Mason as a member of the Masonic fraternity for
fifty years, seventy-five years, or more, and it is a great honor to be recognized

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

257

by the award of the pin.
It is unnecessary to award another apron because the lambskin apron
presented to the Entered Apprentice is more honorable than any other award or
distinction that can be conferred upon a Mason at this or any future period.
This Resolution is not consistent with Grand Lodge policy.
Resolution ADOPTED.

a Masonic affiliated organization, that organization must be recognized by the

Grand Lodge of California. The constitution and by-laws of the Tall Cedars of

Resolution No. 03-12
Limits Admission To The Masonic Home For Children At Covina
To The Sons And Daughters Of Master Masons
The proponents of this Resolution seek to limit admission to the Masonic Home
for Children at Covina to the sons and daughters of Master Masons.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, the incumbent population of the Masonic fraternity in
California has significantly decreased, and accordingly, the resultant children of
a deceased population have also proportionately decreased; and
WHEREAS, the State of California is adequately prepared and suited
for the care of said non-Masonic children, and the voluntary contributions of the
members of the Masonic Fraternity are directly competing with state and federal
tax support of other children facilities; and
WHEREAS, the return on investments is not supporting our funded
endeavors as in previous years; and
WHEREAS, the membership’s average age is steadily increasing and
more members are on fixed incomes; and
WHEREAS, the vast majority of children at the Covina Home are
neither orphans nor Masonicly related; and
WHEREAS, we are bound by obligation to care for our aged, indigent
and infirm Master Masons, widows and orphans.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 8010 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:

§8010. PURPOSE.
The Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes of California shall
control, conduct and manage homes, hospitals and other facilities for
the care, maintenance and support of the aged, indigent or infirm
Master Masons, their wives, widows and children, and schools for the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

259

education of such children, and grant non-resident assistance instead of
admission to the Homes. In addition, the Board of Trustees may
consider for admission to the Masonic Homes any child without a
known blood relationship to a Master Mason on the same rules, terms
and conditions as a child with such a relationship is considered;
provided that preference for admission shall always be given to
children with a blood relationship to a Master Mason of a Lodge
subject to the jurisdiction of this Grand Lodge. Any child without a
known blood relationship to a Master Mason must be sponsored by a
Lodge subject to the jurisdiction of this Grand Lodge. Under
extraordinary circumstances, the Board of Trustees may consider for
admission to the Masonic Homes a mother of a Master Mason of a
Lodge subject to the jurisdiction of this Grand Lodge and may grant
her non-resident assistance instead of admission, provided that
preference for admission shall always be given to Master Masons, their
wives and widows.
The Trustees shall establish rules, terms and conditions, subject to the
approval by the Grand Master, governing admissions to the Masonic
Homes of deserving recipients of Masonic charity. They shall pay the
necessary burial expenses of residents of the Homes who die while
under the supervision of the Trustees, in an amount to be determined
by them.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this resolution seek to limit admission to the
Masonic Homes for Children at Covina to sons and daughters of Master
Masons.
When the Masonic Homes for Children was established, those children
admitted were orphans of master masons who had lost one or both of parents
and who needed a home because they had none.
More recently, throughout the passage of time and changing needs,
children admitted to our homes were almost always grandchildren, great
grandchildren, or distantly related children of a master mason. These children
were more likely the result of broken homes, children who had lived in foster
homes for years, or children who were victims of abuse and/or neglect.
In 1992 the Homes admission policy was modified to accept children
NOT masonically related, whose expenses were borne by a special trust
bequeathed to the homes for that specific purpose.
The Homes Board of Trustees studied this changed admission policy
and was pleased with the results. Therefore, in October 1997, at the request of
the Board of Trustees, Grand Master Allen B. Gresham, in his Recommendation

260

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

No. 2, proposed expanding eligibility to the Masonic Homes for Children to
include any child without a known blood relationship to a master mason, under
certain rules, terms and conditions. His Recommendation was adopted by the
members of this Grand Lodge.
The proponents of this Resolution express concern for the age of our
membership, the return on our investments, and our ability to continue our care
for our aged, indigent and infirm master masons, widows and orphans.
These are also concerns of our Board of Trustees, who have managed
the business of this outstanding charity in an exemplary way for over a hundred
years. Our Homes Board is charged with the control, conduct and management
of all the business of our Homes which include the power to restrict admissions
to either our Homes for Children or Adults should either physical or financial
need ever indicate.
This Resolution would diminish our present community involvement as
well as erode the authority and responsibility of our Board of Trustees to
properly manage the business of our Homes. It is therefore not consistent with
today's Grand Lodge charitable purposes.
Resolution NOT ADOPTED.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Board Meetings
Meetings of the Trustees were held on the first Wednesday of the
month, in the months of November, January, March, July, and September,
alternating between the Union City and Covina Homes. In addition, the Board
held its annual two day strategic planning retreat in June, 2003.
The committee chairmen of the Board of Trustees, Executive Directors,
Executive Vice President and Director of Program Development contribute in
the preparation of this Annual Report, as follows:
Admissions Committee
by Gary A. Yanes, Chairman
The Admission Review Program of the Board is a carefully developed
analysis and evaluation process that continues to be improved and refined to
meet the evolving needs of our Applicants, as well as the Homes Staff and the
Board.
The interview process and on site evaluation is done at the same time
the applicant visits one of the two facilities. A Staff report on this overnight
visit, together with the Medical Director’s comments on his/her review of the
applicant’s medical history and data, is then added to the application
information. The staff then develops and executive summary on the applicants
and presents that to the Admission Committee for review and approval.
Anytime the Staff recommends against admission the full application is given to
the Admissions Committee for detailed review and discussion during the
Admissions Committee meeting. Any recommendation against admission
requires the full Board confirmation.
Prior to the regular Admission Committee meeting, on the second
Wednesday of each month, the six Trustees, who form the Admission
Committee, each receive and carefully study the complete applications not
recommended for Masonic assistance and the executive summaries for
applications recommended for Masonic assistance. Each Trustee reviews and
evaluates the applications and accompanying data, and then discusses his
viewpoint during the Admissions Committee meeting. Home’s Staff and the
Administrators attend Admissions Committee meeting to answer committee
members’ questions and to provide additional information to the committee
members. The committee’s recommendations and comments are then presented
to the full Board for confirmation.
The number of applications handled by the Admission Committee for
couples, individuals and children continues to vary form month to month. From
July 1, 2002 to June 30, 2003, 49 residents have been admitted (44 on partial
assignment of assets and 5 on fee for Service). Union City currently has 250
residents (126 on full assignment of assets, 114 on partial assignment of assets,
and 10 on fee for service); Covina currently has 72 adult residents (28 on full

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

263

assignment of assets, 39 on partial assignment of assets and 3 on monthly fee).
During 2003 the Masonic Homes at Union City opened the Traditions unit to
care for our members with Alzheimer’s or Dementia. Currently this unit cares
for 13 residents.
The Masonic Outreach Services program (formerly known as NonResident Assistance program) continues to grow and is an effective program in
assisting our eligible Brethren and their families or widows. This program
provides the necessary support, financial, housing and/or care management
assistance to applicants who are in need of this type of assistance to remain in
their present community or residence. The Masonic Outreach Service program
is current serving the need of 76 clients and 2 on outplacement. This year the
committee has reviewed and approved 37 applications for this program.
Members of the staff monitor these individuals on a regular basis to ensure that
their needs are being met.
The Covina facility provides hospice care for its terminally ill
residents, thus giving its residents a palliative care option previously only
available through the placement in a skilled nursing setting. The Board recently
approved the development of a Dementia and Alzheimer’s unit at our Union
City facility to meet the ever-changing needs of our applicants.
The Child Care Program continues to develop. All of the new housing
for this program is complete and children are moving into the new homes. This
program is now serving the needs of 41. In 2003, the Child Care Program
received re-certification from Boys Town acknowledging the Behavior
Management Model program currently employed at the Covina Home.
I sincerely thank the other Trustee members of the Admission
Committee who labor long and hard over the applications. They are Charles
Alexander, William Boxell, Mark Gibson, Richard Hopper, and Ken Nagel. I
also extend my appreciation to the Administrators of the Covina and Union City
Homes and their staff, as well as the continued dedication of the Masonic
Assistance Staff to the applicants and Trustees.

Finance Committee
By Larry L. Adamson, Chairman
The role of the Finance Committee is to review and approve the
operating and capital budgets and monitor actual performance against this
budget. The Committee also reviews recommendations from the Investment
Committee of Grand Lodge regarding investment strategies for Homes funds as
well as recommendations for spending guidelines that establish parameters for
operating and capital expenditures.
The Finance Committee reviews the reports of the external auditors as
well as special internal audits that are conducted from time to time, working

264

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

with management to develop any appropriate action plans. In collaboration with
the Strategic Planning Committee, the Finance Committee oversees long term
financial planning as well as the review of financial analyses relating to new
construction and/or program development. The Committee advises the Board as
to the appropriate sources of funding for all proposed projects and programs.
The Masonic Homes of California end FY2003 with a favorable budget
variance of just over 2%, resulting from careful management of operating
expenses and higher revenue than budgeted. This represents relatively flat
operating expenses over the last three years, despite the fact that increases in
many health care expenses have exceeded general inflation by over two times.
In particular, we have experienced significant increases in the costs of worker’s
compensation and other insurance premiums, health care benefits for our
employees and pharmaceuticals.
For the last couple of years, operating and capital expenditures at the
Homes have been guided by spending parameters set at a percentage of a five
year rolling fund balances of the General and Endowment Fund. This approach
ensures operating expense will be somewhat buffered from significant year to
year market swings while protecting the integrity of the fund balances for future
generations of residents. Due to the stock market performance during the last
two years, we expect the recommended spending guidelines will be dramatically
reduced during the next few years. These financial projections are a major
focus of the Committee and the Board as we evaluate strategic options for
maintaining a sound financial position at the Homes.
The Committee reviewed and approved policies for the administration
and use of the various designated funds established for the purposes of granting
scholarship assistance and cultural enrichment for residents and graduates of the
children’s program in Covina. Several of these funds are administered in
conjunction with the California Masonic Foundation.
The Finance Committee reviewed regular reports from the Grand
Treasurer and approved the Grand Treasurer’s recommendation on the selection
of a new auditor, Pricewaterhouse Coopers in 2003. In addition to reviewing
the results of the audit in Fall, 2003, the committee audits at least one large
capital project each year. This year’s special audit is of the dining room
renovation project at Union City.
The Finance Committee worked with management on the development
of the FY2004 budget based on the direction that management attempt to
develop a “zero-based” approach to formulating operating expenses for the
coming year. The resulting budget actually came in below the spending
guidelines adopted by the Board. The favorable variances achieved in 2004 in
relation to the spending guideline will be placed in reserve for future special
projects and/or to cushion future years in which spending guidelines are
expected to decline due to the decreasing asset balances of the Homes.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

265

Working with the Grand Treasurer and management, the Committee
approved the establishment of a reserve for funding depreciation. Contributions
to this fund will be based on annual depreciation levels; in the future, this
reserve as opposed to the spending plan limits will be the source of funding for
capital projects.
Special projects reviewed by the Committee included a plan for dining
room and reception area renovations at Covina and energy conservation plans at
Union City. The Finance Committee, in conjunction with the Strategic
Planning, also recommended to the Board that further development of the Union
City golf course project be suspended due to difficulty in securing all land
necessary for the construction of an ideal course as well as projected spending
limitations over the next few years.

Medical Advisory Committee
by Deborah E. Stebbins, Executive Vice President
Charles H. Alexander, PGM, served as Chairman of the Medical Advisory
Committee during FY 2003.
The Committee oversees the evaluation and continuous improvement
of the quality of service provided to our patients, residents and MOS clients. In
addition, the Medical Advisory Committee monitors compliance with
appropriate licensure and accreditation programs and measures resident
satisfaction.
This year the Committee continued to monitor a number of quality of
care indicators, comparing them both to internal longitudinal trends and, where
available, external industry standards. These include:
Quarterly tracking of statistics relating to adult residential care
indicators: medication management, skin condition, weights, hydration, falls,
and resident participation in activities. The Committee received information
demonstrating that these indicators fell within appropriate ranges for
comparable long term care facilities and reviewed plans for on-going quality
improvement initiatives designed to optimize the health of our residents.
Monitoring of the rates of resident admissions to Skilled Nursing
Facilities and Acute hospitalizations. The goal of this evaluation is to ensure
that the Homes provides an environment in which residents can “age in place”,
remaining independent as long as possible.
Utilization of services such as clinic appointments, dental
ophthalmology and referrals to specialists were reviewed for appropriateness
and timeliness. The Committee received reports from the Medical Director, Dr.

Union City to monitor the utilization of services and referrals to outside

establishment of a quality of care checklist established by the Executive

volunteer activities of the children.

Scholarship Committee
by Deborah E. Stebbins, Executive Vice President
Charles H. Alexander, PGM, served as Chairman of the Scholarship Committee
during FY 2003.
The Scholarship Committee reviewed eight funds that have been
established at Grand Lodge to discern the original intent of the donors and the
degree to which application of the funds were limited. These included the
Student Aid Fund, Heuer, Resh, Hardy, Brigham, Brown, Schultz, and Clarke.
Three of the funds, Hardy, Schultz and Clarke were determined to be restricted
in a manner that they should be used as the first source of any scholarships
awarded to Covina residents by the Scholarship Committee of the Masonic
Homes. The balance of the funds is administered by the California Masonic
Foundation.
The Scholarship committee received four applications for scholarships
and living expenses from residents or former residents of the program:
Two applications were approved, with one student currently attending
California State University – Fresno and the other Mira Costa
Community College.
Two applications for scholarships were denied with the applicants
directed to career placement/counseling services at the expense of the
Homes due to their performance under previously approved
scholarship grants.

Strategic Planning Committee
by George Geanoulis, Chairman
The Strategic Planning Committee of the Masonic Homes of California
is charged with the responsibility of anticipating, researching and responding to
the emerging needs of our membership and aging trends in society. Our goal is
simple: to make the best and highest use of the resources entrusted to our care to
better serve the most vulnerable members of our fraternal family – the elderly
and children.
It is a unique time in our history in that our fraternity is aging as never
before – over 50% of us are over the age of 70. At the same time it is aging, our
overall membership is shrinking. As a result, we are in the middle of a demand
cycle that is disproportionate to our resources.
The budget for the Masonic Homes is based on spending guidelines
that respond and adjust to changing market conditions. Based on these spending
guidelines and market performance over the past few years, we are now facing a
projected funding shortfall. Because we have not diversified our funding

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

268

sources, 80% of our operations are currently funded by investment proceeds.
This is an unusually heavy reliance on investment income, even for a non-profit
organization, which makes us vulnerable to turbulent market conditions.
To further complicate the picture, the health care inflationary rate is
currently 2.5 times that of general inflation, which means that earnings from
investments cannot keep pace with increases in our health care costs. While
philanthropy is an increasingly important funding source, it is unrealistic to
expect that charitable dollars alone will cover the projected shortfall.
For the past few years, we have embarked on a comprehensive
operational review to improve the quality and cost effectiveness of our care. We
have successfully reduced our operating expenses while improving our resident
satisfaction. There is little opportunity to make up the funding deficit by further
reducing our costs without compromising our quality of care.
Given the great need for services among our membership, the Trustees
are exploring every possible alternative to service reduction, including
opportunities to increase our revenue and diversify our funding sources.
In the process of addressing these challenges and issues, the Strategic
Planning Committee has led many projects to successful completion, while
continuing to plan for the future, including:
o

Developing a communications plan to strengthen the connection
between the individual Mason and the Masonic Homes of California.
The Masonic Homes were founded by Masons to provide care on
behalf of all Masons in California. In this regard, it is important that
today’s membership understand the services that are being carried on in
their name. Our outreach efforts included:
o
o
o
o
o

o

Launching News from the Quarries for the leadership of the
fraternity
Improving our outreach to our elderly widows
Making multiple presentations across the state
Expanding our information and referral services and database
of community-based services
Sponsoring and coordinating a community health fair for the
Masonic residents of Paradise Park

Conducting extensive research into aging trends and how other nonprofit organizations are responding to similar social and financial
challenges, which included:
o
o

The aging of our fraternity and the projected need for services
The community-based support services available for seniors
and the likely ‘gaps’ in the safety net

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

o
o
o

269

Benchmarking our operations against other, similar non-profit
organizations
Sharing best practices with representatives from Masonic
Homes across the country
Scenario modeling of strategic and financial alternatives

In response to the diverse and complex needs of our membership, we
also developed a new Alzheimer’s/Dementia unit on our Union City campus and
expanded and diversified our community-based services for those constituents
who choose to age in their own home.
In addition, this committee helped develop and monitor the operating
budget, establish and monitor the goals and objectives for the year and
responded to operational and financial issues as they arose.
One of the highlights of this challenging year was the participation of
representatives from other Masonic Homes in the Board of Trustees’ annual
strategic retreat. The representatives all shared their organizations’ response to
similar challenges – past and present. Their input is helping shape our strategic
direction.
While these are challenging times, they are also exciting as we are
being called to creatively respond to today’s realities while remaining firmly
rooted in our historical mission to care for our most vulnerable members.
I would like to thank the members of this committee for their dedicated
and consistent efforts on behalf of our organization. As Trustees, we are ever
mindful of the awesome responsibility of the lives and charitable dollars
entrusted to our care. It is an honor and a privilege to serve as a member of the
Board of Trustees as we continue to strive to carry out our obligations in a
manner befitting Freemasonry.

President’s Report
By Allen B. Gresham, PGM
In 1873, fully 25 years before the first Masonic Home was opened in
DeCoto, California, Most Worshipful Leonidas Pratt, made a prophetic
proclamation:
“I would make Masonry as practical as is the age in which we live. I
would have you put in practice, day by day, the beautiful lessons and sublime
theories to which you listen in the Lodge-room…I would have you stretch forth
your mighty arm and lift and feed and heal and save.”
Over 100 years ago, our founders made a commitment to provide relief
to the most vulnerable members of our fraternal family – elderly brethren, their
wives, widows and orphans. Inspired by brotherly love, they created a Home

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

270

for our fraternal family that continues to support all Masons in fulfilling our
brotherly obligations.
This year, the Masonic Homes of California has served over 500
seniors and children on our two campuses and in their communities. We strive
to continually improve the services and programs we render. This is reflected
by the one year waiting list for admission we have at both Homes, by the ongoing improvement we see in our resident satisfaction surveys, by the
supportive services, both financial and informational, we provide to MOS
clients who continue to live in their own homes.
The significant accomplishments that have occurred at the Home are
outlined in more detail in the reports of the Board Committee chairman as well
as our management staff. We continually strive for excellence in all that we do
to ensure our services are delivered in a manner befitting our founding ideals.
The Board and, I hope, the majority of the Fraternity believes the dual
focus of the Masonic Homes on services to the elderly and children is
particularly fitting. Not only does this range of services echo our original
commitment to widows and orphans, but to this day, these groups represent the
most vulnerable members of society. In 1997 this Fraternity had the wisdom
and foresight to extend our admissions in the children’s program to nonMasonic children, a step that ensured the survival of the entire children’s
program at the time. Today, we continue to build better communities
throughout California by creating the conditions in which each child entrusted
to our care has the opportunity to develop to his/her highest potential.
Just as was suggested by Most Worshipful Pratt 130 years ago, the
programs and services of the Masonic Homes have evolved over the years in
response to the changing needs of our membership and society while our
commitment to providing Relief has remained unchanged and unwavering:
o

We have introduced new services, including the establishment of a 16
unit dementia/Alzheimer’s unit in Union City. In 1898 when that
facility was built, the average life expectancy in the US was 49 years.
Dementia was a largely unknown condition because people did not live
long enough to experience the devastation this debilitating illness
wreaks on its victims and their families. Today, we project over 4,000
fraternal constituents in California have a need for 24 hour a day
dementia care.

o

We have modernized our facilities at both campuses in recent years.
Our founders built magnificent and sound buildings to last decades.
However, the expectations of today’s resident for an environment that
promotes dignity, wellness and independence made dorm-like spaces in
the children’s program and the cramped apartments for our seniors that
we provided in the past outmoded.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

271

o

We have broadened the venue of where we provide services. As we
age, most all of us would probably prefer to remain in our own homes
and communities for as long as possible. Our Masonic Outreach
Services are a vital compliment to the Homes themselves as people
await admission or simply want to remain in their own homes
permanently with professional support provided by our outreach staff
and financial assistance from the Homes.

o

We have taken advantage of new financing sources over the years,
beginning with a decision to accept social security benefits of our
assignment of assets residents in 1930’s, moving to accepting private
health care insurance, and finally expanding our financial options for
admission to include partial assignment of assets and fee for service in
2001.

So you see the Board of the Masonic Homes on behalf of you, our
constituents, has a legacy of continuing to update our policies and strategies to
remain “relevant and as practical as the age in which we live.”
This year we have continued to study the needs of our constituents and
formulate strategies that will be responsive to their changing needs. What do
we see as the unique challenges facing our fraternity......
o

The fact that our membership is aging will come as a surprise to none
of us. Currently over 50% of the fraternity is over 70 and 35% over
80. What is an unprecedented challenge is that these age groups still
represent the years in which Masonic membership was at its peak.
Furthermore, over the next 10-20 years the needs of this age group will
increase as they experience the full impact of age-related conditions.

o

Many of our aging members planned carefully for their retirement
years only to find that they have outlived their carefully managed
resources. Others have found their asset bases deteriorate with the
market declines of the last two years.

o

Middle-aged California Masons, the next largest segment of the
Fraternity, face a different kind of challenge. As they struggle in this
economically challenging time to raise families, send children to
college, prepare for their own retirement, they find themselves acting
as caretakers and decision-makers for their aging parents. The 50 year
old Mason today has many different needs than the 50 year old Mason
in the mid century, whose parents did not live long enough to present
them with the challenges facing today’s “sandwich generation.”

o

Finally, the most vulnerable of our membership, namely the very
elderly Mason or widow are unfortunately most likely to be
disconnected from the fraternity. Many have not been able to attend
lodge for years – or their lodge has been consolidated, leaving them or

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

272

their widows to lose touch with the fraternity at the time their needs for
support are the greatest.
At the same time it is aging, the overall membership is also shrinking.
As a result, we are in the middle of demand cycle that is disproportionate to our
resources.
The unprecedented aging of the membership has resulted in an
unprecedented need for services. Meeting this level of demand would be
difficult in the best of economic circumstances; our current difficult economic
conditions have resulted in simultaneous pressures of increased demand and
reduced funding.
The Homes budget is based on spending guidelines that fund
operations while protecting our resources for future generations. These
spending guidelines respond to changing market conditions and are intended to
make the best and highest use of the resources entrusted to our care.
Based on our spending guidelines and the downturn we have sustained
in the market over the last two years, we will be faced with a serious funding
shortfall in the next few years. To act in the best long-term interests of our
organization, our options to respond to this funding shortfall include:
o

Considering reductions and/or closures in programs - an alternative we
find unappealing faced with an exponential increase in need, or

o

Identifying new sources of revenue or charitable funding

For the past few years, we have embarked on a comprehensive
operational review to improve the quality and cost effectiveness of our care. In
this regard we have been successful - we have reduced our operating expenses
while improving our resident satisfaction. There is no opportunity to make up
the funding deficit by further reducing our costs without compromising our
quality of care.
This is not to say we do not have sufficient resources to care for those
residents and clients to whom we have already committed lifetime support.
However, our shortfalls could affect our ability to care for additional brethren in
the future.
What is your Board of Trustees doing to respond to this challenge?
In the spirit of collaboration, we consulted six other Masonic Homes
organizations throughout the US. Three of those organizations, Connecticut,
Ohio and Pennsylvania, sent leaders to participate in our Board Strategic
Planning retreat last June. From these brethren, who not only share our fraternal
values and commitments, but also face similar challenges in their jurisdictions,
we learned a number of valuable lessons about funding sources of which we
have heretofore not taken advantage. The Board is continuing to explore these
options and will be developing more specific strategies for follow-up. This

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

273

strategic effort, which will be communicated to all levels of the Craft as it
evolves, will be focused on continuing to ensure that we sustain our mission to
serve all brethren in need.
In closing, I would like to suggest a few ways that each of you, as
individual Masons or through the work of your lodges, can help the work of the
Homes. Together we serve as ”partners” in upholding our fraternal obligations.

o

First, take time to learn about the work of the Homes. If you
or other members of your lodge have not visited one of our
campuses, call to arrange for a tour. Consider scheduling a
presentation about the Homes by a Board members or
members of our management staff at your Lodge.

o

Be cognizant of which of your lodge brethren or families are
currently in the Homes or are clients in our outreach program
and make an effort to see that they are contacted from time to
time.

o

Pick up the phone and call an elderly member of your own
lodge that you haven‘t seen in awhile. Only by working
together will we know that no one has been left behind.

o

Give as generously as you can to the Homes annual giving
program. This year the proceeds of annual giving has gone to
support the expansion of the MOS program. We continue to
rely on that important funding source.

o
I would like to take this opportunity to express my appreciation to each
Board member for his commitment to our organization and the cause of
Freemasonry. My special thanks to the members who took on the added
responsibility of chairing our important committees. Each gave considerable
time to ensure that their Committee’s charges were met. The hard work and
dedication of each and every Trustee has helped us achieve our goals and set a
sound course for the future.

274

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

In closing, the Masonic Homes of California is a great treasure built
upon the hard work, time and generous donations of our forefathers. It has been
said that the brethren who had the foresight a century ago to dream of a Masonic
Home, “built wiser than they knew.” With the unique perspective of 100 years
of service behind us, it is clear that their vision, and the charitable spirit
embodied in Freemasonry, have combined to build a lasting and inspiring
institution that every California Mason should be proud to call his own.
Respectfully submitted,
Allen B. Gresham, PGM, President
