REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE VICE PRESIDENT
September 2003

The management team of the Masonic Homes of California is
comprised of the Executive Director, Union City, Robert Fallon, the Executive
Director, Covina, John Howl, the Director of Program Development and
Masonic Outreach Services, Steffani Kizziar. Each of these positions report to
the Executive Vice President. In addition, the Homes contracts with the Grand
Lodge Corporate Services, who provide a variety of staff support services,
including Finance, Human Resources, Information Services, Membership
Services, and Philanthropic Services. A Director of Finance of Grand
Lodge/Homes, Mark Koenig joined us in March 2003 and Director of
Philanthropic Services, Doug Ismail joined us in November 2002. Both of these
individuals serve as members of the Homes executive team. A new Director of
Human Resources for the Grand Lodge/Homes is currently being recruited. The
introduction of these shared leadership roles between Grand Lodge and the
Homes has been an effective mechanism to coordinate our priorities while
controlling overhead expenses.
The management activities of the Masonic Homes are organized into
four areas, for which annual objectives, approved by the Board of Trustees,
form the basis of the yearly evaluation of the management team. These include:
I.

Strategic Performance and Program Development

Initiatives focused on responding to the evolving needs of the
membership and positioning our organization to remain competitive
and strong for the future.
Following the June, 2002 retreat, the President of the Board of
Trustees established an Alternative Retirement Living Task Force
(ARLTF), chaired by Vice President of the Board, Kenneth Nagel.
The task force members also included trustees Larry L. Adamson,
George Geanoulis, Mark N. Gibson, and Howard D. Kirkpatrick. Other
members of the task force included A.J. Weis, PGM (ex officio
consultant); Deborah E. Stebbins, Executive Vice President; Steffani
Kizziar, Director of Program Development; and David Hamilton,
Financial Consultant. The task force undertook a six month process of
studying industry trends in retirement services and services for the
elderly, demographic trends and preferences of the Masonic
membership, and a review of the strengths and weaknesses of the
existing organization, particularly the financial parameters of the
Masonic Homes. At the conclusion of their work, they presented a
report to the Board of Trustees summarizing their strategic assumptions
about the strategic future of the Homes and their recommendations.

276

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The Board of Trustees reviewed and discussed these
recommendations with great deliberation over the next six months,
including holding extended Board discussions in March 2003 and May
2003 with the guidance of a facilitator. Among the conclusions of the
Committee and, ultimately of the entire Board was a recommitment to
the underlying charitable mission of the Homes. Nevertheless, the
Board also recognized that we have been historically predominantly
reliant on the proceeds of General and Endowment Funds, resulting
from generous donations and bequests from the membership over 104
years. The fact that 80% of our operating and capital expenses at the
Homes are financed from investment earnings and in light of the
dramatic economic downturn of the stock market in 2001 through
2003, the Homes must evaluate alternative sources of revenue in order
to sustain and expand its mission to serve fraternal members in need.
Furthermore, the Board concluded that with a waiting list for admission
to our Homes in excess of at least one year and interest expressed by
many members of the Fraternity we are currently unable to serve that
the Homes should evaluate ways to broaden its services in the future.
These conclusions call for evaluating new and different strategies for
organizing and financing our services.
As a part of the June, 2003 retreat, the Board invited
representatives of Masonic Homes in three other jurisdictions:
Connecticut, Ohio, and Pennsylvania, to discuss their challenges and
strategic decisions. The shared imperatives and the strategic decisions
they conveyed helped solidify the Board’s commitment to evaluate new
and different strategies for organizing and financing our services to
allow us to serve our mission and be good stewards of our resources
for future generations. This culminated in strategic conclusions at the
June, 2003 which will be presented to the leaders and members of the
Fraternity in the coming year.
In addition to this formulation of our strategic direction, the
Board approved a new outreach program to Masonic widows, a group
that we know from research to be the most financially challenged
segment of the Fraternal family. Mailings have been sent to widows to
inform them of the resources available to them not only in the Homes
but also Masonic Outreach services.
In November, 2002, a 16-bed locked Alzheimer’s/Dementia
Unit, called “Traditions” was dedicated at Union City.
This unit,
which provides care to residents at the assisted living level, is an initial
step toward meeting the needs of the estimated over 4000 members of
the fraternity, wives and widows who need 24 hour institutional
dementia care. As life expectancy has increased and over 50% of those
over 85 years suffer some kind of mental impairment, dementia care,

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

277

either for the Fraternity is a form of distress that was unknown at the
time the Homes were originally established. Nevertheless, addressing
this new form of “distress” is an important part of our mission.
A final analysis of the golf course project at Union City was
conducted in 2002 with the conclusion that the project, while an
attractive adjunct to the Union City campus, was not financially
attractive enough to warrant diverting our resources from our core
business, namely, the provision of services to seniors and children at
the Homes and through outreach.
This year management participated in approximately 60
presentations to lodges and other fraternal groups to explain the
mission and structure of the Homes. We continue to attempt to
strengthen our communications with lodges in order to coordinate our
mutual obligation to serve the membership in need of fraternal support.
In addition, management collaborated with the Grand Master to
initiate the new newsletter, News from the Quarries, which along with
the California Freemason, also disseminates information about services
at the Homes and MOS.

II. QUALITY IMPROVEMENT
Initiatives focused on improving the quality of the services we provide
with a corresponding positive impact on the quality of life of our
residents and clients.
Management of the Homes strives to continually evaluate and
improve the quality of care and services provided to our residents and
clients. This is done based on many sources of input: formal resident
surveys, comparison with industry service standards, careful
monitoring of resident and patient health, and evaluation of the
cleanliness and attractiveness of our facilities.
This year we completed several intensive projects to enhance
the quality of our children’s program, which for the second year in a
row received accreditation by the Boy’s and Girl’s Town Program. We
have developed our own more rigorous standards for monitoring the
success our work with the children, including health indicators,
academic performance and community service and involvement by the
children. These indicators are regularly reviewed with the Board of
Trustees.
We have established stronger ties with the alumni of this
children’s program, which hold annual reunions at the Covina home.
Based on feedback from a recent survey of graduates of the program
within the past 10 years, we developed a post graduate support

278

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

program for young adults graduating from the children’s program to
provide them vocational counseling and enhance their successful
integration into the community. We plan to formalize our tracking of
alumni to measure the results of the post-graduate program.
The Masonic Outreach Program acts as increasingly important
adjunct to the residential support offered at the Homes. With the
existence of a significant waiting list for admissions, applicants in need
of financial assistance or referrals to community support are served by
the outreach program. In addition, Masonic Outreach offers a life-line
for many fraternal members who wish to remain in their own homes.
We now maintain a 100% certified outreach staff. This year a
computerized tracking system was put in place to allow us to monitor
the frequency and quality of staff contact with clients.
Resident satisfaction surveys provide us with feedback on
resident concerns and requests for improvement. This year we had an
increase in overall satisfaction of residents over the prior year’s survey.
Nevertheless there are always areas for improvement. In response to
resident responses, we upgraded our dining programs at both homes
under the leadership of a new Director of Food Service, John Marshall.
John, who is professionally trained and has served as executive chef
and/or food service director at a number of luxury hotels and clubs,
provides oversight for the food program at both facilities. The
feedback from residents regarding the new menu, recipes and special
touches has been extremely positive. In addition, the dining room was
dramatically renovated at Union City and similar renovations are
underway at Covina.
Improvement of the quality of our recreation programs was a
priority at both Homes. We have brought in more experienced
leadership into those areas. Union City is averaging 43% participation
on the part of our skilled nursing facility patients in community wide
activities.
An important aspect of quality of life in a retirement
community is the involvement and input of residents in determining
their surroundings. Wherever possible, we attempt to involve residents
in key decisions about life in the Homes. At Covina, this had been
done by an active resident council. This year a similar council of
seven members was established at Union City.
The Council
membership was determined by a vote of all residents from a slate of
candidates. The Council provides important input for the Executive
Director. In addition, at Union City a small committee of respected
Masons serves as “resident counselors” to which residents who are not
behaving in accordance with Masonic values can be referred. Such

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

279

interventions, done diplomatically, help to sustain harmony among our
residents.
While we do not offer skilled nursing services on site at
Covina, largely because of the smaller size of the adult program, we
want to ensure that residents of Covina who choose to be placed in offsite SNF’s rather than relocate to Union City receive the same high
caliber of care that they would at the Northern California campus. This
year a quality checklist was developed against which 10 local facilities
were evaluated during site visits by our Executive Director, Medical
Director and Director of Health Services. Three of those were selected
as preferred placement sites for future SNF admissions from Covina.
We continue to monitor our quality of care indicators for the
adult program, measuring health indices of the residents as well as
utilization of consultant visits, skilled nursing admissions and
hospitalizations to ensure that residents have their health care needs
met in a cost effective manner.

III. ORGANIZATION
Initiatives focused on strengthening, educating, and improving the
staff, management and executive teams and Board of Trustees to
increase job satisfaction and quality of services provided.
Fully updated Administrative Policy and Procedure Manuals
were completed at both Homes and for Masonic Outreach Services.
Wherever possible we have an objective of operating under standard
policies for the two Homes.
The Homes and Grand Lodge management teams have
collaborated on sharing staff resources wherever possible with the goal
of improved coordination between the organizations and cost
effectiveness. This year we have begun to share the important roles of
Director of Finance and Director of Human Resources. A complete
outside analysis of our Human Resources function was conducted this
year and goals have been set for improving the effectiveness of
services to our employees and managers.
Following the administration of a comprehensive middle
management training program in FY2002, we have increased manager
involvement and accountability for budget management and have
instituted a performance based incentive plan in which managers are
rewarded both for overall organizational and individual performance.
Each member of the Executive team has participated in state
and national association meetings for the long term care committee.

280

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Many members of the team serve on committees associated with these
organizations, whose goal is to improve the caliber of services
provided to the elderly in the United States.
Board members have also participated in several of these
meetings, including the Masonic Executives Association of North
America, held in 2003 in Virginia. The Board completed its second
annual self-evaluation, the results of which determined its goals for
education for the year. The Board sets aside some portion of each
meeting to hear an educational presentation.

IV. PERFORMANCE AND COST IMPROVEMENT
For the third consecutive year the Masonic Homes of
California has experienced a favorable variance in budgeted revenue
and expenses, causing an overall positive variance in operations of
over 2%. This has been the result of focused efforts to improve
operational effectiveness while maintaining and improving quality and
resident satisfaction. Our costs per resident day have remained stable
or decreased in this time period as our Homes operate at full capacity
and despite that fact that health care related costs are increasing at 2.5
times the rate of general inflation.
Nevertheless there are considerable future economic
challenges ahead for the Homes. The Board has approved operational
spending guidelines that place a cap on expenditures based upon a
percent of a five year rolling average balances of our general and
endowment funds. These investment earnings currently cover 80% of
our total operating expenditures. Naturally, with the downturn in
market performance over the last two years, this cap is expected to
decrease significantly over the next few years. The operational
improvements we have instituted, and our efforts to increase annual
philanthropic donations will help mitigate these decreases but they will
not be sufficient to eliminate our shortfall completely.
As outlined in the reports of President Gresham and the
Strategic Planning Committee, the Board and management are devoting
a major focus in the coming year to identify additional sources of
revenue to supplement our traditional investment earnings.
We are fully able to cover our financial commitments for
lifetime care to current residents and clients. By continuing to find
ways to be even better stewards of the resources entrusted to us we can
ensure we will continue to make similar commitments to future
generations of Masons.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

281

In closing, I would like to express my sincere appreciation to all of our
hardworking Trustees for their gifts of time and talent this year. This year their
time commitment was unusually demanding due to the significance of the
strategic issues facing the organization. I never cease to be impressed with the
untiring devotion to the Homes and our mission. It is an honor and a privilege
to work with them on behalf of service to our residents and clients and in
furtherance of that mission.
Respectfully submitted,
Deborah E. Stebbins, Executive Vice President

282

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR
MASONIC HOME, UNION CITY
2002 - 2003
It is a pleasure to once again submit my annual communication report
to the Grand Lodge of California. We continue to revise the staffing structure at
the Union City Home. Over the past year we reduce staffing ratios to be in line
with industries standards while maintaining quality services. This year we were
able to reduce management positions to further streamline operations and to be
fiscally responsible. During this present economic slump along with a reduction
in the Masonic Home investments, the need to maintain a watch full eye on the
Union City operating budget is essential.
One of this year’s highlights was the dedication of the original
Administration building and the first building of the Union City Home, in honor
of Ralph H. Head, Past Grand Treasurer and editor of the “Freemason”
magazine for over 30 years. The Head Administration Building was dedicated
in September 2002 by our Most Worshipful, Grand Master William Holsinger.
Since the beginning of my employment at the Union City Home, I have
heard from residents that they were not included in the decisions that provide
direction for the management of the Home. This past year we have empowered
the community through the establishment of the first democratically elected
Resident Council. This Council of seven residents was elected by the residents
and will be the collective voice for the community. The purpose of the Resident
Council is to represent the community and to advise the Executive Director.
The Resident Council has become an important and valuable voice, providing a
majority perspective on issues from the community. Empowerment is
something new for the residents because they have to think like a community
and of each other rather than thinking as individuals.
We continue to be involved with the local community. This past
Christmas season, our annual Christmas Tree Lighting was also supported by
the City of Union City. The 110’ Norfolk Island Pine Tree was proclaimed the
official Union City Christmas Tree. Over 1,000 residents of Union City
attended the tree lighting event, which included many local schools. The
residents of the home had a great time and loved to see all the children visiting
the home. One of our oldest residents, Mr. Bill Diaper at age 102, was the
honoree who turned on the 4,000 Christmas tree lights. We hope to make this
an annual event and are looking forward to this year’s Christmas.
We have completed a major renovation of the Dining Room in the
Sedam Building. This upgrade along with bringing management in-house and
eliminating the outside food contract service, has dramatically improved our
food service program. The residents have strongly approved these changes,
believe the food is excellent and welcome the more professional service.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

283

This past year we have also opened the first Alzheimer/Dementia
Special Care Unit for residents who reside in either Masonic Home. This unit
has also been available for those Masons, their wives and widows through out
of state who might benefit from this environment. With the addition of this new
Special Care Unit we can now serve residents in all levels of care with out
rejecting potential residents due to dementia.
As we look towards the future, we will continue to provide high quality
care and services to our residents while being fiscally responsible. We will
continue to explore renovations in both the Lorber and Sedam Building.
Upgrading carpeting, painting, wallpaper, lighting and the common areas is
essential in creating a quality home for our residents.
Notable residents achievements this year included the birthdays of
Adolph Lehman
Eleanor Webb
William Diaper
Edna Creek
Peggy Franklin
Viola Albertson
Ione Thompson-Forsyth
Ludwig (Ernie) Kraft

Robert M. Fallon, Administrator

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR
MASONIC HOME, COVINA
2002 - 2003
Greetings!
On behalf of the residents and staff of the Masonic Homes at Covina, it
is my pleasure to present our annual report. As you read our report, we hope
that you will sense our strong commitment to our enduring mission and the
Masonic Values of brotherly love, relief, and truth. We would also like to
express our sincere appreciation for the dedicated, visionary leadership of our
Board of Trustees, and the generous support of our Masonic Lodges.

Senior Services
•

Over the past year we served an average senior population of 72
residents. During this period of time we had 12 admissions, 2
withdrawals, 1 transfer to Union City and 11 deaths. Also during this
period of time our waiting list has grown to 29 households representing
an average waiting time of approximately 1 year for admission. Should
a prospective resident need more immediate assistance; our Masonic
Outreach Programs will provide them with resources and support.

•

Our senior model of care continues to offer a full continuum of
services and the opportunity for a resident to “age in place”. Through
an interdisciplinary care planning approach, each resident’s needs and
services plan is personalized to meet their changing needs. This past
year we restructured our nursing department to include a nursing care
coordinator that manages the care planning process. Our residents
expressed a high level of satisfaction with this change.

•

Also as part of our continuum of services, we carefully evaluated the
quality of skilled nursing facilities in our community. After a thorough
evaluation of quality indicators and on-site reviews, we developed a
special network of three high quality, mission driven, skilled nursing
facilities to serve our residents needs. Should a resident prefers to
transfer to our Union City skilled nursing facility, we honor that
request and facilitate that process.

•

In an effort to improve HMO benefits and the outside care delivery
systems available to our residents, we evaluated the various Senior
HMO programs and services in our community. As a result of our due
diligence we introduced our residents to an additional HMO option and
we identified an experienced geriatrician to serve our residents needs.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

294
•

In the spring of 2003 we conducted our second annual resident
satisfaction survey. This professional survey focuses on the residents
general feelings about our community and the quality of services we
provide. The results of our survey showed increased resident
satisfaction in nearly every area of service. The most positive
significant changes in satisfaction occurred in nursing care,
maintenance, security and food service.

Children’s Services
•

Over the past year we served an average population of 45 children
representing 23 girls and 22 boys. During this period of time we had
11 admissions, 11 withdrawals and 2 graduates. Nearly 50% of our
population has an indirect Masonic affiliation through a family
member, and all children are sponsored by a Masonic Lodge. The
average length of stay is approximately 3.5 years.

•

We continue to offer a family model of care that is certified through
Girls and Boys Town. Our model of care has a behavioral focus,
which emphasizes and teaches social, academic, and independent living
skills. Given the psychological profile of each child, which includes
abuse, neglect and abandonment, we also provide routine therapeutic
and psychiatric services through outside professionals. This next year
we will conduct a thorough analysis of our model of care to assure best
practices and full integration of services. In addition we will assess our
programs and services that focus on parenting and family reunification.

•

One of our strategic initiatives this past year was evaluating and
updating our post graduate programs to serve a more diverse
population of children. Through this effort we have expanded our
academic scholarship programs to include trade schools and
apprenticeship programs. In addition we offer career counseling and
job placement services to eligible graduates. We hope this initiative
will further assist our young adults to become stable and productive
members of society.

Other Strategic Initiatives
•

During the past fiscal year we initiated a number of important capital
projects for our campus. To name a few, we redecorated the corridors
of the resident apartment buildings, completed a major erosion control
and landscape project for Rainbow Park, and launched a major
renovation of our Senior Community Center Building. The later
project includes a variety of decorating and design improvements

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

295

including, a new reception area, fitness center, information center,
billiards and game room, new arts and crafts space, beauty and
barbershop. Our project also includes a two story hydraulic elevator
and handicapped accessible doors at various entrances. It should be
completed in the fall of 2003.
•

Given the unique service we offer to seniors and children on a single
campus, we have strategically planned a number of intergenerational
activities and volunteer opportunities. From mentoring programs to
adopt a grandparent, our children and seniors are building enjoyable
and rewarding relationships.

In closing let me say that it is an honor and privilege for us to be a part
of the Grand Lodge family. We pledge to you our commitment to fulfill our
mission providing quality care to California Master Masons, their wives,
widows and mothers as well as to children with and without Masonic
affiliations, with dignity and compassion, within an atmosphere, based on
Masonic tenets, that fulfill the physical, social, spiritual and nurturing needs of
our extended community.
Respectfully submitted
John E. Howl, Executive Director

REPORT OF THE TRUSTEES OF THE
CALIFORNIA MASONIC FOUNDATION
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Most Worshipful, Grand Lodge Officers, Past Grand Masters, Past
Grand Officers, Distinguished Visitors, Members of the Grand Lodge and my
Brethren.
It is with great pride that I am able to update you on the activities of the
Board of Trustees of the California Masonic Foundation. This is my sixth year
as President, and I extend my thanks to each of the Board members for their
confidence in me, for their dedicated service to our Fraternity and to each of you
for your continued support.
The Board meets quarterly, in the Covina Home or in the Grand Lodge
office in order to fulfill the mission of the Foundation – to support the youth of
our country as they strive to become productive citizens.
My Brethren, because you believe in this great Fraternity and have
supported the activities of the California Masonic Foundation, we have made it
possible for teachers to do the job for which they were trained. WE have given
them the tools to identify students at risk and the skills to provide the necessary
interventions for successful learning experiences. This year many more students
were referred to a student assistance team trained in Covina through the MSAP
Program. Many of those referred received some type of intervention. During
the past eight years of our program we now have trained 3,387 educators in 885
different schools or districts, and they have received 31,407 referrals for
assistance. Special help was provided to more than 16,494 students. We thank
you for your generous support of our program.
The Foundation has made the following expenditures this year:
1.

$10,000.00 was given to assist the National Masonic Foundation
in the expansion of the Masonic Student Assistance Program
throughout the United States.

2.

$2,500.00 was presented to each of the four youth groups for use
in their leadership training which they could not provide without
our support.

3.

Certificates of Recognition and $1,000.00 checks to each of the
five “California Teachers of the Year” to be used in their
respective school districts as they wish and Certificates of
Recognition and $500.00 checks to each of the two alternates.
These presentations were made in the teacher’s own community
thereby affording us the best public relations program possible.
Each one was awarded by a member of the Foundation Board of

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

300

Trustees along with the Grand Master or Deputy Grand Master
with the intent that awareness of our Fraternity’s involvement in
public schools through the Masonic Student Assistance Program
and our scholarship program could be made to the local
community leaders and the press.
4.

As you are all aware, the cost of a college or university education
continues to increase and many more students would not be able to
attend without financial assistance. In spite of the severe loss in
the financial market I am pleased to report your Foundation
presented eight C.E. Towne Scholarships of $12,000.00 this year.
All renewals were provided those students who were eligible. The
major expenditures in this area were to bring all named
scholarships up to their original levels. Therefore, limited new
scholarships could be awarded.

5.

The MSAP Program continues to be fully subscribed again this
year. The number of classes for the 2002-2003 school year was
four with the number of booster classes being 2. This has been
made possible by the generosity of our Masonic Homes of
California Board of Trustees who have agreed to allow us to use
the Covina Home for our training without cost. The Board of
Trustees of the California Masonic Foundation would again like to
thank Most Worshipful Allen Gresham, PGM and his Board for
their continued support and express our sincere thanks to the staffs
of each of our Homes for their untiring efforts to make our
program run smoothly. I invite you to visit our display on “EX”
Floor and read some of the responses from the school teams. It
will help you realize the value of your contributions to insure the
success of the educational system of California.

6.

The greatest public relations outreach of Masons in California
continues to be the Child ID Program. This year the Foundation
expanded its support of “KIDS” or Child ID Program. Be sure to
see the program in operation on “EX” Floor. This year money has
been allocated to upgrade the software needed with the latest
versions of Windows. The current software is not compatible with
the new formats.

It was with great sadness that it became necessary to lose our full-time
Administrative Assistant, Judy Liang, due to the decrease in number of MSAP
classes because of the financial condition of our investments. She will be sorely
missed and we express our sincere thanks and appreciation for her devotion to
our Foundation. We wish her much success and happiness for the future.
My Brethren, the future of our Country, the freedom of future
generations, the legacy we hope to leave this world are expressed in the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

301

Foundation’s motto:
“An Investment in our Country’s Future Through Education.”
I urge each of you to respond as generously as possible to the new
united Masonic Philanthropy Program. With your help we will make a
difference.
Respectfully submitted,
Donald A. Nielsen, President
Frank Loui, Vice President
Wayne L. Butterbaugh
Donald C. Gross
Myron Tisdel

Howard D. Kirkpatrick, DGM

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF TRUSTEES
OF THE CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
As before, this annual report of the Board of Trustee of the California
Masonic Memorial Temple (CMMT) is presented as an encapsulated and very
general report of the activities relative to the maintenance and preservation of
the beautiful edifice which houses our Grand Lodge and all the staff functions
for running that corporation. Those wanting more detailed information may
retrieve them as they are recorded in the quarterly minutes of the CMMT Board
of Trustees, through the Grand Secretary.
While this edifice is generally in good condition, it becomes necessary
to replace equipment that has outlived its life cycle. These include the boilers,
which have recently been replaced; dimmer lights to enhance the availability of
the auditorium for rental; and other equipment necessary to provide adequate
and more up-to-date facility.
After the September 11, 2001 incidents in New York, Washington,
D.C. and Pennsylvania, it was determined that the building a complete security
system to provide adequate safety for those working and/or using the facility.
That project was completed at a cost of approximately $137,000.
Without the Nob Hill Masonic Management Corporation and their
Board, many of these projects would not have been completed. They have
provided the CMMT Board of Trustees a Five Year Capital Spending Program
that will assist the Nob Hill Masonic Management Corporation to facilitate
renting the facility and providing further revenue.

302

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

This year a new contract was negotiated with the company that
manages the parking structure. The City and County of San Francisco has
implemented a new requirement that it will be necessary to install revenue
control equipment in the parking garage that the City/County of San Francisco
approves. The CMMT Board has approved that project along providing a like
piece of equipment on P5 Level thus providing an entrance/exit on Pine Street
for easier ingress and egress. Improvement work on that entrance will begin
soon.
Again to enhance revenue, the CMMT Board has authorized the Staff
of Nob Hill to provide advertising space in the garage. The advertising will be
of quality commercial enterprises along with one that will provide an individual
the opportunity to learn more about Freemasonry.
Major concerns and discussions have been held by the CMMT Board
as it relates to the financing necessary to provide adequate funds for the
continual upkeep of the building. After months of discussion, the Board
authorized a study to be made by the Pine Street Task Force to determine the
future of the apartments owned by the CMMT on Pine Street adjacent to the
Masonic Temple. After a great deal of discussion, the CMMT Board, with the
approval of the Grand Lodge Executive Committee, approved the sale of the
apartments to provide the necessary funding for the future up keep of the
California Masonic Memorial Temple and that the resultant funds would be
placed in a restrict fund and the interest from this fund would be used to pay for
the maintenance and upkeep of the present building. It was also required that a
height limitation be placed on the apartment property to provide light for the
Mosaic Window seen in the lobby of the Memorial Temple. The sale of the
property has been consummated as of this date. The Craft should note that as
our building ages, it will be necessary to provide an increased level of
maintenance so that the edifice remains useable.
Our California Grand Lodge also provides oversight, through the
CMMT Board of Trustees, for the jurisdiction’s historical sites, buildings, and
cemeteries. We are presently working with the City of Los Angeles to renovate
the Old Masonic Hall at the Plaza de Los Angeles. The Board will develop a
business plan to attempt to redevelop this hall as the City of Los Angeles is
unable to provide funding to complete the project. This plan will also include
an operation plan once the building has been restored and opened. The CMMT
Board has authorized the repair and paving of the driveway at the Columbia
Cemetery. Worshipful Ron Broaddus is the Chairman of this very important
committee.
The Institute for Masonic Studies is also the responsibility of the
CMMT Board and is proud of the work for the last several years of the
Institute’s sponsoring of the California Masonic Symposium with this year’s
program being held at UCLA with PGM R. Stephen Doan as the Moderator.
The attendance goal for the Symposium was met and the attendees were pleased

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

303

with the discussions on Freemasonry throughout the world.
The CMMT Board has several major projects that they will be
discussing in the future including the negotiating of a new contract with the Nob
Hill Masonic Management Corporation; space utilization for the Library and
Museum; and a more aggressive program to rent out space in the auditorium. It
is imperative that the facility remains one of the prime locations for
conventions, corporate meetings, and entertainment.
We are sorry that this year that Worshipful Brother Lloyd Clayton had
to resign as President of the CMMT Board of Trustees due to health reasons.
Worshipful Brother Lloyd has been a tireless worker to improve this facility.
Also, Chris Carpenter, the Executive Vice-President and General Manager, was
selected by the City of San Mateo to provide leadership at their convention
facility.
The CMMT Board of Trustees would like to thank our Grand
Treasurer, Warren Blomseth, PGM for his outstanding overseeing of our longterm finances. His ability to provide the financial acumen is outstanding. Also,
we would like to thank Grand Secretary John L. Cooper III and Mark Koenig
for their outstanding staff work, which provides us the necessary information to
make good decisions.
I would be remiss if I didn’t thank the Board Members themselves for
giving unselfishly of their time and talent to provide the outstanding leadership
to the Board. Each member is a caring Mason who provides the input necessary
to make those wise business decisions that provide this jurisdiction with an
outstanding facility.
Respectfully submitted,
Frederick L. Sorsabal, Junior Grand Warden, Acting President
Robert Winston, Vice President

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF DIRECTORS OF
NOB HILL MASONIC MANAGEMENT, INC.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
The Board of Directors of Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc. is
pleased to make its eleventh annual report to Grand Lodge on its operations of

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

304

the California Masonic Memorial Center. Nob Hill was established by the
Grand Lodge to lease the Center’s auditorium, parking garage, exhibit hall,
conference rooms, and various rental properties from the California Masonic
Memorial Temple (CMMT), the owner of this Center, and operate them for
profit.
This report is for the fiscal year July 1, 2002 through June 30, 2003,
and will include limited financial highlights for the year just completed. Full,
detailed, and audited Financial Statements are included in the Appendices of
these Proceedings, and you are encouraged to review these reports.
During this past year, the economy made a slight recovery and we were
able to reverse the trend of the previous year. Even so, we still sustained a loss
of $56,000 in Net Retained Income after taxes, dividend, investment market
value adjustments, and depreciation. In view of our optimism for the coming
years, we honored the commitment we made last October and paid Grand Lodge
a dividend of $70,000 prior to June 30, 2003. While our revenues again did not
meet our expectations, they were only 7.5% below budgeted expected revenues,
and we were still able to further reduce our operating expenses while continuing
our high standards of maintenance.
We think it would be instructive, as was done in last year’s report by
MW Warren Blomseth, to give you a percentage breakdown of where Nob
Hill’s revenue goes. Nob Hill operates much as your temple manager operates
in your home Lodge. He is an employee of your Temple Board, just as Nob Hill
is a lessee of the California Masonic Memorial Temple (CMMT). Here are
some of the statistics from this past year’s operation:
•

Rent, taxes, insurance, outside audit fees, and corporate services
were $804,000 (Approx. 31% of all expenses)

•

Staffing, including related benefits were $656,000 (Approx. 25%
of all expenses)

•

Utilities: Electric, Gas, & Garbage were $186,528 (Approx. 7% of
all expenses)

•

Dividend was paid at $70,000 (Approx. 3% of all expenses)

•

Garage operations were $407,000 (Approx l6% of all expenses)

Note that these five categories account for 82% of our expenses, and
are more or less fixed in nature. This means that our discretionary expenditures
must be carefully managed on a daily, weekly, and monthly basis. These
expenses are all carefully budgeted against anticipated revenues, and we are
engaged in a continuing marketing and revenue-generating effort to provide the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

funds necessary to bring needed improvements to the Center.

305

306

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Now we report on the projects undertaken during the past year. The
Security System which was reported on last year, is now complete. We have
implemented card control access to the Exhibition Floor, the Second Floor, the
Third Floor (Grand Lodge offices), and the Pine Street entrance. In addition,
there is a full-time Security Guard at a desk just inside the main entrance to the
First Floor lobby. The card system will be deactivated during the daytime
sessions of the Annual Communication.
Our garage management contractor (AMPCO) had been in place since
1993, and while they were performing in an adequate manner, we deemed it
prudent to re-compete the contract after such a long period. Accordingly, a
Request for Proposals was prepared and issued to a number of parking
management companies considered qualified for the job. We received three
bids, and after review by an evaluation committee, the decision was made to
remain with AMPCO.
A project to implement parking revenue control at the Pine Street
entrance was initiated. This will also include replacement of the vehicle ramp at
that entrance. This project should be completed early this year, and will allow
revenue control at Pine Street as well as California Street. This will greatly
improve garage egress during high attendance events.
A decision was made by the California Masonic Memorial Temple
(CMMT) to sell the Pine Street properties. This is being reported on separately
by CMMT. This sale will, of course, affect our revenues as well as our
expenses, and an appropriate arrangement will be negotiated with CMMT to
accommodate these changes.
With regard to staffing, we regret to report that Chris Carpenter, Executive Vice
President and General Manager has resigned to accept another position.. Chris
has been a valuable member of the team for the past three years, and he will be
sorely missed. Terry Hobson, Assistant General Manager, has been appointed
interim General Manager until the issue of a permanent replacement for Chris is
resolved. Also, during this past year, we hired Jody Brandes as Senior
Coordinator of Booking and Events. Jody will be a valuable addition to the
staff and we look forward to her contribution to the marketing effort. The
remainder of the staff continues to contribute to the fullest: the engineering staff
keeps the building functioning at peak efficiency, the custodial staff keeps it
looking its best, our event technical director operates the auditorium to its
maxi..mum capability, and our administrator helps pull it all together. The
Board extends its thanks and appreciation to them all for a job well done.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

307

Lastly, the Officers and Directors of Nob Hill Masonic Management,
Inc., thank the Grand Master, M. William Holsinger, Jr., for the opportunity to
be of service to this Grand Lodge. We also wish to acknowledge the Junior
Grand Warden, Frederick L. Sorsabal, the Grand Treasurer, Warren J.
Blomseth, and the Grand Secretary, John L. Cooper, III, for their individual and
collective contributions to our success.

Garrett S. Chan
Verl B. Lobb

Respectfully submitted,
Dan R. McDaniel, President
Donald B. McClary, Vice President

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON CHILD ID PROGRAM
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge F.& A.M. of California:
The Child ID Program was instituted in April 1998 by the Grand
Lodge of California and is financially supported by the California Masonic
Foundation. Four systems reside in Northern California and seven systems are
in Southern California. Since the program was started over 300,000 ID Sheets
have been provided to parents and guardians. The committee’s responsibility is
to schedule and support local Lodges who wish to use Grand Lodge equipment
and participate at local county fairs, street fairs, festivals, malls and other events.
The committee also maintains the equipment. In addition, there is a
professional booth available for either a 10 x 10 or a 10 x 20 presentation at
fairs which run 10 days or more.
In addition to the Grand Lodge equipment approximately 40 Lodges
have also purchased complete systems for use in their areas.
This program has been extremely well received by the public and the
participating Lodges. Members of the fraternity are very enthusiastic about the
program because it provides them with the opportunity to become known in
their communities and they receive positive feedback from parents about the
good work they are doing for their communities.
Respectfully submitted,
V. Allen Winter, Chairman
Mark E. Pressey, Vice Chairman

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON FINANCE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Finance Committee reports that it has reviewed and inspected all
of the Grand Masters Decisions and Recommendations, the carryover
Resolutions and the proposed legislation for the current year.
Several items, including Grand Masters Recommendations number 2, 3 and 4 as
well as carryover Resolution 02-01 and 02-07 will be reported upon at the
appropriate time. All other Decisions, Recommendations and all of the 2003
Resolutions will have no financial impact upon Grand Lodge. Therefore in the
interest of time, our committee will not report on the proposed legislation other
then those items listed above.
I would like to take a few minutes and give you a recap of our Grand
Lodge finances since I first became a part of this committee in 1995. The
budget approved by you for the 1995/96 Grand Lodge fiscal year projected total
expenses of $1,686,792. Per Capita assessment was $15.49 and was based on a
membership of 113,000 members. The Grand Lodge budget you will be asked
to approve tomorrow projects total expenditures of $2,029,222, an increase of
$342,430 over an eight year period. This computes to an increase of 20.3% for
the eight years or 2.5% per year. The per capita assessment upon which this

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

311

next years budget was based was $22.16, based on a membership of 76,000.
This is a reduction of 37,000 members or 33% over eight years. Almost entirely
because of this 33% loss of membership, your per capita assessment has gone
from $15.49 to $22.16, an increase of $6.67 or 43% over eight years.
During this last year, your Finance Committee projected membership
and per capita assessments into the year 2020. Our projected membership in the
year 2020 is 41,000 and a per capita assessment for that year of $56.51.
My brethren the future is not bright, but at the same time it is not
desperate. The future depends upon each of you. You are the leaders of our
great fraternity. I don’t particularly like raising per capita assessment each year,
but it is fiscally necessary. My personal opinion is we sell our fraternity much
too cheap. Our dues should be 3 or 4 times what they are today. I don’t
particularly like the idea of the one day class, but we need to do something to
reverse our membership trend and I am willing to try almost anything.
The future of our fraternity is in your hands. You are the leaders the
rest of us rely upon. Don’t let us down.
Respectfully submitted
Donald W. Finton, Chairman
George A. Anderson
D.E. Griffiths
Gary C. Young

Miguel A. Cheng
V. Allen Winter, Jr.
H. Donald Young

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON INVESTMENT
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
The Investment Committee is charged with the prudent handling of
Grand Lodge funds, which consist primarily of the General and Endowment
funds for the Masonic Homes of California, but also include the investment
portfolios of the California Masonic Foundation, California Masonic Memorial
Temple, Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc. and the Grand Lodge itself. We
take this charge very seriously. In its simplest terms, we manage the funds with
an eye both for growth and preservation of capital. We strive to maintain a
diversified portfolio that is designed to generate the necessary funds for the
continuing operation of the Masonic functions as well as allow for growth for
future needs.
The equity markets showed some signs of life over the past year,
although high volatility once again proved that the stock market is no place for
the faint of heart. The negative 17% return in the 3rd quarter of 2002 proved to
be the worst period since the bear market officially began in the spring of 2000.

312

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Markets rebounded strongly in the 4th quarter of 2002, retreated again in the
spring of this year, but bounced back significantly in the 2nd quarter of 2003,
producing the best returns since the end of 1999. Compounding the returns over
the past four quarters ending June 30, 2003, stocks were up a very modest 0.8%.
There remains a considerable amount of debate in the investment
industry as to whether we are seeing a sustainable turnaround in the economy
and a reversal of the bear market. Some pundits are convinced share prices
remain expensive based on historical earnings. Others are more optimistic,
citing an accommodating Fed, improving corporate profits and low inventory
levels. Although the markets may remain volatile as this tug-of-war plays out,
the Investment Committee will continue to oversee the management of the
portfolios under its charge in the same consistent and prudent manner that has
been utilized in the past.
The Investment Committee does not attempt to take advantages of
perceived short-term anomalies in the markets by chasing the most recent top
performing asset class. It could be argued that, given the negative returns and
high volatility of the equity markets over the past few years, one would be better
avoiding stocks altogether and investing only in bonds until the markets turn.
The problem with this strategy is that research shows that accurately predicting
when the markets will change direction is extremely difficult, if not impossible.
For example, had the Committee panicked last December (as many individual
and institutional investors did) and recommended to the Boards that they alter
the asset allocations by investing only in bonds, as of June 30th, the portfolio
would have foregone gains of just under 9%, or over $45 million. This figure
would have grown even larger since June 30th, as stocks have continued to
perform well and bonds have come under pressure due to rising long-term
interest rates.
The Investment Committee arrives at its recommended allocation to
stocks and bonds and other asset classes only after very careful due diligence
and consideration. The purpose of the funds, their spending requirements,
investment time horizon and ability to assume risk are all factored into the
recommendations. The possibility and duration of potential losses are carefully
weighed against the long-term potential for appreciation of assets after
withdrawals are taken into account. The Investment Committee recommends
asset allocations that will provide the highest probability that the funds will be
able to meet their respective objectives over the long-term.
In order to fulfill our responsibilities in accordance with the Grand
Lodge Investment Policy and Philosophy, the Investment Committee meets
quarterly with our Investment Consultant, Ennis/Knupp and our individual
portfolio managers. We review their respective performance compared to
various indices representing their objective. This process provides the
Investment Committee with the necessary data to adequately evaluate each

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

313

investment manager on a timely basis, and to observe whether they continue to
achieve their mandate.
As of June 30, 2003, the combined funds of Grand Lodge and the
related Masonic entities were up 0.1% for the twelve-month period. This
compares to 1.8% for our benchmark custom index. While consistent trailing
performance relative to the benchmark would be of concern to us, the
Investment Committee is not troubled over the results of the past year. This
relative underperformance was due primarily to the fact that many speculative,
unprofitable companies that were severely discounted over the past three years
rallied significantly in 2003.
Recent equity market returns were dominated by stocks of these types
of companies. For example, in the second quarter of 2003, large capitalization
companies with negative earnings outperformed companies with positive
earnings by over 20 percentage points (2,000 basis points). This phenomenon
was even more pronounced with small capitalization companies, where
unprofitable businesses returned 40% more than companies with positive
earnings.
By design, the investment mangers utilized by the Masonic Grand
Lodge and related entities focus on stocks of higher quality, profitable firms.
While this strategy was out of favor recently, longer-term results continue to be
well ahead of the benchmark. For the cumulative three-year period ending June
30th, the combined Grand Lodge and related entities funds were down 2.9%
versus -6.5% for the benchmark. Over the past five-year period, the funds were
up 3.0% versus the 0.4% return of the benchmark.
These favorable long-term results were achievable in part because we
held a relatively large portion of the portfolio in fixed income assets and we
over-weighted “value” and smaller company stocks whose performance has
been relatively positive of late. This strong relative performance can also be
attributed to the above-benchmark returns by each of our investment managers.
In fact, the returns of our funds have consistently surpassed the performance of
the benchmark since inception, while at the same time incurring an overall
lower level of risk. We believe the portfolio is well balanced going forward,
with an appropriate blend of large and small U.S. stocks (balanced between
growth and value), foreign stocks and bonds.
The market value of the Masonic Homes General and Endowment
portfolios on June 30, 2003 was $552,326,827 compared to $555,164,092 a year
earlier. This, combined with the other investment portfolios on which the
Investment Committee advises, resulted in combined assets totaling just under
$600,000,000. Please refer to the attachments that follow for a complete
performance summary from each manager for periods covering since inception
and by calendar quarters and years.

314

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

While the Committee has continued to recommend investing in
equities, it has also taken steps to reduce the overall volatility of returns. In the
fall of 2002, a search was conducted which led to the hiring of two alternative
investment managers whose products’ performance had low or negative
correlations to that of the Masonic Grand Lodge and related entities portfolios.
In the spring of this year, a small portion (about 5%) of the Masonic Homes
General and Endowment funds was allocated to these highly qualified firms that
met the Committee’s stringent requirements and passed our rigorous due
diligence process. The addition of these portfolios to the funds is expected to
reduce the overall portfolio risk somewhat. While it is too early to reach a
definite conclusion, initial performance has been encouraging. At some point,
these options may be recommended for other portfolios under our charge.
As reported last year, the Investment Committee facilitated the
Masonic Grand Lodge and related entities in being able to take advantage of
some economies of scale through the creation of a “master trust arrangement.”
This arrangement allowed the smaller accounts to ride the “coattails” of the
Homes’ institutional-sized portfolios. The conversion to the master trust had
just been completed at the time of the last Annual Communication. The master
trust has been a great benefit to the smaller funds’ portfolios by allowing them
access to institutional quality investment managers at significantly lower fees
than could otherwise be obtained. This has been accomplished without causing
disruptions to the Homes portfolios and has even reduced the investment
management expenses to some degree for the two Masonic Homes portfolios.
An additional enhancement has been made to the administrative
process. Investment related expenses are now being paid directly out of the
master trust and are allocated to each portfolio according to its respective share
of the total market value. Prior to this change, the Grand Lodge paid the
investment expenses and was later repaid by the appropriate entity. Since all
investment-related expenses are now allocated directly to the respective plan, it
provides more realistic investment results.
The Investment Committee continues to work closely with the various
Boards in developing sound policies regarding distributions from the
investments. Most portfolios now utilize a “distribution guideline” that calls for
the investment portfolio to payout 5% per year based on a five-year rolling
average market value. This strategy is in line with that of other, similar entities
and one that we believe is in the best interest of our constituents.
A number of personnel changes have taken place at the Grand Lodge
level which has been beneficial not only to Grand Lodge and the other Masonic
entities, but especially to the Investment Committee. As you may recall, the
Chief Financial Officer of Grand Lodge retired early last year. As a result, the
office of Grand Treasurer assumed the added responsibilities of Chief Financial
Officer and Mark Koenig was hired in November of last year to serve in the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

315

newly created position of Director of Finance, reporting to the Grand
Treasurer/Chief Financial Officer. Two additional positions were created in the
finance department to complete the restructuring. David Schwarzenberger was
hired early this year as the Manager of Investments and Reporting Analysis.
Mr. Schwarzenberger provides an added level of oversight to the managers,
custodians and investment consultant and to the day-to-day administration of the
investment portfolios. He has improved the reporting of investment results to
the Boards of Trustees and has also been instrumental in revising the Investment
Policy Statements of the Investment Committee and the Grand Lodge and
related entities. Stacy Robson was also hired this year as the Grand Lodge’s
Manager of Financial Reporting. Mr. Robson has been working closely with
Mark and other Grand Lodge staff to develop more meaningful and timely
financial statements. This team of financial professionals has greatly improved
and facilitated the accountability for and management of the investment
portfolios. We are very grateful to have this financial team assisting us in our
responsibilities to this our Grand Lodge and its entities.
The Committee continues to work closely with the Director of
Development, Doug Ismail, to ensure that donors to the various Grand Lodge
charities are provided the most competitive investment options. The Gift
Annuity program has been greatly enhanced, offering potential donors with
much higher income streams than were previously available. This improvement
should translate into a greater number of gifts. In fact, recent participation
levels in the program are quite encouraging.
The Committee has also been evaluating potential options for the
Lodge Investment Program. With the help of our Manager of Investment
Reporting and Analysis, a program is being developed that will offer Lodges
well-needed support with the management of their assets. Anticipating the
passage of the carry-over legislation relating to investments, we will be prepared
to roll out the new program shortly after this Annual Communication.
Investment Committee members will make themselves available to the Lodges
for assistance.
I am appreciative for the dedication of Most Worshipful Warren
Blomseth in his role as Grand Treasurer and the support he provides us.
And my most sincere appreciation is extended to the Grand Lodge
Investment Committeemen for the commitment and devotion they have for the
Masonic causes. They are always been forthright with their opinions and
expertise. I am convinced we have the finest group of investment professionals
fulfilling our very important mission.
In summary, while the financial markets continue to be volatile and
difficult, we are pleased with the results we continue to achieve. Rest assured
we remain ever diligent in our attempts to achieve adequate returns to support

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

316

the Homes and other Masonic charities while at the same time preserving our
asset base for future ages.
Respectfully submitted,
Russell E. Charvonia, Chairman

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MASONIC HISTORY

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.&A.M. of California:
Our 2001 report ended with the sentence “In the future we can foresee
a closer working relationship between our committee and the Institute for
Masonic Studies as well as with our Library and Museum.” This year our
prediction will come to fruition.
At the last Annual Communication our membership voted to
restructure the Committee system within Grand Lodge and merge the duties of
our Committee with those under the Institute for Masonic Studies. This
restructuring will better serve our brethren in the future.
During the publication of California Freemasonry 1850-2000 the last
fifty years we noticed that there was not a lot of archival material from which to
draw on for the “human interest” side of our history. During the previous fifty
years many of our Past Grand Masters had passed away and taken those
memories of what their time in office meant to them. Our living Past Grand
Masters were each given the opportunity to write or review the section of the
book pertaining to their year. This personal touch certainly added to the story of
our Grand Lodge by not relying solely upon the proceedings for each year.
Many of our members also provided photos for the book, which added to its
readability.
I would recommend to our successors that they contact each Past
Grand Master after his term of office and request that he write a recap of his
year with particular emphasis on those items not detailed in the proceedings. A
request should also be made for photographs of events (with the subject matter
and persons clearly identified). These items should be filed by year in the
museum archives for future authors of our Grand Lodge history.
On behalf of myself and all your committee members over the past
years I convey our thanks to the brethren for allowing us to serve you and
document our Grand Lodge History. Your committee published its first work in
1900 covering our first fifty years then a 100 year, 125 year and finally a 150th
year volume. These combined works were well received by the brethren and
offer a complete history of the life of Freemasonry here in our Golden State.
Respectfully submitted,
Brett A. Mac Donald, Chairman
Jorge Soto

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

319

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MEMBERSHIP DEVELOPMENT

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
I would first like to take this opportunity to publicly acknowledge and
thank the committeemen, the Grand Lodge Officers and staff who unselfishly
contributed their time and efforts to the creation of our New Membership
Development program.
The Committee Members: Wor. Gary L. Peterson, PM, Laguna
Saddleback Lodge No. 672; Wor. James E. Banta, PM, Fresno Lodge No. 247;
Wor. Roberto G. Perez, PM, Mariposa Lodge No. 24; Wor. Salvatore A.
Sciortino, PM, Wisdom Lodge No. 202; Wor. Felipe G. Leon, PM, San Jacinto
Lodge No. 338; and Wor. Edward Avertisian, PM, Burbank Lodge No. 406.
The Grand Lodge Officers and Staff Members: Right Worshipful Fred
Sorsabal, Junior Grand Warden; Wor. Allan L. Casalou, Assistant Grand
Secretary; and Bro. Angel Alvarez-Mapp, Acalanes Fellowship Lodge No. 480.
Thanks to the Grand Master, Most Worshipful M. William Holsinger,
for having faith and confidence in the selection of our team to develop this
program plan which is so dear to his heart.
At the beginning of his Masonic Year as Grand Master, Most
Worshipful Bill charged our committee with the task of developing a
comprehensive membership development program.
During our first meeting, we agreed that whatever program we could
develop would have to be supported by two external factors that the
membership would need to embrace – the need for leadership and a clear
understanding of our purpose as a fraternity.
We believe that strong leadership at the lodge level is the key for
Masonry to be a preeminent, viable and growing fraternity. Leaders innovate,
focus on people, inspire trust, and have their eyes on the future. These leaders
will assure that Freemasonry and Freemasons make a difference in the world
and continue to grow. Leaders, as we are all aware, manage the dream,
communicate the vision, recruit meticulously, reward, retrain and reorganize.
A membership plan must seek modern men who are determined and
confident in their sense of direction, unafraid to take risks, bold and courageous,
inspiring and uplifting. The fundamental beliefs of dignity of manhood,
strength of brotherhood, and the virtue of truth must be the basic principles
encompassed in a vision for our Fraternity. With such a vision, Freemasons can
view the future as worth achieving as each Mason encourages and promotes
Masonry.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

320

We believe that a man who becomes a Mason should expect to find in
his fraternity:
A.

A non-discriminatory Brotherhood of men who believe in a
Supreme Being

B.

An organization well led with the opportunity to learn and to
lead

C.

Outstanding fellowship, enjoyment, dignity, pride and
personal connections

D.

A role with his family and in his community

E.

Participation as a Brother with his friends, neighbors, sons,
supervisors and peers

Men with leadership abilities must be impressive enough to convince
potential candidates and their families that to be a Freemason is worth their time
and effort.
Friendships and self-improvement, including leadership
development and networking, must be among the benefits we offer any man
who asks for a petition.
Our committee also recognized that membership in Freemasonry is not
a problem solved by either a Grand Lodge or Lodge; it is an opportunity for the
individual Mason.
Grand Master Holsinger’s them for this year – “Freemasonry Seeks
Good Men and Makes Them Better – Tell a Friend!” led our thinking as we
began to devise a program to empower the individual Mason, as well as to
inspire him to share the legacy of membership in Freemasonry with others.
Through the centuries, membership in Masonry has been passed on
from father to son, from brother to brother, and from friend to friend.
Today, however, when you mention Freemasonry to most men between
the ages of 25 and 50, they say: “I think my grandfather was a Mason.” For
reasons which we may never be able to fully explain, Freemasonry may have
missed a good portion of a whole generation of men. In order to attract the
current generation, we must reach out to them, we must talk about Masonry with
them, so that once again, Masonry will be passed on from one generation to the
next.
Thus, the title of our new membership program, which will serve as
our core program, is titled “Pass It On.”
“Pass It On” is a tool to build the individual lodge members’ skills in
membership development. Based on recent research and the collective
experiences of many California Masons, the program is a step-by-step process
for attracting and retaining lodge members.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

321

There are two goals for the “Pass It On” program. The first is to assist
and encourage Masons to talk about Masonry with their family, friends and
colleagues and the second is to help each lodge attain the goal of five new
Master Masons per year, which is a number we have concluded is necessary if a
lodge is to remain viable.
The elements of the “Pass It On” program are contained within a kit.
The kit includes a comprehensive guide on how to use the program and several
new tools to be used for membership development and retention. The new tools
include an updated brochure, wallet brochure, a new packet to send to
applicants, and an additional packet to be sent to candidates who have been
elected to receive the degrees. Also included are candidate satisfaction surveys
and template letters to be sent to interested men and their wives. These surveys
and letters are provided on a CD, which is also included in the kit.
Here’s how to use the “Pass It On” program for the greatest benefit to
your respective lodges:
A.

Assign at least 3 members to a membership development
committee.

B.

All committee members should thoroughly review the “Pass It
On” guide and become familiar with the tools enclosed in the
program kit.

C.

Responsibility should then be assigned to each committee
member for various phases of the program.

D.

It will then be necessary to implement each phase of the
program as recommended.

My Brothers, I’m sure you will agree that membership development is
critical to the future of your lodge and the Masons of California.
When you receive the “Pass It On” kit for your lodge, you will notice
that many man hours were consumed in its development. Members of our
committee will be available at any time by way of telephone or e-mail to assist
in the implementation of the program if your lodge finds it necessary. We will
also contact your respective lodges on a periodic basis to evaluate your success
and use of the program.
Each lodge will receive one “Pass It On” Kit. A representative of the
lodge, be it the Master, Secretary, or a Senior or Junior Warden, may pick up
and sign for the “Pass It On” kit for your lodge this afternoon from noon until
5pm and from 8am until noon tomorrow. The kits will be distributed in the
outer lobby on the first floor.
The lodges not present will receive theirs in the mail.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

322

My brethren, on behalf of the Membership Development Committee
for the Grand Lodge of California, we thank you and the Most Worshipful M.
William Holsinger for giving us the opportunity to develop and present a
program that we feel will truly benefit our great fraternity for many years to
come.
Respectfully submitted,
Phillip L. McAllister, Sr., Chairman
Edward Avetisian

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE ON
OFFICERS MANAGEMENT WORKSHOPS
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
As has been the case in past years, there were six Officers Management
Workshops (OMWs) conducted around the state of California in 2003. This
report will describe those activities.
Contents of workshops:
Each workshop contains several elements. There is an opening
overview talk, in which the concepts of the Lodge Plan and Lodge Management
Team are presented. There is also a final wrap-up session that is designed to
create closure and a sense of enthusiasm for becoming a leader. These are
presented to all of the registrants at one time. However, the main teaching
activities of the OMWs are the smaller interactive breakout sessions that occur
between these two talks. Prior to, or at the time of (if the individual has not
preregistered), registration, the participants are randomly assigned to smaller
groups (designated by a color, usually red, blue, and gold) so that individual
lodge experiences can be represented in each group. Each group is assigned to a
room. There are six 40-minute interactive discussions on topics to be noted
below; each topic is presented separately to each of the color-coded groups.
Since there are usually 3 such groups (although there were only 2 in Eureka),
each presenter makes 3 presentations during the day. It is to be appreciated that
these small group presentations are conducted in as interactive a manner as
possible; most of the discussion comes from the audience.
There are 3 separate programs of breakout sessions, designated cohorts
A, B, and C. Since there are 3 OMWs in the North and 3 in the South, A, B, or
C is presented one time in each geographical region each year. Since the North
and South has further been subdivided into smaller units (San Francisco bay
area, San Joaquin Valley/Sacramento area, and the area farther in the North, Los
Angeles area, San Diego area, and an area north of Los Angeles in the South),

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

323

the cohorts are also rotated in each of those areas. Thus, over a three-year
period, any Mason who attends all of the OMWs in his area would complete the
entire program.
The curricular contents of cohorts A, B, and C are as follows:
Cohort A:

History – Masonry before Grand Lodge California
Temple association and lodge relationship
New membership development
Community Awareness
Budget
Planning an event

Cohort B:

History – Grand Lodge of California
Conflict resolution
Candidate mentoring
Public schools
Financial reporting
Stated meeting

Cohort C:

History – Symbols of Masonry
Calendar
Motivating members
Public relations/communications
Investment guidelines
Fundraising

*Although neither one spoke, credit must also be given to John Adams in the
